\mathcal{T}_{p+1}(J)\leq O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\!\left(d^{-3(1-\delta)/2+\epsilon}\right)R+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\!\left(d^{-3(1-\delta)/2+\epsilon/2}\right)R.

Since
\delta\in(0,1)
, the previous display is bounded by

\mathcal{T}_{p+1}(J)\leq O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\!\left(d^{-1+\delta+\epsilon}\right)R.

The deterministic bias
\kappa_{d}\Gamma_{p+1}
is absorbed similarly. Indeed,
using
\|\Gamma_{p+1}\|_{{\rm{op}}}\leq\tr(\Gamma_{p+1})
, we get

\kappa_{d}\|(\Gamma_{p+1})_{J,J}\|_{{\rm{op}}}\leq C_{0}^{2}d^{2\delta-2}\tr(\Gamma_{p+1})\lesssim d^{2\delta-2}R\leq d^{-1+\delta}R.

Combining the previous bounds gives the diagonal-block estimate

\|E_{J,J}\|_{{\rm{op}}}=\|(\widehat{\Gamma}-\Gamma_{\leq p})_{J,J}\|_{{\rm{op}}}\leq\mathcal{R}(J).

It remains to control the off-diagonal block.
The standard PSD block inequality gives

\|\widehat{\Gamma}_{S,N}\|_{{\rm{op}}}\leq\sqrt{\|\widehat{\Gamma}_{S,S}\|_{{\rm{op}}}\|\widehat{\Gamma}_{N,N}\|_{{\rm{op}}}}.

Using the diagonal-block estimate with
J=S
and
J=N
, we obtain

\|\widehat{\Gamma}_{J,J}\|_{{\rm{op}}}\leq\|(\Gamma_{\leq p})_{J,J}\|_{{\rm{op}}}+\mathcal{R}(J),\qquad J\in\{S,N\}.

Consequently, the off-diagonal block of
E
satisfies

\displaystyle\|E_{S,N}\|_{{\rm{op}}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\widehat{\Gamma}_{S,N}\|_{{\rm{op}}}+\|(\Gamma_{\leq p})_{S,N}\|_{{\rm{op}}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\Big(\|(\Gamma_{\leq p})_{S,S}\|_{{\rm{op}}}+\mathcal{R}(S)\Big)\Big(\|(\Gamma_{\leq p})_{N,N}\|_{{\rm{op}}}+\mathcal{R}(N)\Big)}+\|(\Gamma_{\leq p})_{S,N}\|_{{\rm{op}}}.

Finally, for any symmetric block matrix one has

\left\|\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
B^{\top}&D\end{pmatrix}\right\|_{{\rm{op}}}\leq\max\{\|A\|_{{\rm{op}}},\|D\|_{{\rm{op}}}\}+\|B\|_{{\rm{op}}}.

Applying this inequality to
E=\widehat{\Gamma}-\Gamma_{\leq p}
, with the
block decomposition
S\cup N=[d]
, gives

\displaystyle\|\widehat{M}-M_{\leq p}\|_{{\rm{op}}}

\displaystyle=\|E\|_{{\rm{op}}}

\displaystyle\leq\max\{\mathcal{R}(S),\mathcal{R}(N)\}+\|(\Gamma_{\leq p})_{S,N}\|_{{\rm{op}}}

\displaystyle+\sqrt{\Big(\|(\Gamma_{\leq p})_{S,S}\|_{{\rm{op}}}+\mathcal{R}(S)\Big)\Big(\|(\Gamma_{\leq p})_{N,N}\|_{{\rm{op}}}+\mathcal{R}(N)\Big)}.

This proves the theorem.
∎

D.1
Proof of Theorem
2

We work in an orthonormal coordinate system aligned with the relevant subspace.
In this basis,

S=[r],\qquad N=[d]\setminus[r].

Throughout the proof,
r
and
\ell
are fixed. We write

e_{f}:=\|f^{*}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2},\qquad\mu:=\mu(U).

Define the coherence scale

\Theta_{U}:=N_{r,\ell}^{2}\frac{r^{2}\mu}{d},\qquad N_{r,\ell}:=\binom{r+\ell}{\ell}.

Since
r
and
\ell
are fixed, this scale satisfies

\Theta_{U}\lesssim\frac{\mu}{d}.

Thus, in the regime where
\Theta_{U}
is sufficiently small,
Theorem
23
gives the block estimates

\displaystyle\|(\Gamma_{\leq p})_{S,S}\|_{{\rm{op}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim e_{f},

\displaystyle\|(\Gamma_{\leq p})_{S,N}\|_{{\rm{op}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim e_{f}\frac{\mu}{d},

\displaystyle\|(\Gamma_{\leq p})_{N,N}\|_{{\rm{op}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim e_{f}\frac{\mu^{2}}{d^{2}}.

The same theorem also gives the corresponding diagonal estimates:

(\Gamma_{\leq p})_{s,s}\lesssim\begin{cases}e_{f},&s\in S,\\[5.69054pt]
e_{f}\mu^{2}/d^{2},&s\in N.\end{cases}

We next estimate the diagonal profiles that enter

\mathcal{R}(S)
and
\mathcal{R}(N)
. Since
A_{s}
takes the form

A_{s}=\sum_{q=0}^{p}(\Gamma_{q})_{s,s}=(\Gamma_{\leq p})_{s,s},

the preceding diagonal bounds imply

A_{s}\lesssim\begin{cases}e_{f},&s\in S,\\[5.69054pt]
e_{f}\mu^{2}/d^{2},&s\in N.\end{cases}

The estimate for
B_{s}
follows from Cauchy’s inequality. Indeed, because
each
\Gamma_{q}\succeq 0
,

B_{s}=\sum_{q=0}^{p}\sqrt{(\Gamma_{q})_{s,s}}\leq\sqrt{p+1}\left(\sum_{q=0}^{p}(\Gamma_{q})_{s,s}\right)^{1/2}.

Since
p
is fixed in the present regime, this gives

B_{s}\lesssim\begin{cases}e_{f}^{1/2},&s\in S,\\[5.69054pt]
e_{f}^{1/2}\mu/d,&s\in N.\end{cases}

We now sum these coordinatewise bounds. Since
|S|=r=O(1)
and

|N|\leq d
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|A_{S}\|_{1}+\sqrt{|S|}\|A_{S}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim e_{f},

\displaystyle\|B_{S}\|_{1}+\sqrt{|S|}\|B_{S}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim e_{f}^{1/2},

\displaystyle\|A_{N}\|_{1}+\sqrt{|N|}\|A_{N}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim e_{f}\frac{\mu^{2}}{d},

\displaystyle\|B_{N}\|_{1}+\sqrt{|N|}\|B_{N}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim e_{f}^{1/2}\mu.

Substituting these profile estimates into the definition of
\mathcal{R}(S)

gives the active-block bound

\mathcal{R}(S)\lesssim e_{f}\left(d^{-\delta/2+\epsilon}+d^{-1+\delta+\epsilon}\right).

Here the other active-block contributions are dominated by the displayed terms
because
\delta\in(0,1)
. Similarly, the inactive-block contribution satisfies

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(N)\lesssim e_{f}\big(

\displaystyle d^{-\delta+\epsilon}+d^{-1+\delta+\epsilon}

\displaystyle+\mu^{2}d^{-1-\delta/2+\epsilon}+\mu d^{-(1+\delta)/2+\epsilon/2}\big).

We now apply Theorem
24
. With

\varepsilon_{\mathrm{agop}}:=\|\widehat{M}-M_{\leq p}\|_{{\rm{op}}},

the theorem yields

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathrm{agop}}

\displaystyle\leq\max\{\mathcal{R}(S),\mathcal{R}(N)\}+Ce_{f}\frac{\mu}{d}

\displaystyle+\Bigl[\bigl(Ce_{f}+\mathcal{R}(S)\bigr)\bigl(Ce_{f}\mu^{2}/d^{2}+\mathcal{R}(N)\bigr)\Bigr]^{1/2},

where
C=C_{r,\ell}
is a constant depending only on
r
and
\ell
.

Since
\epsilon>0
is chosen sufficiently small, we have

\mathcal{R}(S)=o_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(e_{f}).

Consequently, the active factor inside the square root is
O_{d,{\mathbb{P}},r,\ell}(e_{f})
.
For the inactive factor, the preceding bound on
\mathcal{R}(N)
gives

\displaystyle Ce_{f}\frac{\mu^{2}}{d^{2}}+\mathcal{R}(N)\lesssim e_{f}\big(

\displaystyle\mu^{2}d^{-2}+d^{-\delta+\epsilon}+d^{-1+\delta+\epsilon}

\displaystyle+\mu^{2}d^{-1-\delta/2+\epsilon}+\mu d^{-(1+\delta)/2+\epsilon/2}\big).

Taking square roots, and renaming the arbitrarily small polynomial slack

\epsilon
, the square-root term is therefore bounded by

\displaystyle e_{f}\big(

\displaystyle d^{-\delta/2+\epsilon}+d^{-(1-\delta)/2+\epsilon}+\mu d^{-1}

\displaystyle+\mu d^{-1/2-\delta/4+\epsilon}+\mu^{1/2}d^{-(1+\delta)/4+\epsilon}\big).

Combining the preceding estimates gives

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathrm{agop}}\lesssim e_{f}\big(

\displaystyle d^{-\delta/2+\epsilon}+d^{-(1-\delta)/2+\epsilon}+\mu d^{-1}

\displaystyle+\mu^{1/2}d^{-(1+\delta)/4+\epsilon}+\mu d^{-1/2-\delta/4+\epsilon}

\displaystyle+\mu^{2}d^{-1-\delta/2+\epsilon}+\mu d^{-(1+\delta)/2+\epsilon/2}\big).

Since
\mu\geq 1
and
\delta\in(0,1)
, the terms

\mu d^{-1}

and

\mu d^{-(1+\delta)/2+\epsilon/2}

are both absorbed by

\mu d^{-1/2-\delta/4+\epsilon}.

Hence we conclude

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathrm{agop}}\lesssim e_{f}\big(

\displaystyle d^{-\delta/2+\epsilon}+d^{-(1-\delta)/2+\epsilon}

\displaystyle+\mu^{1/2}d^{-(1+\delta)/4+\epsilon}+\mu d^{-1/2-\delta/4+\epsilon}

\displaystyle+\mu^{2}d^{-1-\delta/2+\epsilon}\big).

which proves (
12
).

Finally, suppose that for some constant
\gamma>0
, the following holds

\mu(U)\leq d^{1/2+\delta/4-\gamma}.

Then we reach

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathrm{agop}}\lesssim{}

\displaystyle d^{-\delta/2+\epsilon}+d^{-(1-\delta)/2+\epsilon}

\displaystyle+d^{-\delta/8-\gamma/2+\epsilon}+d^{-\gamma+\epsilon}+d^{-2\gamma+\epsilon}.

Choosing
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small, the right-hand side tends to zero, which completes the proof.

Appendix E
Proof of Lemma
3

Let
\Pi_{U}:=U^{\top}U
and
A:=\Pi_{U}M_{\leq p}\Pi_{U}
. We first identify
the relevant eigenspace of
A
. Since
UU^{\top}=I_{r}
, the matrix
A
has
the representation

A=U^{\top}(UM_{\leq p}U^{\top})U.

Hence the nonzero eigenvalues of
A
are exactly the eigenvalues of

UM_{\leq p}U^{\top}
. In particular, the eigenvalue identities

\lambda_{r}(A)=s_{p},\qquad\lambda_{r+1}(A)=0

hold. Moreover, the range of
A
is contained in
\mathrm{row}(U)
, so the
top-
r
eigenspace of
A
is precisely
\mathrm{row}(U)
.

Define the perturbation
E:=\widehat{M}-A
. The triangle inequality, together
with the definitions of
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{agop}}
and
\rho_{p}
, gives
the perturbation bound

\left\|E\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\left\|\widehat{M}-M_{\leq p}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}+\left\|M_{\leq p}-\Pi_{U}M_{\leq p}\Pi_{U}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\varepsilon_{\mathrm{agop}}+\rho_{p}.

(56)

For brevity, set
\Delta:=\varepsilon_{\mathrm{agop}}+\rho_{p}
.

We consider two cases. If
\Delta\geq s_{p}/4
, then

4\Delta/s_{p}\geq 1
. Since
\left\|\sin_{\Theta}(\widehat{U},U)\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1

always, the desired estimate is immediate in this case.

It remains to consider the case
\Delta<s_{p}/4
. Weyl’s inequality gives the
lower bound

\lambda_{r}(\widehat{M})\geq\lambda_{r}(A)-\left\|E\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\geq s_{p}-\Delta\geq\frac{3}{4}s_{p}.

The same inequality gives the upper bound

\lambda_{r+1}(\widehat{M})\leq\lambda_{r+1}(A)+\left\|E\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\Delta\leq\frac{1}{4}s_{p}.

These two bounds show that the top-
r
eigenvalues of
\widehat{M}
are
separated from the remaining spectrum by a gap of at least
s_{p}/2
.

We may now apply the Davis–Kahan
\sin\Theta
theorem to the pair

A
and
\widehat{M}=A+E
. The
r
-dimensional invariant subspace of

A
corresponding to its nonzero eigenvalues is
\mathrm{row}(U)
, while
the top-
r
eigenspace of
\widehat{M}
is
\mathrm{row}(\widehat{U})
by
assumption. Davis–Kahan gives the estimate

\left\|\sin_{\Theta}(\widehat{U},U)\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\frac{\left\|E\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}}{s_{p}/2}\leq 2\,\frac{\Delta}{s_{p}}\leq 4\,\frac{\Delta}{s_{p}}.

Combining the two cases yields

\left\|\sin_{\Theta}(\widehat{U},U)\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\min\left\{1,\,4\,\frac{\rho_{p}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{agop}}}{s_{p}}\right\}.

Finally, under the condition

(\rho_{p}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{agop}})/s_{p}=o_{d}(1)
, the right-hand side of (
15
) tends
to zero. Therefore the subspace error satisfies

\left\|\sin_{\Theta}(\widehat{U},U)\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=o_{d}(1),

which proves that
\widehat{U}
consistently recovers
\mathrm{row}(U)
.

Appendix F
Proof of Corollary
4

Set

e_{M}:=\left\|M_{\leq p}-U^{\top}\Sigma_{p}U\right\|_{\mathrm{op}},\qquad\varepsilon_{\mathrm{agop}}:=\left\|\widehat{M}-M_{\leq p}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}.

Lemma
1
gives the deterministic comparison bound

e_{M}=O_{d}\!\left(\frac{\mu(U)}{d}\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}\right).

The gap-stability assumption therefore implies
e_{M}=o_{d}(\kappa)
. Moreover,
the weak coherence condition gives
\mu(U)/d=o_{d}(1)
, so the same bound also
implies
e_{M}=o_{d}(e_{f})
.

Let
P_{U}:=P_{\mathrm{row}(U)}=U^{\top}U
. Since
U^{\top}\Sigma_{p}U
is
supported on
\mathrm{row}(U)
, the identity

P_{U}(U^{\top}\Sigma_{p}U)P_{U}=U^{\top}\Sigma_{p}U

holds. Hence the definition of
\rho_{p}
gives the inequality

\rho_{p}=\left\|M_{\leq p}-P_{U}M_{\leq p}P_{U}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\left\|M_{\leq p}-U^{\top}\Sigma_{p}U\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}+\left\|P_{U}(M_{\leq p}-U^{\top}\Sigma_{p}U)P_{U}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 2e_{M}.

Thus
\rho_{p}=o_{d}(e_{f})
. Similarly, the compression error satisfies

\left\|UM_{\leq p}U^{\top}-\Sigma_{p}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\left\|U(M_{\leq p}-U^{\top}\Sigma_{p}U)U^{\top}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq e_{M},

where the equality uses
UU^{\top}=I_{r}
. Weyl’s inequality then yields

s_{p}=\lambda_{\min}(UM_{\leq p}U^{\top})\geq\lambda_{\min}(\Sigma_{p})-e_{M}\geq\kappa-e_{M}.

Since
e_{M}=o_{d}(\kappa)
, this lower bound implies
s_{p}\geq\kappa/2
for all
sufficiently large
d
.

It remains to control the empirical AGOP error. Under

\mu(U)=O_{d}(d^{1/2+\delta/4-\gamma})
, choose
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small
relative to
\delta
and
\gamma
. Then the rate
R_{d}(U)
in
Theorem
2
satisfies
R_{d}(U)=o_{d}(1)
, and therefore
Theorem
2
gives the stochastic bound

\varepsilon_{\mathrm{agop}}=o_{d,\mathbb{P}}(e_{f}).

Combining this bound with
\rho_{p}=o_{d}(e_{f})
gives

\rho_{p}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{agop}}=o_{d,\mathbb{P}}(e_{f}).

Finally, Lemma
3
and the lower bound

s_{p}\geq\kappa/2
imply

\left\|\sin_{\Theta}(\widehat{U},U)\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 4\,\frac{\rho_{p}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{agop}}}{s_{p}}\leq 8\kappa^{-1}(\rho_{p}+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{agop}})=o_{d,\mathbb{P}}\!\left(\kappa^{-1}e_{f}\right).

This proves the claim.

Appendix G
Proof of Theorem
5

In this section, we follow the notation of
[
37
]
. In
particular, the space denoted by
L_{p}
elsewhere in the paper is written as

L^{p}
in this proof.

Set
g_{d}:=\mathcal{H}(f_{U}^{*})\in L^{2}(\tau_{d})
. For each

k\in\{0,\ldots,d\}
, let

V_{d,k}:=\mathrm{span}\{\chi_{S}:\ |S|=k\},\qquad\chi_{S}(x):=\prod_{i\in S}x_{i}.

The Walsh characters form an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\tau_{d})
, giving the
orthogonal decomposition

L^{2}(\tau_{d})=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{d}V_{d,k},\qquad\dim(V_{d,k})=\binom{d}{k}.

Because the first-step kernel is an inner-product kernel on the hypercube, each
space
V_{d,k}
is an eigenspace of the associated kernel operator. Let

\xi_{d,k}^{2}
denote the corresponding eigenvalue, and let
P_{k}
be the
orthogonal projector onto
V_{d,k}
. With this notation,

g_{d}=\sum_{k=0}^{d}P_{k}g_{d},\qquad g_{d,\leq p}:=\sum_{k=0}^{p}P_{k}g_{d},\qquad g_{d,>p}:=\sum_{k\geq p+1}P_{k}g_{d}.

By definition of the low-degree truncation of the multilinearized target,

g_{d,\leq p}=\mathcal{H}(f_{U}^{*})_{\leq p}
.

We next identify the leading eigenspace appearing in Theorem 4 of

[
37
]
. Let

m:=\sum_{k=0}^{p}\binom{d}{k}.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
2
, the hypercube
verification in Appendix D.2 of
[
37
]
applies. In
particular, the degree ordering of the kernel eigenspaces is compatible with
the Walsh decomposition above: the leading
m
eigenspaces are exactly

\bigoplus_{k=0}^{p}V_{d,k}.

Equivalently, the projector
P_{\leq m}
in the notation of

[
37
]
coincides with the Walsh projector

P_{\leq p}:=\sum_{k=0}^{p}P_{k}
. Hence
P_{>m}g_{d}=g_{d,>p}
.

The same hypercube spectral estimates give the fixed-degree scaling

\xi_{d,k}^{2}\asymp d^{-k}
. Since
p
and
\ell
are fixed, there exist
constants
c,C>0
, independent of
d
, such that

\min_{0\leq k\leq p}\xi_{d,k}^{2}\geq c\,d^{-p},\qquad\max_{p+1\leq k\leq\ell}\xi_{d,k}^{2}\leq C\,d^{-p-1},

(57)

where the second bound is void when
\ell\leq p
.

Let
\lambda_{1}
denote the regularization parameter used in the first KRR
step, so that
\hat{f}_{1}=\hat{f}_{\lambda_{1}}
. By the assumptions of
Theorem
2
, the sample size and regularization satisfy

n=d^{p+\delta},\qquad 0<\delta<1,\qquad\lambda_{1}\in[0,\lambda^{\star}],\qquad\lambda^{\star}:=\operatorname{Tr}(H_{d,>m}).

The hypercube kernel assumptions imply

\operatorname{Tr}(H_{d,>m})=\Theta(1)
. Therefore the effective
regularization parameter from Theorem 4 of
[
37
]
,

\gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}:=\lambda_{1}+\operatorname{Tr}(H_{d,>m}),

satisfies

\gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}=\Theta(1),\qquad\frac{\gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}}{n}=\Theta(d^{-p-\delta}).

(58)

Theorem 4 of
[
37
]
gives the effective population
estimator in diagonal form:

\hat{f}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}}=\sum_{k=0}^{d}s_{d,k}\,P_{k}g_{d},\qquad s_{d,k}:=\frac{\xi_{d,k}^{2}}{\xi_{d,k}^{2}+\gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}/n}.

For the low-degree modes
k\leq p
, the lower bound in
(
57
) and the scale estimate
(
58
) give the shrinkage bound

1-s_{d,k}=\frac{\gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}/n}{\xi_{d,k}^{2}+\gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}/n}\leq\frac{\gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}/n}{\xi_{d,k}^{2}}\lesssim d^{-\delta}.

For the high-degree modes that can appear in
g_{d}
, namely

p+1\leq k\leq\ell
, the upper bound in
(
57
) gives

s_{d,k}=\frac{\xi_{d,k}^{2}}{\xi_{d,k}^{2}+\gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}/n}\leq\frac{\xi_{d,k}^{2}}{\gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}/n}\lesssim d^{\delta-1}.

We now bound the deterministic bias of the effective estimator. Since
g_{d}

has degree at most
\ell
, the projection
P_{k}g_{d}
vanishes for every

k>\ell
. Orthogonality of the Walsh degree decomposition and the preceding
shrinkage bounds give

\displaystyle\big\|\hat{f}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}}-g_{d,\leq p}\big\|_{L^{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{p}(1-s_{d,k})^{2}\|P_{k}g_{d}\|_{L^{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}+\sum_{k=p+1}^{\ell}s_{d,k}^{2}\|P_{k}g_{d}\|_{L^{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim d^{-2\delta}\sum_{k=0}^{p}\|P_{k}g_{d}\|_{L^{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}+d^{2\delta-2}\sum_{k=p+1}^{\ell}\|P_{k}g_{d}\|_{L^{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\bigl(d^{-2\delta}+d^{2\delta-2}\bigr)\|g_{d}\|_{L^{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}.

(59)

Since
0<\delta<1
, both
d^{-2\delta}
and
d^{2\delta-2}
converge to
zero. Thus (
G
) implies

\big\|\hat{f}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}}-g_{d,\leq p}\big\|_{L^{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}=o_{d}(1)\,\|g_{d}\|_{L^{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}.

(60)

It remains to compare the empirical KRR estimator with the effective population
estimator. Theorem 4 of
[
37
]
, together with the
identification
P_{>m}g_{d}=g_{d,>p}
, gives the stochastic comparison

\big\|\hat{f}_{1}-\hat{f}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}}\big\|_{L^{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}=o_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)\left(\|g_{d}\|_{L^{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}+\|g_{d,>p}\|_{L^{2+\eta}(\tau_{d})}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right).

(61)

We control the
L^{2+\eta}
-term using the bounded degree of
g_{d}
. Since

\deg(g_{d})\leq\ell
, the function
g_{d,>p}
also has degree at most

\ell
. Set
q_{0}:=\lceil 2+\eta\rceil
. Monotonicity of
L^{q}
-norms on a
probability space and the Boolean hypercontractivity estimate from Lemma 18 of

[
37
]
give

\|g_{d,>p}\|_{L^{2+\eta}(\tau_{d})}^{2}\leq\|g_{d,>p}\|_{L^{q_{0}}(\tau_{d})}^{2}\leq(q_{0}-1)^{\ell}\|g_{d,>p}\|_{L^{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}.

Since
g_{d,>p}
is an orthogonal projection of
g_{d}
, the preceding
estimate implies

\|g_{d,>p}\|_{L^{2+\eta}(\tau_{d})}^{2}\lesssim\|g_{d}\|_{L^{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}.

Substituting this bound into
(
61
) yields

\big\|\hat{f}_{1}-\hat{f}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}}\big\|_{L^{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}=o_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)\left(\|g_{d}\|_{L^{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right).

(62)

Finally, we combine the empirical-to-effective error with the deterministic
effective bias. The elementary inequality

\|a+b\|_{2}^{2}\leq 2\|a\|_{2}^{2}+2\|b\|_{2}^{2}
, applied in
L^{2}(\tau_{d})
with

a=\hat{f}_{1}-\hat{f}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}},\qquad b=\hat{f}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}}-g_{d,\leq p},

gives

\displaystyle\|\hat{f}_{1}-g_{d,\leq p}\|_{L^{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\big\|\hat{f}_{1}-\hat{f}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}}\big\|_{L^{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}+2\big\|\hat{f}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}}-g_{d,\leq p}\big\|_{L^{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle=o_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)\left(\|g_{d}\|_{L^{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right)+o_{d}(1)\,\|g_{d}\|_{L^{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle=o_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)\left(\|g_{d}\|_{L^{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right).

(63)

Since
g_{d,\leq p}=\mathcal{H}(f_{U}^{*})_{\leq p}
, the estimate
(
G
) is the desired claim.

Appendix H
Proof of Proposition
6

Let

B:=U^{\top}\Sigma_{p}U,\qquad\Delta_{d}:=\left\|\widehat{M}_{1}-B\right\|_{\mathrm{op}},\qquad m_{d}:=\max\{d^{-\delta/2},\,d^{-1+\delta}\}.

By construction,

M_{2}=\frac{d}{c_{\eta}}\,(\widehat{M}_{1}+\eta I_{d}),\qquad c_{\eta}=\tr(\widehat M_1+\eta I_d).

We first identify the deterministic approximation term. Let

Q:=\begin{bmatrix}U\\
U_{\perp}\end{bmatrix}\in O(d).

Since
U_{\perp}
spans
\mathrm{row}(U_{\perp})
, we have

I_{d}=U^{\top}U+(U_{\perp})^{\top}U_{\perp}.

Using this decomposition, we may write

B+\eta I_{d}=U^{\top}(\Sigma_{p}+\eta I_{r})U+\eta(U_{\perp})^{\top}U_{\perp}=Q^{\top}\begin{bmatrix}\Sigma_{p}+\eta I_{r}&0\\
0&\eta I_{d-r}\end{bmatrix}Q.

Taking principal square roots yields

\sqrt{B+\eta I_{d}}=Q^{\top}\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{\Sigma_{p}+\eta I_{r}}&0\\
0&\sqrt{\eta}\,I_{d-r}\end{bmatrix}Q.

Therefore, by the definition of
\widehat{x}
, we have

\widehat{x}=\sqrt{B+\eta I_{d}}\,x.

This identity allows us to rewrite the approximation error as

\displaystyle\bigl\|M_{2}^{1/2}x-\sqrt{\tfrac{d}{c_{\eta}}}\,\widehat{x}\bigr\|_{L_{2}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{d}{c_{\eta}}}\,\bigl\|\bigl(\sqrt{\widehat{M}_{1}+\eta I_{d}}-\sqrt{B+\eta I_{d}}\bigr)x\bigr\|_{L_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{d}{c_{\eta}}}\,\left\|\sqrt{\widehat{M}_{1}+\eta I_{d}}-\sqrt{B+\eta I_{d}}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\|x\|_{L_{2}}.

We next control the square-root perturbation term. Since both

\widehat{M}_{1}+\eta I_{d}
and
B+\eta I_{d}
are bounded below by
\eta I_{d}
, the square-root map is operator-Lipschitz on
[\eta,\infty)
, which gives

\left\|\sqrt{\widehat{M}_{1}+\eta I_{d}}-\sqrt{B+\eta I_{d}}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\eta}}\,\left\|\widehat{M}_{1}-B\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\frac{\Delta_{d}}{2\sqrt{\eta}}.

Substituting this bound into the previous display, we obtain

\displaystyle\bigl\|M_{2}^{1/2}x-\sqrt{\tfrac{d}{c_{\eta}}}\,\widehat{x}\bigr\|_{L_{2}}\leq\frac{\sqrt{d}}{2\sqrt{c_{\eta}\eta}}\,\Delta_{d}\,\|x\|_{L_{2}}.

(64)

It remains to identify the scale of
c_{\eta}
. Expanding the trace, we have

c_{\eta}=\eta d+\tr(B)+\tr(\widehat M_1-B).

Since
UU^{\top}=I_{r}
, this gives

\tr(B)=\tr(U^\top\Sigma_p U)=\tr(\Sigma_p),

and therefore we have

|c_{\eta}-\eta d|\leq\tr(\Sigma_p)+d\,\Delta_{d}.

Now Corollary
4
, applied with
\epsilon=\zeta/2
, yields

\Delta_{d}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\!\bigl(d^{\zeta/2}m_{d}\bigr).

Because
\eta=d^{\zeta}m_{d}
, we have

d\,\Delta_{d}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\!\bigl(d^{-\zeta/2}\eta d\bigr)=o_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(\eta d).

Moreover, the following holds

\eta d\geq d^{\zeta}\cdot d^{-1+\delta}\cdot d=d^{\zeta+\delta}\to\infty,

while
\tr(\Sigma_p)=O_{d}(1)
. Hence
\tr(\Sigma_p)=o(\eta d)
, and we conclude that

c_{\eta}=(1+o_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(1))\eta d.

Finally, inserting this asymptotic into (
64
) yields

\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\sqrt{c_{\eta}\eta}}=(1+o_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(1))\frac{1}{\eta},

and therefore we obtain

\bigl\|M_{2}^{1/2}x-\sqrt{\tfrac{d}{c_{\eta}}}\,\widehat{x}\bigr\|_{L_{2}}\leq(1+o_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(1))\frac{\Delta_{d}}{2\eta}\,\|x\|_{L_{2}}.

Using again
\Delta_{d}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(d^{\zeta/2}m_{d})
and
\eta=d^{\zeta}m_{d}
, we obtain

\frac{\Delta_{d}}{\eta}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(d^{-\zeta/2}),

which gives

\bigl\|M_{2}^{1/2}x-\sqrt{\tfrac{d}{c_{\eta}}}\,\widehat{x}\bigr\|_{L_{2}}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(d^{-\zeta/2})\,\|x\|_{L_{2}}.

This is exactly (
20
).

Appendix I
Proof for preliminaries

I.1
Proof of Lemma
13

We begin with the polynomial truncation. Applying Theorem
10
with truncation order
2m+1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|K-K_{2m+1}-\left(g(1)-g_{2m+1}(1)\right)I_{n}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(\frac{\log^{m+1}(n)}{d^{m+1-p-\delta}}\right).

(65)

We next expand
K_{2m+1}
in the Fourier basis. By definition of the truncated kernel, we have

K_{2m+1}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{2m+1}g^{(\ell)}(0)\cdot\frac{1}{\ell!}\left(\frac{XX^{\top}}{d}\right)^{\odot\ell}.

Applying the refined identity (
30
) term by term, this gives

\displaystyle K_{2m+1}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{2m+1}g^{(\ell)}(0)\left(d^{-\ell}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\ell}}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\ell}}^{\top}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j:\,0\leq j<\ell,\\
\ell-j~\mathrm{is~even}\end{subarray}}\widetilde{d}_{j}^{(\ell)}\,\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j}}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j}}^{\top}\right).

Collecting together the terms with the same Fourier degree, we may therefore write

\displaystyle K_{2m+1}=\Phi_{\leq p}D\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}+\sum_{k=p+1}^{2m+1}\theta_{k}\,\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{k}}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{k}}^{\top},

(66)

where, for each
j=0,\ldots,p
,

D_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j}}:=\left(\frac{g^{(j)}(0)}{d^{j}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell:\,j<\ell\leq 2m+1,\\
\ell-j~\mathrm{is~even}\end{subarray}}g^{(\ell)}(0)\,\widetilde{d}_{j}^{(\ell)}\right)\,I_{|{\mathcal{S}}_{j}|},

and, for each
k=p+1,\ldots,2m+1
,

\theta_{k}:=\frac{g^{(k)}(0)}{d^{k}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell:\,k<\ell\leq 2m+1,\\
\ell-k~\mathrm{is~even}\end{subarray}}g^{(\ell)}(0)\,\widetilde{d}_{k}^{(\ell)}.

These formulas immediately yield the required coefficient bounds. Indeed, since

\widetilde{d}_{j}^{(\ell)}=O_{d}(d^{-(j+\ell)/2})
and
\ell-j
is a positive even integer in the correction terms, we necessarily have
\ell\geq j+2
. It follows that

\widetilde{d}_{j}^{(\ell)}=O_{d}(d^{-j-1}).

Because the number of indices
\ell
is bounded in terms of
m
, the whole correction sum is still of order
O_{d}(d^{-j-1})
. Hence, for
j=0,\ldots,p
, we obtain

\|D_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j}}-g^{(j)}(0)d^{-j}I_{|{\mathcal{S}}_{j}|}\|_{\rm op}=O_{d}(d^{-j-1}),

while for
k=p+1,\ldots,2m+1
, we have

\theta_{k}=\frac{g^{(k)}(0)}{d^{k}}+O_{d}(d^{-k-1}).

This proves the coefficient estimates in the statement.

I.2
Proof of Lemma
14

Let
X\sim\tau_{d}
. For each
S\subseteq[d]
with
|S|=m
, set

a_{S}:=\mathbb{E}[z^{\alpha}(X)X^{S}]
. The degree-
m
Walsh component of

\mathcal{H}(z^{\alpha})
is

[\mathcal{H}(z^{\alpha})]_{\deg m}(x)=\sum_{|S|=m}a_{S}x^{S}.

For
\lambda\in\Lambda_{m}(\alpha)
, write

H_{\lambda}(x)=\sum_{|S|=m}h_{\lambda,S}x^{S},\qquad h_{\lambda,S}:=\mathbb{E}[z^{\lambda}(X)X^{S}].

Fix
S\subseteq[d]
with
|S|=m
. Expanding

z^{\alpha}=\prod_{j=1}^{r}z_{j}^{\alpha_{j}}
amounts to assigning each slot
corresponding to direction
j
to a coordinate
i\in[d]
. Such an
assignment contributes to
a_{S}
precisely when, for each coordinate
i
, the
total multiplicity assigned to
i
has parity
\mathbf{1}_{\{i\in S\}}
.

We first count the principal assignments. Fix

\lambda\in\mathcal{A}_{m}(\alpha)
, and put

\nu=\nu(\alpha,\lambda)=(\alpha-\lambda)/2
. A contributing assignment is
called principal of type
\lambda
if it has the following structure: each
coordinate
i\in S
is hit exactly once; among these singleton hits, exactly

\lambda_{j}
come from direction
j
; the remaining
2\nu_{j}
slots of
direction
j
are grouped into
\nu_{j}
same-direction pairs; and the
pair-support coordinates are distinct and lie in
S^{c}
.

Let
G_{\lambda,S}
denote the total contribution of the principal
assignments of type
\lambda
. A direct count gives the identity

G_{\lambda,S}=\mathsf{A}_{\alpha,\lambda}\,h_{\lambda,S}\,C_{\nu(\alpha,\lambda)}(S^{c}).

(67)

The factor
h_{\lambda,S}
accounts for the singleton placements on
S
,
while
C_{\nu(\alpha,\lambda)}(S^{c})
encodes the pair-support coordinates in

S^{c}
. For direction
j
, the slot combinatorics contribute the factor

\binom{\alpha_{j}}{\lambda_{j}}\frac{(2\nu_{j})!}{2^{\nu_{j}}\nu_{j}!}\nu_{j}!=\frac{\alpha_{j}!}{\lambda_{j}!\,2^{\nu_{j}}}.

This is the
j
-th factor in

\mathsf{A}_{\alpha,\lambda}=\prod_{j=1}^{r}\frac{\alpha_{j}!}{\lambda_{j}!\,2^{\nu_{j}}}.

Summing over all admissible
\lambda
, the coefficient
a_{S}
decomposes as

a_{S}=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{A}_{m}(\alpha)}G_{\lambda,S}+B_{S},

where
B_{S}
denotes the total contribution of the non-principal assignments.

We next replace
C_{\nu}(S^{c})
by
C_{\nu}
. Since all coefficients in the
generating function defining
C_{\nu}(T)
are nonnegative, the difference

C_{\nu}-C_{\nu}(S^{c})
is obtained by forcing at least one pair-support
coordinate to lie in
S
. Since
|\nu|\leq\ell
, this difference satisfies

|C_{\nu}-C_{\nu}(S^{c})|\leq C_{\ell}\sum_{i\in S}q_{i}.

Because
|S|=m\leq\ell
, the preceding bound is at most

C_{\ell}m\max_{i}q_{i}\leq C_{\ell}\Delta_{U}
. Using
(
67
), the finiteness of
\mathcal{A}_{m}(\alpha)
, and the
bound
\|H_{\lambda}\|_{L_{2}}\leq\|z^{\lambda}\|_{L_{2}}\leq C_{\ell}
, the omission
error satisfies

\sum_{|S|=m}\left|\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{A}_{m}(\alpha)}\mathsf{A}_{\alpha,\lambda}\bigl(C_{\nu(\alpha,\lambda)}(S^{c})-C_{\nu(\alpha,\lambda)}\bigr)h_{\lambda,S}\right|^{2}\leq C_{\ell}\Delta_{U}^{2}.

(68)

It remains to bound the non-principal assignments. We first record an
elementary coefficient estimate. For a fixed ordered label list

\sigma=(\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{m})\in[r]^{m}
, define

K_{\sigma,S}:=\sum_{\psi:\,[m]\to S\ \mathrm{bijection}}\prod_{a=1}^{m}\bigl|u_{\psi(a)}^{[\sigma_{a}]}\bigr|.

The following bound will be used repeatedly:

\sum_{|S|=m}K_{\sigma,S}^{2}\leq C_{\ell}.

(69)

Indeed,
K_{\sigma,S}
is the absolute-value analogue of a degree-
m

Walsh coefficient of a product of
m
linear forms. Since the degree-
m

projection is an
L_{2}(\tau_{d})
-contraction, Hölder’s inequality and the
Khintchine inequality give

\left(\sum_{|S|=m}K_{\sigma,S}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\leq\left\|\prod_{a=1}^{m}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}|u_{i}^{[\sigma_{a}]}|X_{i}\right)\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq\prod_{a=1}^{m}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{d}|u_{i}^{[\sigma_{a}]}|X_{i}\right\|_{L_{2m}}\leq C_{\ell}.

This proves (
69
).

We now decompose the non-principal assignments. We overcount them by making
two bounded-complexity choices. First, for each coordinate
i\in S
, choose
one distinguished hit among the odd number of hits at
i
. Second, choose
one distinguished witness of non-principality. Since
|\alpha|\leq\ell
, the
number of such choices is bounded by
C_{\ell}
.

The distinguished singleton hits on
S
have an ordered label list

\sigma=(\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{m})\in[r]^{m}
, and their total absolute
contribution is bounded by
K_{\sigma,S}
. After these distinguished
singleton hits are fixed, all remaining multiplicities are even. We impose the
following priority rule for the remaining defects.

1.

First, consider assignments for which at least one remaining block touches

S
. Choose such a block as the distinguished witness. Let

j_{1},\dots,j_{t}
, with
t\geq 2
, be its fixed labels. For a support
coordinate
i\in S
, this block contributes at most

\prod_{a=1}^{t}|u_{i}^{[j_{a}]}|\leq q_{i}^{t/2}\leq q_{i},

where the last inequality uses
q_{i}\leq 1
. Summing over the possible support
coordinate in
S
gives the bound

\sum_{i\in S}q_{i}\leq m\max_{i}q_{i}\leq C_{\ell}\Delta_{U}.

2.

Second, consider the remaining assignments for which no remaining block
touches
S
, but some coordinate in
S^{c}
carries remaining multiplicity at
least
4
. Choose such a block as the distinguished witness. If its fixed
labels are
j_{1},\dots,j_{t}
, with
t\geq 4
, then its total absolute
contribution is at most

\sum_{i=1}^{d}\prod_{a=1}^{t}|u_{i}^{[j_{a}]}|\leq\sum_{i=1}^{d}q_{i}^{t/2}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{d}q_{i}^{2}=\rho\leq\Delta_{U}.

3.

Third, consider the remaining assignments for which no remaining block touches

S
, and no coordinate in
S^{c}
carries remaining multiplicity at least

4
. At this stage, every remaining block is a
2
-block in
S^{c}
, and
the supports of these
2
-blocks are distinct. If the assignment is still
non-principal, then at least one such
2
-block is mixed-direction. Choose a
mixed block with labels
j\neq k
as the distinguished witness. After all
other remaining blocks have been fixed, the allowed support coordinates for
this mixed block are
i\notin T
, where
T
contains
S
and the supports
of the other remaining blocks. In particular,
|T|\leq C_{\ell}
. The
orthogonality of the rows of
U
gives the identity

\sum_{i\notin T}u_{i}^{[j]}u_{i}^{[k]}=-\sum_{i\in T}u_{i}^{[j]}u_{i}^{[k]}.

This identity bounds the absolute contribution of the mixed block by

\left|\sum_{i\notin T}u_{i}^{[j]}u_{i}^{[k]}\right|\leq\sum_{i\in T}q_{i}\leq C_{\ell}\max_{i}q_{i}\leq C_{\ell}\Delta_{U}.

It remains to control the non-distinguished even blocks. For a fixed block
with labels
j_{1},\dots,j_{t}
, where
t\geq 2
, Hölder’s inequality gives

\sum_{i=1}^{d}\prod_{a=1}^{t}|u_{i}^{[j_{a}]}|\leq\prod_{a=1}^{t}\|u^{[j_{a}]}\|_{t}\leq\prod_{a=1}^{t}\|u^{[j_{a}]}\|_{2}=1.

Restrictions on the allowed support coordinates can only decrease this
absolute sum. Since the number of remaining blocks is bounded by
\ell
, all
non-distinguished even blocks together contribute at most
C_{\ell}
.

The three prioritized defect estimates give the pointwise bound

|B_{S}|\leq C_{\ell}\Delta_{U}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{\alpha,m}}K_{\sigma,S},

where
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha,m}
is a finite set of ordered label lists with
cardinality bounded by
C_{\ell}
. Squaring, summing over
S
, and using
(
69
) gives

\sum_{|S|=m}|B_{S}|^{2}\leq C_{\ell}\Delta_{U}^{2}.

(70)

Finally, define the coefficient error by

E_{S}:=a_{S}-\sum_{\lambda\in\mathcal{A}_{m}(\alpha)}\mathsf{A}_{\alpha,\lambda}C_{\nu(\alpha,\lambda)}h_{\lambda,S}.

The estimates (
68
) and
(
70
) imply

\sum_{|S|=m}|E_{S}|^{2}\leq C_{\ell}\Delta_{U}^{2}.

By Parseval’s identity, the left-hand side is exactly the squared

L_{2}
-error of the degree-
m
approximation. Taking square roots proves the
lemma.

I.3
Proof of Lemma
16

Define
L_{i}(w):=\sum_{j=1}^{r}(u_{i}^{[j]})^{2}w_{j}
. The generating function
factorizes as

\prod_{i=1}^{d}(1+L_{i}(w))=\exp\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}L_{i}(w)\right)\exp(R(w)),\qquad R(w):=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\bigl(\log(1+L_i(w))-L_{i}(w)\bigr).

Since
\sum_{i}(u_{i}^{[j]})^{2}=1
for every
j
, the linear term satisfies

\sum_{i=1}^{d}L_{i}(w)=\sum_{j=1}^{r}w_{j}
. Thus the coefficient of
w^{\nu}
in
the first exponential factor is

[w^{\nu}]\exp\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}L_{i}(w)\right)=[w^{\nu}]\exp\left(\sum_{j=1}^{r}w_{j}\right)=\frac{1}{\nu!}.

We first consider the case
\Delta_{U}\geq 1
. Since

1+L_{i}(w)\leq\exp(L_i(w))
coefficientwise, the coefficient
C_{\nu}

satisfies

0\leq C_{\nu}\leq[w^{\nu}]\exp\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}L_{i}(w)\right)=\frac{1}{\nu!}.

In this case, the desired error bound follows from

\left|C_{\nu}-\frac{1}{\nu!}\right|\leq\frac{1}{\nu!}\leq C_{\ell}\Delta_{U}.

It remains to consider the case
\Delta_{U}<1
. In this case,

\rho\leq\Delta_{U}<1
. The series
R(w)
has no constant or linear terms.
Therefore, every coefficient of
R(w)
up to total degree
|\nu|\leq\ell

is a finite linear combination, with coefficients depending only on
\ell
,
of coefficients of
L_{i}(w)^{m}
, where
2\leq m\leq|\nu|
. For such
m
, the
coefficient bound

|[w^{\omega}]L_{i}(w)^{m}|\leq C_{\ell}q_{i}^{m}\leq C_{\ell}q_{i}^{2}

holds because
0\leq q_{i}\leq 1
. After summing over
i
, every relevant
coefficient of
R(w)
is bounded by

C_{\ell}\sum_{i=1}^{d}q_{i}^{2}=C_{\ell}\rho.

Since
\rho<1
and
|\nu|\leq\ell
, every coefficient of

\exp(R(w))-1
up to total degree
|\nu|
is also
O_{\ell}(\rho)
.
Multiplication by
\exp(\sum_j w_j)
, whose coefficients up to degree

\ell
are bounded by
C_{\ell}
, gives

\left|C_{\nu}-\frac{1}{\nu!}\right|\leq C_{\ell}\rho\leq C_{\ell}\Delta_{U}.

This proves the lemma.

I.4
Proof of Theorem
17

Write the monomial expansion of
h
as

h(z)=\sum_{\alpha\in\Lambda_{b}}b_{\alpha}z^{\alpha}.

We first record the coefficient comparison

\sum_{|\alpha|\leq\ell}|b_{\alpha}|+\sum_{|\lambda|\leq\ell}|a_{\lambda}|\leq C_{\ell}N_{r,\ell}^{1/2}\|h\|_{L_{2}(\gamma_{r})}.

(71)

Indeed, Hermite orthogonality gives

\sum_{|\lambda|\leq\ell}|a_{\lambda}|\leq N_{r,\ell}^{1/2}\left(\sum_{|\lambda|\leq\ell}a_{\lambda}^{2}\lambda!\right)^{1/2}.

Using (
43
), the preceding display becomes

\sum_{|\lambda|\leq\ell}|a_{\lambda}|\leq N_{r,\ell}^{1/2}\|h\|_{L_{2}(\gamma_{r})}.

The same bound for the monomial coefficients follows by expanding each

{\rm{He}}_{\lambda}
into monomials. Since
|\lambda|\leq\ell
, the

\ell_{1}
-norm of the monomial coefficient vector of
{\rm{He}}_{\lambda}
is
bounded by
C_{\ell}
. This proves (
71
).

Applying Corollary
15
to each monomial in
h
, and then
using (
71
), gives

\mathcal{H}(f^{*})=\sum_{|\lambda|\leq\ell}c_{\lambda}H_{\lambda}+E,

where

\|E\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}\leq C_{\ell}N_{r,\ell}^{1/2}\|h\|_{L_{2}(\gamma_{r})}\Delta_{U}.

Here the coefficients
c_{\lambda}
are given by

c_{\lambda}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\Lambda_{b}\\
\lambda\in\mathcal{A}(\alpha)\end{subarray}}b_{\alpha}\mathsf{A}_{\alpha,\lambda}C_{\nu(\alpha,\lambda)}.

On the other hand, the Hermite coefficient formula
(
41
) gives

a_{\lambda}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\Lambda_{b}\\
\lambda\in\mathcal{A}(\alpha)\end{subarray}}b_{\alpha}\mathsf{A}_{\alpha,\lambda}\frac{1}{\nu(\alpha,\lambda)!}.

Subtracting the two coefficient formulas and applying
Lemma
16
yields

|c_{\lambda}-a_{\lambda}|\leq C_{\ell}N_{r,\ell}^{1/2}\|h\|_{L_{2}(\gamma_{r})}\Delta_{U}.

Moreover, the layer norm satisfies

\|H_{\lambda}\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}\leq\|z^{\lambda}\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}\leq C_{\ell},

where the last inequality follows from Hölder’s inequality and the Khintchine
inequality. Since there are at most
N_{r,\ell}
indices
\lambda
, we get

\left\|\sum_{|\lambda|\leq\ell}(c_{\lambda}-a_{\lambda})H_{\lambda}\right\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}\leq C_{\ell}N_{r,\ell}^{3/2}\|h\|_{L_{2}(\gamma_{r})}\Delta_{U}.

Since
N_{r,\ell}^{3/2}\leq N_{r,\ell}^{2}
, the preceding display is bounded
by

C_{\ell}\|h\|_{L_{2}(\gamma_{r})}\Theta_{U}.

Combining this estimate with the bound on
E
, and using
(
42
), proves

\|P-F\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}\leq C_{\ell}\|f^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\gamma_{d})}\Theta_{U}.

This completes the proof.

I.5
Proof of Proposition
18

The cases
m=0
and
m=1
are immediate, so assume
m\geq 2
. Choose
ordered label lists
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{m}\in[r]
and

\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{m}\in[r]
such that

\#\{p:\sigma_{p}=j\}=\alpha_{j},\qquad\#\{p:\tau_{p}=j\}=\beta_{j}.

With this choice, the two monomials can be written as

z^{\alpha}=\prod_{p=1}^{m}z_{\sigma_{p}},\qquad z^{\beta}=\prod_{p=1}^{m}z_{\tau_{p}}.

Expanding each linear form shows that the top-degree layers keep exactly the
injective terms. Thus

H_{\alpha}(x)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\dots,i_{m}\in[d]\\
\text{all distinct}\end{subarray}}\Bigl(\prod_{p=1}^{m}u_{i_{p}}^{[\sigma_{p}]}\Bigr)x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{m}},

and similarly

H_{\beta}(x)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v_{1},\dots,v_{m}\in[d]\\
\text{all distinct}\end{subarray}}\Bigl(\prod_{p=1}^{m}u_{v_{p}}^{[\tau_{p}]}\Bigr)x_{v_{1}}\cdots x_{v_{m}}.

Let
X\sim\tau_{d}
. The expectation is nonzero precisely when the two
injective tuples have the same underlying coordinate set. Hence we have

\langle H_{\alpha},H_{\beta}\rangle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\dots,i_{m}\\
\text{all distinct}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\pi\in S_{m}}\prod_{p=1}^{m}u_{i_{p}}^{[\sigma_{p}]}u_{i_{p}}^{[\tau_{\pi^{-1}(p)}]}.

We compare this restricted sum with the unrestricted sum

M:=\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m}\in[d]}\sum_{\pi\in S_{m}}\prod_{p=1}^{m}u_{i_{p}}^{[\sigma_{p}]}u_{i_{p}}^{[\tau_{\pi^{-1}(p)}]}.

For fixed
\pi
, the sum over
(i_{1},\dots,i_{m})
factorizes as

\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m}}\prod_{p=1}^{m}u_{i_{p}}^{[\sigma_{p}]}u_{i_{p}}^{[\tau_{\pi^{-1}(p)}]}=\prod_{p=1}^{m}\langle u^{[\sigma_{p}]},u^{[\tau_{\pi^{-1}(p)}]}\rangle.

By orthonormality, this product equals
1
exactly when

\sigma_{p}=\tau_{\pi^{-1}(p)}
for every
p
, and otherwise it equals

0
. Such permutations exist if and only if
\alpha=\beta
. In that case,
their number is

\prod_{j=1}^{r}\alpha_{j}!=\alpha!.

Therefore, we get

M=\mathbf{1}_{\{\alpha=\beta\}}\alpha!.

It remains to control the contribution of tuples with collisions. Since

\langle H_{\alpha},H_{\beta}\rangle
is obtained from
M
by restricting to
tuples with all entries distinct, we have

M-\langle H_{\alpha},H_{\beta}\rangle=\sum_{\pi\in S_{m}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\dots,i_{m}\\
\text{not all distinct}\end{subarray}}\prod_{p=1}^{m}u_{i_{p}}^{[\sigma_{p}]}u_{i_{p}}^{[\tau_{\pi^{-1}(p)}]}.

Using the elementary bound

\mathbf{1}_{\{\text{not all distinct}\}}\leq\sum_{1\leq p<q\leq m}\mathbf{1}_{\{i_{p}=i_{q}\}},

and then taking absolute values, we get

|M-\langle H_{\alpha},H_{\beta}\rangle|\leq\sum_{\pi\in S_{m}}\sum_{1\leq p<q\leq m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\dots,i_{m}\\
i_{p}=i_{q}\end{subarray}}\prod_{c=1}^{m}\big|u_{i_{c}}^{[\sigma_{c}]}u_{i_{c}}^{[\tau_{\pi^{-1}(c)}]}\big|.

Fix
\pi\in S_{m}
and
p<q
, and write
i=i_{p}=i_{q}
. For any

j,k\in[r]
, we have

|u_{i}^{[j]}u_{i}^{[k]}|\leq\frac{1}{2}\bigl((u_{i}^{[j]})^{2}+(u_{i}^{[k]})^{2}\bigr)\leq q_{i}.

Thus the two constrained positions contribute at most

q_{i}^{2}.

For each remaining position, Cauchy–Schwarz gives

\sum_{v=1}^{d}|u_{v}^{[\sigma_{c}]}u_{v}^{[\tau_{\pi^{-1}(c)}]}|\leq 1.

Therefore each constrained collision sum is bounded by

\sum_{i=1}^{d}q_{i}^{2}=\rho.

Summing over
\pi\in S_{m}
and over all pairs
p<q
, we obtain

\big|\langle H_{\alpha},H_{\beta}\rangle-\mathbf{1}_{\{\alpha=\beta\}}\alpha!\big|\leq m!\binom{m}{2}\rho.

Since
m\leq\ell
and
\rho\leq\Delta_{U}
, the desired bound follows.

I.6
Proof of Proposition
19

We first prove the active-direction estimate. Fix
s\in[r]
, and choose an
ordered label list
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{q}\in[r]
such that

\#\{p:\sigma_{p}=j\}=\lambda_{j}
for each
j\in[r]
. The all-distinct layer

H_{\lambda}
admits the injective expansion

H_{\lambda}(x)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}t_{1},\dots,t_{q}\in[d]\\
\text{all distinct}\end{subarray}}\left(\prod_{p=1}^{q}u_{t_{p}}^{[\sigma_{p}]}\right)x_{t_{1}}\cdots x_{t_{q}}.

For a tuple
(t_{v})_{v\neq p}
, write
S=\{t_{v}:v\neq p\}
. Differentiating
the preceding expansion term by term gives the directional derivative formula

(u^{[s]})^{\top}\nabla H_{\lambda}(x)=\sum_{p=1}^{q}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(t_{v})_{v\neq p}\\
\text{all distinct}\end{subarray}}\left(\prod_{v\neq p}u_{t_{v}}^{[\sigma_{v}]}\right)x^{S}\sum_{t\notin S}u_{t}^{[s]}u_{t}^{[\sigma_{p}]}.

The orthonormality of the rows of
U
gives, for each
j\in[r]
, the
identity

\sum_{t\notin S}u_{t}^{[s]}u_{t}^{[j]}=\mathbf{1}_{\{s=j\}}-\sum_{t\in S}u_{t}^{[s]}u_{t}^{[j]}.

Substituting this identity into the derivative formula separates the main term
from the error term. The contribution of
\mathbf{1}_{\{s=\sigma_{p}\}}
is
exactly
\lambda_{s}H_{\lambda-e_{s}}
, with the convention that this term is
zero when
\lambda_{s}=0
. The remaining terms define
R_{s,\lambda}
, giving
the decomposition

(u^{[s]})^{\top}\nabla H_{\lambda}=\lambda_{s}H_{\lambda-e_{s}}+R_{s,\lambda}.

It remains to estimate the remainder. For every
(q-1)
-element set
S
,
the correction factor satisfies

\left|\sum_{t\in S}u_{t}^{[s]}u_{t}^{[\sigma_{p}]}\right|\leq|S|\max_{i}q_{i}\leq C_{\ell}\Delta_{U}.

After grouping the
p
-th summand by the underlying set
S
, its Walsh
coefficients are those of
H_{\lambda-e_{\sigma_{p}}}
multiplied by this
correction factor. Since

\|H_{\lambda-e_{\sigma_{p}}}\|_{L_{2}}\leq\|z^{\lambda-e_{\sigma_{p}}}\|_{L_{2}}\leq C_{\ell}
, Parseval’s identity gives

\|R_{s,\lambda}^{(p)}\|_{L_{2}}\leq C_{\ell}\Delta_{U}.

Summing over
p\in[q]
, with
q\leq\ell
, yields

\|R_{s,\lambda}\|_{L_{2}}\leq C_{\ell}\Delta_{U}.

We now prove the inactive-direction estimate. Let
v\in\mathcal{U}^{\perp}
be
a unit vector. The same injective expansion gives

v^{\top}\nabla H_{\lambda}(x)=\sum_{p=1}^{q}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(t_{a})_{a\neq p}\\
\text{all distinct}\end{subarray}}\left(\prod_{a\neq p}u_{t_{a}}^{[\sigma_{a}]}\right)x^{S}\sum_{t\notin S}v_{t}u_{t}^{[\sigma_{p}]}.

Since
v\perp u^{[\sigma_{p}]}
, the inner sum satisfies

\sum_{t\notin S}v_{t}u_{t}^{[\sigma_{p}]}=-\sum_{t\in S}v_{t}u_{t}^{[\sigma_{p}]}.

Thus
v^{\top}\nabla H_{\lambda}
consists only of remainder terms; write

v^{\top}\nabla H_{\lambda}=R_{v,\lambda}
.

Let
c_{S}^{(p)}
denote the coefficient of
x^{S}
in

H_{\lambda-e_{\sigma_{p}}}
. By Cauchy–Schwarz, the coefficient multiplying

x^{S}
in the
p
-th remainder satisfies

\left|\sum_{i\in S}v_{i}u_{i}^{[\sigma_{p}]}\right|^{2}\leq(q-1)\sum_{i\in S}v_{i}^{2}(u_{i}^{[\sigma_{p}]})^{2}.

Consequently, Parseval’s identity gives the preliminary bound

\|R_{v,\lambda}^{(p)}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}\leq C_{\ell}\sum_{i=1}^{d}v_{i}^{2}(u_{i}^{[\sigma_{p}]})^{2}\sum_{S\ni i}|c_{S}^{(p)}|^{2}.

(72)

Claim 1
.

For every
i\in[d]
, the coefficient localization bound holds:

\sum_{S\ni i}|c_{S}^{(p)}|^{2}\leq C_{\ell}q_{i}.

Proof of Claim
1
.

Set
\eta:=\lambda-e_{\sigma_{p}}
. Since
c_{S}^{(p)}
is the coefficient of

x^{S}
in
H_{\eta}
, Parseval’s identity gives

\sum_{S\ni i}|c_{S}^{(p)}|^{2}=\|\partial_{i}H_{\eta}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}.

Choose an ordered label list
\rho_{1},\dots,\rho_{q-1}
for
\eta
. The
injective expansion of
H_{\eta}
is

H_{\eta}(x)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}t_{1},\dots,t_{q-1}\in[d]\\
\text{all distinct}\end{subarray}}\left(\prod_{a=1}^{q-1}u_{t_{a}}^{[\rho_{a}]}\right)x_{t_{1}}\cdots x_{t_{q-1}}.

Differentiating this expansion with respect to
x_{i}
exposes one label at a
time. In the term exposing the label
\rho_{a}
, the coefficient

u_{i}^{[\rho_{a}]}
appears, and the remaining factor is an all-distinct layer
of degree
q-2
with coordinate
i
excluded from its support. The same
Khintchine and Hölder bounds used above show that this remaining layer has

L_{2}(\tau_{d})
-norm at most
C_{\ell}
. Therefore the derivative norm satisfies

\|\partial_{i}H_{\eta}\|_{L_{2}}\leq C_{\ell}\sum_{a=1}^{q-1}|u_{i}^{[\rho_{a}]}|.

Since
q\leq\ell
, Cauchy–Schwarz gives the bound

\|\partial_{i}H_{\eta}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}\leq C_{\ell}\sum_{a=1}^{q-1}(u_{i}^{[\rho_{a}]})^{2}.

The exposed labels are among the relevant directions, so

\sum_{a=1}^{q-1}(u_{i}^{[\rho_{a}]})^{2}\leq C_{\ell}\sum_{j=1}^{r}(u_{i}^{[j]})^{2}=C_{\ell}q_{i}.

Combining the preceding two estimates proves the claim.
∎

Substituting Claim
1
into
(
72
) gives

\|R_{v,\lambda}^{(p)}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}\leq C_{\ell}\sum_{i=1}^{d}v_{i}^{2}(u_{i}^{[\sigma_{p}]})^{2}q_{i}.

Since
(u_{i}^{[\sigma_{p}]})^{2}\leq q_{i}
, the last sum satisfies

\sum_{i=1}^{d}v_{i}^{2}(u_{i}^{[\sigma_{p}]})^{2}q_{i}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{d}v_{i}^{2}q_{i}^{2}\leq(\max_{i}q_{i})^{2}.

Using
\max_{i}q_{i}\leq\Delta_{U}
, we obtain

\|R_{v,\lambda}^{(p)}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}\leq C_{\ell}\Delta_{U}^{2}.

Equivalently, the
p
-th remainder satisfies

\|R_{v,\lambda}^{(p)}\|_{L_{2}}\leq C_{\ell}\Delta_{U}.

Summing over
p\in[q]
, with
q\leq\ell
, gives

\|R_{v,\lambda}\|_{L_{2}}\leq C_{\ell}\Delta_{U}.

This completes the proof.

I.7
Proof of Lemma
20

Since
P=\mathcal{H}(f^{*})
agrees with
f^{*}
on
\{\pm 1\}^{d}
, we have

\|P\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}=\|f^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}
. By
Theorem
17
, there is a decomposition

P=F+E,\qquad\|E\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}\leq C_{\ell}\|f^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\gamma_{d})}\Theta_{U}.

(73)

Using (
42
), the error bound in
(
73
) may equivalently be written as

\|E\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}\leq C_{\ell}\|h\|_{L_{2}(\gamma_{r})}\Theta_{U}.

We first compare the surrogate
F
with the latent polynomial
h
. Different
Walsh degrees are exactly orthogonal, and Proposition
18

controls the inner products within each fixed degree. Therefore the squared
norms satisfy

\left|\|F\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}-\sum_{|\lambda|\leq\ell}a_{\lambda}^{2}\lambda!\right|\leq C_{\ell}\Delta_{U}\left(\sum_{|\lambda|\leq\ell}|a_{\lambda}|\right)^{2}.

The Hermite identity

\sum_{|\lambda|\leq\ell}a_{\lambda}^{2}\lambda!=\|h\|_{L_{2}(\gamma_{r})}^{2}
, the coefficient bound
(
71
), and the inequality

N_{r,\ell}\Delta_{U}\leq\Theta_{U}
give the key comparison

\left|\|F\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}-\|h\|_{L_{2}(\gamma_{r})}^{2}\right|\leq C_{\ell}\Theta_{U}\|h\|_{L_{2}(\gamma_{r})}^{2}.

(74)

Since
\Theta_{U}\leq 1
, the comparison (
74
) also
implies

\|F\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}\leq C_{\ell}\|h\|_{L_{2}(\gamma_{r})}.

We now transfer the comparison from
F
to
P
. The decomposition
(
73
) gives the deterministic bound

\displaystyle\left|\|P\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}-\|F\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2\|F\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}\|E\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}+\|E\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\ell}\Theta_{U}\|h\|_{L_{2}(\gamma_{r})}^{2}.

(75)

In the last step, we used the preceding bound on
\|F\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}
, the
error estimate for
E
, and the assumption
\Theta_{U}\leq 1
.

Combining (
74
) and
(
I.7
) gives

\left|\|P\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}-\|h\|_{L_{2}(\gamma_{r})}^{2}\right|\leq C_{\ell}\Theta_{U}\|h\|_{L_{2}(\gamma_{r})}^{2}.

Finally, using

\|P\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}=\|f^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}
and
(
42
), we obtain the desired estimate

\left|\|f^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}-\|f^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\gamma_{d})}^{2}\right|\leq C_{\ell}\Theta_{U}\|f^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\gamma_{d})}^{2}.

(76)

If
\Theta_{U}
is sufficiently small, then
(
76
) implies the two-sided bound

(1-C_{\ell}\Theta_{U})\|f^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\gamma_{d})}^{2}\leq\|f^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}\leq(1+C_{\ell}\Theta_{U})\|f^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\gamma_{d})}^{2}.

This proves the stated norm equivalence.

I.8
Proof of Proposition
21

For
s\in[d]
, define

D_{s}:=(u^{[s]})^{\top}\nabla F_{q}.

By Proposition
19
, we have

D_{s}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\sum_{|\lambda|=q}a_{\lambda}\lambda_{s}H_{\lambda-e_{s}}+\mathcal{R}_{s},&s\in[r],\\[8.61108pt]
\mathcal{R}_{s},&s\in[d]\setminus[r],\end{cases}

where the remainder satisfies

\|\mathcal{R}_{s}\|_{L_{2}}\leq C_{\ell}\Delta_{U}\sum_{|\lambda|=q}|a_{\lambda}|.

If

N_{r,q}:=\#\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{N}}^{r}:\ |\lambda|=q\},

then Cauchy–Schwarz gives

\sum_{|\lambda|=q}|a_{\lambda}|\leq N_{r,q}^{1/2}A_{q}^{1/2}.

Therefore, we have

\|\mathcal{R}_{s}\|_{L_{2}}\leq C_{\ell}N_{r,q}^{1/2}\Delta_{U}A_{q}^{1/2}.

Assume first that
s\in[r]
. Reindexing with
\gamma=\lambda-e_{s}
gives

D_{s}=\sum_{|\gamma|=q-1}(\gamma_{s}+1)a_{\gamma+e_{s}}H_{\gamma}+\mathcal{R}_{s}.

Denote the leading term by

L_{s}:=\sum_{|\gamma|=q-1}(\gamma_{s}+1)a_{\gamma+e_{s}}H_{\gamma}.

Using Proposition
18
within degree
q-1
, we obtain

\|L_{s}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}\leq C_{\ell}A_{q}+C_{\ell}\Delta_{U}\left(\sum_{|\gamma|=q-1}|(\gamma_{s}+1)a_{\gamma+e_{s}}|\right)^{2}.

By Cauchy–Schwarz, this implies

\|L_{s}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}\leq C_{\ell}A_{q}+C_{\ell}N_{r,q}\Delta_{U}A_{q}.

Since
N_{r,q}\leq N_{r,\ell}
and
\Theta_{U}=N_{r,\ell}^{2}\Delta_{U}\leq 1
, we
get

\|L_{s}\|_{L_{2}}\leq C_{\ell}A_{q}^{1/2}.

Together with the remainder bound, this yields

\|D_{s}\|_{L_{2}}\leq C_{\ell}A_{q}^{1/2},\qquad s\in[r].

If
s\in[d]\setminus[r]
, then
D_{s}=\mathcal{R}_{s}
, and hence

\|D_{s}\|_{L_{2}}\leq C_{\ell}A_{q}^{1/2}\Theta_{U}.

We first consider the active-active block. If
s,t\in[r]
, then expanding the
covariance gives

\mathbb{E}[D_{s}D_{t}]=\langle L_{s},L_{t}\rangle+O_{\ell}(\Theta_{U}A_{q}).

Using Proposition
18
, the leading inner product satisfies

\langle L_{s},L_{t}\rangle=(G_{q})_{s,t}+O_{\ell}(N_{r,q}\Delta_{U}A_{q}).

Since
N_{r,q}\Delta_{U}\leq\Theta_{U}
, we obtain

\big|(\Xi_{q})_{s,t}-(\widetilde{G}_{q})_{s,t}\big|\leq C_{\ell}\Theta_{U}A_{q},\qquad s,t\in[r].

Next suppose exactly one of
s,t
is inactive. Then we immediately have

(\widetilde{G}_{q})_{s,t}=0.

One derivative has
L_{2}
-norm at most
C_{\ell}A_{q}^{1/2}
, while the other
has
L_{2}
-norm at most
C_{\ell}A_{q}^{1/2}\Theta_{U}
. Therefore, we have

\big|(\Xi_{q})_{s,t}-(\widetilde{G}_{q})_{s,t}\big|\leq C_{\ell}\Theta_{U}A_{q}.

Finally, if
s,t>r
, then both derivatives are inactive remainders. Thus we have

\big|(\Xi_{q})_{s,t}-(\widetilde{G}_{q})_{s,t}\big|\leq C_{\ell}N_{r,q}\Delta_{U}^{2}A_{q}.

Since
N_{r,q}\leq N_{r,\ell}
, this is bounded by

C_{\ell}\Theta_{U}^{2}A_{q}.

Combining the three cases proves the entrywise estimate. The same proof works
uniformly if any inactive basis vector is replaced by an arbitrary unit vector
in
\mathcal{U}^{\perp}
, because Proposition
19
is
uniform over such unit vectors.

I.9
Proof of Proposition
22

Set

E_{q}:=P_{q}-F_{q}.

By (
46
), we have

\|E_{q}\|_{L_{2}}\leq C_{\ell}\|f^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\gamma_{d})}\Theta_{U}.

Since
E_{q}
is homogeneous multilinear of degree
q
, for any

v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, its directional derivative has the expansion

v^{\top}\nabla E_{q}=\sum_{|T|=q-1}\left(\sum_{j\notin T}v_{j}\widehat{E_{q}}(T\cup\{j\})\right)x^{T}.

By Cauchy–Schwarz, this implies

\|v^{\top}\nabla E_{q}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}\leq q\|v\|_{2}^{2}\|E_{q}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}.

Thus, for every unit vector
v
, we have

\|v^{\top}\nabla E_{q}\|_{L_{2}}\leq C_{\ell}\|f^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\gamma_{d})}\Theta_{U}.

For convenience, write

D_{s,q}^{P}:=(u^{[s]})^{\top}\nabla P_{q},\qquad D_{s,q}^{F}:=(u^{[s]})^{\top}\nabla F_{q},\qquad D_{s,q}^{E}:=(u^{[s]})^{\top}\nabla E_{q}.

Since
P_{q}=F_{q}+E_{q}
, these derivatives satisfy

D_{s,q}^{P}=D_{s,q}^{F}+D_{s,q}^{E}.

Expanding the covariance difference gives

(\Gamma_{q})_{s,t}-(\Xi_{q})_{s,t}=\mathbb{E}[D_{s,q}^{E}D_{t,q}^{F}]+\mathbb{E}[D_{s,q}^{F}D_{t,q}^{E}]+\mathbb{E}[D_{s,q}^{E}D_{t,q}^{E}].

Taking absolute values and applying Cauchy–Schwarz yields

\big|(\Gamma_{q})_{s,t}-(\Xi_{q})_{s,t}\big|\leq\|D_{s,q}^{E}\|_{L_{2}}\|D_{t,q}^{F}\|_{L_{2}}+\|D_{s,q}^{F}\|_{L_{2}}\|D_{t,q}^{E}\|_{L_{2}}+\|D_{s,q}^{E}\|_{L_{2}}\|D_{t,q}^{E}\|_{L_{2}}.

The derivative bound for
E_{q}
gives

\|D_{s,q}^{E}\|_{L_{2}}\leq C_{\ell}\|f^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\gamma_{d})}\Theta_{U}

for all
s\in[d]
. The proof of
Proposition
21
gives

\|D_{s,q}^{F}\|_{L_{2}}\leq C_{\ell}A_{q}^{1/2},\qquad s\in[r],

and also gives

\|D_{s,q}^{F}\|_{L_{2}}\leq C_{\ell}A_{q}^{1/2}\Theta_{U},\qquad s\in[d]\setminus[r].

Using (
48
), we have

A_{q}^{1/2}\leq\|f^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\gamma_{d})}.

Therefore, we have

\big|(\Gamma_{q})_{s,t}-(\Xi_{q})_{s,t}\big|\leq C_{\ell}\|f^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\gamma_{d})}^{2}\left(\Theta_{U}\mathbf{1}_{\{s\leq r\ \text{or}\ t\leq r\}}+\Theta_{U}^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\{s>r,\ t>r\}}\right).

Combining this estimate with Proposition
21

gives

\big|(\Gamma_{q})_{s,t}-(\widetilde{G}_{q})_{s,t}\big|\leq C_{\ell}\|f^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\gamma_{d})}^{2}\left(\Theta_{U}\mathbf{1}_{\{s\leq r\ \text{or}\ t\leq r\}}+\Theta_{U}^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\{s>r,\ t>r\}}\right).

If
\Theta_{U}
is sufficiently small, then
Lemma
20
allows

\|f^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\gamma_{d})}^{2}
to be replaced by

\|f^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}
. The uniform version with arbitrary inactive
unit vectors follows from the same unit-vector derivative estimate and the
uniform inactive-direction estimate in Proposition
19
.

I.10
Proof of Theorem
23

Since

P_{\leq L}=\sum_{q=0}^{L}P_{q},

the derivative
(u^{[s]})^{\top}\nabla P_{q}
is homogeneous multilinear of
degree
q-1
. Therefore the contributions from distinct degrees are
orthogonal in
L_{2}(\tau_{d})
. Hence, for
q\neq q^{\prime}
, we have

\Big\langle(u^{[s]})^{\top}\nabla P_{q},\,(u^{[t]})^{\top}\nabla P_{q^{\prime}}\Big\rangle_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}=0.

It follows that

(\Gamma_{\leq L})_{s,t}=\sum_{q=1}^{L}(\Gamma_{q})_{s,t}.

(77)

On the Gaussian side, (
50
) gives

(\widetilde{G}_{\leq L})_{s,t}=\sum_{q=1}^{L}(\widetilde{G}_{q})_{s,t}.

(78)

Subtracting (
78
) from
(
77
), applying
Proposition
22
for each

q=1,\dots,L
, and absorbing the finite number of degrees into
C_{\ell}

proves the entrywise bounds. The same summation gives the stated uniform
bounds involving arbitrary inactive unit vectors.

It remains to pass from the entrywise comparison to the block-operator
comparison. In the orthonormal basis

\{u^{[1]},\dots,u^{[d]}\},

the matrix
M_{\leq L}
is represented by
\Gamma_{\leq L}
, while

U^{\top}\Sigma_{L}U
is represented by
\widetilde{G}_{\leq L}
. Hence the
desired operator norm equals

\|\Gamma_{\leq L}-\widetilde{G}_{\leq L}\|_{{\rm{op}}}.

The active-active block has size
r\times r
. Its entrywise bound therefore
contributes at most

C_{\ell}r\,\|f^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}\Theta_{U}

to the operator norm. For the active-inactive block, the uniform
inactive-direction estimate implies that, for every unit vector

v\in\mathcal{U}^{\perp}
and every
s\in[r]
, the corresponding mixed
bilinear form is bounded by

C_{\ell}\|f^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}\Theta_{U}.

Therefore the active-inactive block has operator norm at most

C_{\ell}\sqrt{r}\,\|f^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}\Theta_{U}.

This is absorbed by

C_{\ell}r\,\|f^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}\Theta_{U}.

For the inactive-inactive block, the uniform inactive-inactive estimate gives
operator norm at most

C_{\ell}\|f^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}\Theta_{U}^{2}.

Since
\Theta_{U}\leq 1
in the regime of interest and
r\geq 1
, this is also
absorbed by

C_{\ell}\|f^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}r\Theta_{U}.

Combining the three block estimates gives

\left\|M_{\leq L}-U^{\top}\Sigma_{L}U\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq C_{\ell}\|f^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}r\Theta_{U}.

Since
\overline{\Theta}_{U}=r\Theta_{U}
, this proves
(
51
).

Appendix J
Proof of Theorem
25

Extend
u^{[1]},\ldots,u^{[r]}
to an orthonormal basis
u^{[1]},\ldots,u^{[d]}
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, with
u^{[r+1]}\ldots,u^{[d]}\subseteq U^{\perp}
.
For any
u^{[s]},u^{[t]}\in\{u^{[i]}\}_{i=1}^{d}
, we focus on the estimate

\displaystyle(u^{[s]})^{\top}\mathbb{E}_{n}\left[\nabla\hat{f}(\nabla\hat{f})^{\top}\right]u^{[t]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{nd^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}u_{i}^{[s]}\partial_{i}\hat{f}(x^{(k)})\partial_{j}\hat{f}(x^{(k)})u_{j}^{[t]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{nd^{2}}y^{\top}K_{\lambda}^{-1}{\mathsf{Diag}}(Xu^{[s]})(K^{\prime})^{2}{\mathsf{Diag}}(Xu^{[t]})K_{\lambda}^{-1}y.

(79)

By the assumption
{\mathcal{H}}(f^{*})\in L_{2}(\tau_{d})
, we can expand
{\mathcal{H}}(f^{*})
into Fourier-Walsh basis:

{\mathcal{H}}(f_{U}^{*})(x^{(i)})=\phi(x^{(i)})^{\top}\mathsf{c}=\sum_{S\subseteq[d],|S|\leq\ell}\mathsf{c}_{S}\phi_{S}(x^{(i)}),

(80)

for some coefficients

\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{S}:=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\tau_{d}}\left[{\mathcal{H}}(f^{*}_{U})x^{S}\right],~~~S\subseteq[d],

(81)

which satisfies
\|\mathsf{c}\|_{2}<\infty
.
Since
\mathbb{E}[y^{2}_{i}]=\mathbb{E}|{\mathcal{H}}(f_{U}^{*})|^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}
we deduce the estimate

\mathbb{E}[\|y\|_{2}^{2}]=n(\mathbb{E}|{\mathcal{H}}(f_{U}^{*})|^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2})=n(\left\|f_{U}^{*}\right\|^{2}_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}).

Markov’s inequality subsequently shows

\left\|y\right\|^{2}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(n\log(n)\cdot(\left\|f_{U}^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2})).

We summarize this observation in the following proposition.

Proposition 26
.

With probability at least
1-1/\log(n)
we have
\left\|y\right\|^{2}\leq n\log(n)(\left\|f_{U}^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}(\tau_{d})}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2})
.

We omit the subscript
U
in
f^{*}_{U}
for simplicity.

Applying Lemma
12
with the two kernels
K
and
K^{\prime}
shows that for any
\epsilon>0
there exist matrices
\Delta,\Delta_{1}
satisfying
\left\|\Delta\right\|_{\mathrm{op}},\left\|\Delta_{1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(d^{(\delta-1)/2+\epsilon})
and

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle=\Phi_{\leq p}D\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}+\rho I_{n}+\Delta_{1},

(82)

\displaystyle K^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\Phi_{\leq p}D^{\prime}\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}+\rho^{\prime}I_{n}+\Delta,

(83)

where we define
\rho:=g(1)-g_{p}(1)
and
\rho^{\prime}:=g^{\prime}(1)-g_{p+1}(1)
, and the diagonal matrices
D
and
D^{\prime}
satisfy
\|D_{{\mathcal{S}}_{k}}-g^{(k)}(0)d^{-k}I_{|{\mathcal{S}}_{k}|}\|_{\rm op}=O_{d}(d^{-k-1})
and
\|D^{\prime}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{k}}-g^{(k+1)}(0)d^{-k}I_{|{\mathcal{S}}_{k}|}\|_{\rm op}=O_{d}(d^{-k-1})
for
k=0,\ldots,p
.

With the expression (
83
) in place of
K^{\prime}
, equation (
J
) becomes

\displaystyle\frac{1}{nd^{2}}y^{\top}K_{\lambda}^{-1}{\mathsf{Diag}}(Xu^{[s]})\left(\Phi_{\leq p}D^{\prime}\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}+\rho^{\prime}I_{n}+\Delta\right)^{2}{\mathsf{Diag}}(Xu^{[t]})K_{\lambda}^{-1}y.

Letting
\beta=K_{\lambda}^{-1}y
and expanding the square gives

\displaystyle~~~(u^{[s]})^{\top}\mathbb{E}_{n}\left[\nabla\hat{f}(\nabla\hat{f})^{\top}\right]u^{[t]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{nd^{2}}\underbrace{\beta^{\top}{\mathsf{Diag}}(Xu^{[s]})\left(\Phi_{\leq p}D^{\prime}\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}\right)^{2}{\mathsf{Diag}}(Xu^{[t]})\beta}_{T_{1}(s,t)}+\frac{1}{nd^{2}}\underbrace{\beta^{\top}{\mathsf{Diag}}(Xu^{[s]})(\rho^{\prime}I_{n}+\Delta)^{2}{\mathsf{Diag}}(Xu^{[t]})\beta}_{T_{2}(s,t)}

\displaystyle~~~~+\frac{1}{nd^{2}}\underbrace{\beta^{\top}{\mathsf{Diag}}(Xu^{[s]})\left(\left(\Phi_{\leq p}D^{\prime}\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}\right)(\rho^{\prime}I_{n}+\Delta)+(\rho^{\prime}I_{n}+\Delta)\left(\Phi_{\leq p}D^{\prime}\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}\right)\right){\mathsf{Diag}}(Xu^{[t]})\beta}_{T_{3}(s,t)}

The following claim is the central ingredient of the proof. Once
T_{1}(s,t)
,
T_{2}(s,t)
and
T_{3}(s,t)
are estimated, the desired bound for
(u^{[s]})^{\top}\mathbb{E}_{n}\left[\nabla\hat{f}(\nabla\hat{f})^{\top}\right]u^{[t]}
follows immediately. Accordingly, the remainder of this section is devoted to the proof of this claim. In particular, the bound for
T_{3}(s,t)
follows immediately from the bound for
T_{1}(s,t)
and
T_{2}(s,t)
using Cauchy-Schwarz.

Recall that

\displaystyle(\Gamma_{q})_{s,t}:=\mathbb{E}\!\big[(u^{[s]})^{\top}\nabla{\mathcal{H}}(f^{*})_{q}\,(\nabla{\mathcal{H}}(f^{*})_{q})^{\top}u^{[t]}\big],\qquad s,t\in[d].

(84)

To simplify the notation, for
0\leq q\leq p+1
, define

A_{q}(s,t):=\sqrt{(\Gamma_{q})_{s,s}}+\sqrt{(\Gamma_{q})_{t,t}},

and define

A_{\leq p}(s,t):=\sum_{0\leq q\leq p}A_{q}(s,t),\qquad B_{\leq p}(s,t):=\sum_{0\leq q\leq p}\bigl((\Gamma_{q})_{s,s}+(\Gamma_{q})_{t,t}\bigr).

Claim 2
.

The following estimates hold for any
\epsilon>0
uniformly over all
s,t\in[d]
:

(a)

\frac{1}{nd^{2}}\left|T_{2}(s,t)\right|={O}_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-2+\epsilon}\right)\cdot\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right).

(b)

\left|\frac{1}{nd^{2}}T_{1}(s,t)-{\sum_{0\leq q\leq p}(\Gamma_{q})_{s,t}+C_{0}^{2}d^{2\delta-2}(\Gamma_{p+1})_{s,t}}\right|\\
=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-\frac{\delta}{2}+\epsilon}\right)\Bigg[B_{\leq p}(s,t)+d^{2\delta-2}\left((\Gamma_{p+1})_{s,s}+(\Gamma_{p+1})_{t,t}\right)\Bigg]\\
+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-\frac{1+\delta}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\right)A_{\leq p}(s,t)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\\
\qquad+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{\frac{-4+3\delta}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\right)A_{p+1}(s,t)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\\
\qquad+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-1-\delta+\epsilon}+d^{-2+\delta+\epsilon}\right)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right),

(c)

\frac{1}{nd^{2}}\left|T_{3}(s,t)\right|=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-{\delta}+\epsilon}\right)\Bigg[B_{\leq p}(s,t)+d^{2\delta-2}\left((\Gamma_{p+1})_{s,s}+(\Gamma_{p+1})_{t,t}\right)\Bigg]\\
+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-\frac{1+3\delta}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\right)A_{\leq p}(s,t)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\\
\qquad+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{\frac{-4+\delta}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\right)A_{p+1}(s,t)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\\
\qquad+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-1-2\delta+\epsilon}+d^{-2+\delta+\epsilon}\right)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right),

J.1
Proof of Item
(a)
of Claim
2

Sub-multiplicativity of the operator norm directly implies

\displaystyle T_{2}(s,t)

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\rho^{\prime}I+\Delta\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\max_{i}|(u^{[s]})^{\top}x^{(i)}|\max_{i}|(u^{[t]})^{\top}x^{(i)}|\left\|K_{\lambda}^{-1}y\right\|_{2}^{2}.

Note that the expression (
82
) directly implies

\displaystyle\left\|K_{\lambda}^{-1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1/(\rho+\lambda-\left\|\Delta_{1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}})=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(1).

(85)

where we use the fact that
D
is positive semi-deifnite since
D
is diagonally dominant. Therefore, taking into account Proposition
26
, we deduce

\left\|K_{\lambda}^{-1}y\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|K_{\lambda}^{-1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|y\|_{2}\leq O_{d,\mathbb{P}}\left(\sqrt{n\log(n)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right)}\right)

(86)

By Hoeffding’s inequality we have for any
t>0
, with probability at least
1-2e^{-t^{2}/2}
over the random data
x^{(i)}
, the following holds for any unit vector
u
:

|u^{\top}x^{(i)}|\leq t.

(87)

Letting
t=\log(d)
and taking the union bound over
\{x^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{n}
and
\{u^{[j]}\}_{j=1}^{d}
, we have

\displaystyle\max_{i\in[n],j\in[d]}\left|(u^{[j]})^{\top}x^{(i)}\right|=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(\log(d)).

With
\left\|\rho^{\prime}I+\Delta\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\leq 2|\rho^{\prime}|^{2}+2\left\|\Delta\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(1)
, consequently, we conclude

\displaystyle\frac{1}{nd^{2}}|T_{2}(s,t)|^{2}={O}_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(\frac{\log^{2}(d)}{d^{2}}\cdot\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right)\right)={O}_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-2+\epsilon}\cdot\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right)\right)

(88)

for any
\epsilon>0

as desired.

J.2
Proof of Item
(b)
of Claim
2

Expanding the square and invoking Theorem
8
, the term
\frac{1}{nd^{2}}T_{1}(s,t)
can be reduced to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{nd^{2}}T_{1}(s,t)=\frac{1}{d^{2}}\beta^{\top}{\mathsf{Diag}}(Xu^{[s]})\Phi_{\leq p}D^{\prime}(I+\widetilde{\Delta})D^{\prime}\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}{\mathsf{Diag}}(Xu^{[t]})\beta,

(89)

where
\left\|\widetilde{\Delta}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(d^{-\delta/2+\epsilon})
.
To simplify the notation, letting

h^{[j]}:=\tfrac{1}{d}D^{\prime}\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}{\mathsf{Diag}}(Xu^{[j]})\beta

we write

\displaystyle\frac{1}{nd^{2}}T_{1}(s,t)=(h^{[s]})^{\top}h^{[t]}+(h^{[s]})^{\top}\widetilde{\Delta}h^{[t]}.

(90)

Observe that
\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}u_{i}X_{i}\right)\Phi_{\leq p}
lies in the subspace
\mathrm{span}\{\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2},\ldots,\Phi_{p+1}\}
. Collecting coefficients with respect to this basis, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\beta^{\top}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}u_{i}X_{i}\right)\Phi_{\leq p}D^{\prime}=\beta^{\top}\Phi_{\leq p+1}E,

(91)

where the sparse matrix
E\in{\mathbb{R}}^{L(p+1)\times L(p)}
corresponds to coefficients.
A direct computation yields

\displaystyle E_{S,J}=\begin{cases}\tfrac{1}{d}D^{\prime}_{J,J}\cdot u_{i},~~&\mathrm{if}~S=J\sqcup\{i\}~\mathrm{or}~J\setminus\{i\};\\
0,~~&\mathrm{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Here
E_{J\setminus\{i\},J}
is induced by coordinate collapse. We observe that each column
E_{:,J}
is a sparse vector with at most
d
non-zero entries.

Next, we analyze
(h^{[s]})^{\top}h^{[t]}
and
(h^{[s]})^{\top}\widetilde{\Delta}h^{[t]}
in equation (
91
) to bound
T_{1}
. Combining these two bounds completes the estimate for
T_{1}
. Specifically, we will prove the following holds with constant
C_{0}=(\rho+\lambda)^{-1}g^{(p+1)}(0)
:

\displaystyle~~~\left|(h^{[s]})^{\top}h^{[t]}-\left(\sum_{0\leq q\leq p}(\Gamma_{q})_{s,t}+C_{0}^{2}d^{2\delta-2}(\Gamma_{p+1})_{s,t}\right)\right|

\displaystyle=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-\delta+\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\right)B_{\leq p}(s,t)

\displaystyle\qquad+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-\frac{1+\delta}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\right)\left(A_{\leq p}(s,t)+\left(d^{\frac{-3+4\delta}{2}}\right)A_{p+1}(s,t)\right)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\qquad+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-1-\delta+\epsilon}+d^{-2+\delta+\epsilon}\right)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right)

(92)

and

\displaystyle\left|(h^{[s]})^{\top}\widetilde{\Delta}h^{[t]}\right|

\displaystyle=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-\frac{\delta}{2}+\epsilon}\right)B_{\leq p}(s,t)

\displaystyle\qquad+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{\frac{3\delta}{2}-2+\epsilon}\right)\left((\Gamma_{p+1})_{s,s}+(\Gamma_{p+1})_{t,t}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-1-\frac{3\delta}{2}+\epsilon}+d^{-2+\frac{\delta}{2}+\epsilon}\right)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right).

(93)

Term
(h^{[s]})^{\top}h^{[t]}
.

We decompose indices of vector (
91
) into
j=0,\ldots,p-1
and
j=p
, which correspondingly decomposes
(h^{[s]})^{\top}h^{[t]}
into two terms:

\displaystyle(h^{[s]})^{\top}h^{[t]}=\left<h^{[s]}_{\leq p-1},h^{[t]}_{\leq p-1}\right>+\left<h^{[s]}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}},h^{[t]}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}\right>.

(94)

Following the same convention, we let
c_{\leq q}
denote the vector
[c_{S}]_{\cup_{S:|S|\leq q}}
.
To simplify the notation, corresponding to each direction
j\in[d]
, we denote

\displaystyle r^{[j]}:=(E^{[j]})^{\top}D_{\leq p+1}^{-1}c_{\leq p+1}.

(95)

Next, we present two key lemmas that show
h
can be approximated by
r
. The proof appears in Appendix
K.1
and
K.2
respectively.

Lemma 27
.

The following estimate holds for any
\epsilon>0
and uniformly for any
j\in[d]

\displaystyle\left\|h^{[j]}_{\leq p-1}-r^{[j]}_{\leq p-1}\right\|_{2}^{2}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(d^{-2\delta+\epsilon})\left\|r^{[j]}_{\leq p-1}\right\|_{2}^{2}+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(d^{-1-\delta+\epsilon})(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}).

(96)

Lemma 28
.

The following estimate holds for any
\epsilon>0
and uniformly for any
j\in[d]
with constant
C_{0}=(\rho+\lambda)^{-1}g^{(p+1)}(0)
:

\displaystyle\left\|h^{[j]}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}-C_{0}\cdot d^{\delta-1}r^{[j]}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(d^{-2+\epsilon})\left\|r^{[j]}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}\right\|_{2}^{2}+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(d^{-2+\delta+\epsilon})(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}).

(97)

The following proposition connects
\left<r^{[s]}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{q}},r^{[t]}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{q}}\right>
to
\Gamma_{s,t}
. The proof appears in Appendix
K.3
.

Proposition 29
.

The following estimate holds for any
q=0,1,\ldots,p

\displaystyle\left|\left<r^{[s]}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{q}},r^{[t]}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{q}}\right>-\left(\Gamma_{q+1}\right)_{s,t}\right|={O}_{d}(d^{-2})A_{q+1}(s,t)\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}+O_{d}(d^{-4})\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2},

(98)

Then, we have the following estimate. The proof appears in Appendix
K.4
.

Proposition 30
.

for any
\epsilon>0
, the following estimates hold:

\displaystyle\left|\left<h^{[s]}_{\leq p-1},h^{[t]}_{\leq p-1}\right>-\sum_{0\leq q\leq p}\left(\Gamma_{q}\right)_{s,t}\right|

\displaystyle=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-\delta+\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\right)B_{\leq p}(s,t)

\displaystyle\qquad+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-\frac{1+\delta}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\right)A_{\leq p}(s,t)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\qquad+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-1-\delta+\epsilon}\right)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right),

(99)

and

\displaystyle\left|\left<h^{[s]}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}},h^{[t]}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}\right>-C_{0}^{2}d^{2\delta-2}\left(\Gamma_{p+1}\right)_{s,t}\right|

\displaystyle=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{\frac{-4+3\delta}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\right)A_{p+1}(s,t)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\qquad+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-2+\delta+\epsilon}\right)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right).

(100)

Plugging the above two estimates into equation (
94
) finishes the bound for (
J.2
).

Term
(h^{[s]})^{\top}\widetilde{\Delta}h^{[t]}
.

First, applying (
94
) with
s=t
, together with
Proposition
30
, gives

\displaystyle\left|\left\|h^{[s]}\right\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}B_{\leq p}(s,s)-C_{0}^{2}d^{2\delta-2}(\Gamma_{p+1})_{s,s}\right|

\displaystyle\leq O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-\delta+\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\right)B_{\leq p}(s,s)

\displaystyle\qquad+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-\frac{1+\delta}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\right)A_{\leq p}(s,s)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\qquad+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{\frac{-4+3\delta}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\right)A_{p+1}(s,s)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\qquad+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-1-\delta+\epsilon}+d^{-2+\delta+\epsilon}\right)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right).

(101)

Here the factor
1/2
appears because

B_{\leq p}(s,s)=2\sum_{0\leq q\leq p}(\Gamma_{q})_{s,s}
.

Next, since

A_{\leq p}(s,s)^{2}=4\left(\sum_{0\leq q\leq p}\sqrt{(\Gamma_{q})_{s,s}}\right)^{2}\leq 2(p+1)B_{\leq p}(s,s),

the weighted AM-GM inequality implies, after applying the estimate with a
smaller
\epsilon
and then renaming it as
\epsilon
, that

\displaystyle O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-\frac{1+\delta}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\right)A_{\leq p}(s,s)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=o_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(1)B_{\leq p}(s,s)+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-1-\delta+\epsilon}\right)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right).

(102)

Similarly, since

A_{p+1}(s,s)=2\sqrt{(\Gamma_{p+1})_{s,s}},

another application of weighted AM-GM gives

\displaystyle O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{\frac{-4+3\delta}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\right)A_{p+1}(s,s)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=o_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(1)d^{2\delta-2}(\Gamma_{p+1})_{s,s}+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-2+\delta+\epsilon}\right)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right).

(103)

Therefore, substituting (
102
) and
(
103
) into (
101
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|h^{[s]}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2}+o_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(1)\right)B_{\leq p}(s,s)+\left(C_{0}^{2}+o_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(1)\right)d^{2\delta-2}(\Gamma_{p+1})_{s,s}

\displaystyle\qquad+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-1-\delta+\epsilon}+d^{-2+\delta+\epsilon}\right)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right).

(104)

Likewise, replacing
s
by
t
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|h^{[t]}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2}+o_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(1)\right)B_{\leq p}(t,t)+\left(C_{0}^{2}+o_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(1)\right)d^{2\delta-2}(\Gamma_{p+1})_{t,t}

\displaystyle\qquad+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-1-\delta+\epsilon}+d^{-2+\delta+\epsilon}\right)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right).

(105)

Consequently, adding (
104
) and (
105
), and using

\frac{1}{2}B_{\leq p}(s,s)+\frac{1}{2}B_{\leq p}(t,t)=B_{\leq p}(s,t),

we get

\displaystyle\left\|h^{[s]}\right\|_{2}^{2}+\left\|h^{[t]}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(1+o_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(1)\right)B_{\leq p}(s,t)

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(C_{0}^{2}+o_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(1)\right)d^{2\delta-2}\left((\Gamma_{p+1})_{s,s}+(\Gamma_{p+1})_{t,t}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-1-\delta+\epsilon}+d^{-2+\delta+\epsilon}\right)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right).

(106)

Finally, by Cauchy’s inequality and AM-GM, the following holds

\left|(h^{[s]})^{\top}\widetilde{\Delta}h^{[t]}\right|\leq\left\|\widetilde{\Delta}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\left\|h^{[s]}\right\|_{2}\left\|h^{[t]}\right\|_{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\|\widetilde{\Delta}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\left(\left\|h^{[s]}\right\|_{2}^{2}+\left\|h^{[t]}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right).

Thus, using

\left\|\widetilde{\Delta}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-\frac{\delta}{2}+\epsilon}\right),

and substituting (
106
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left|(h^{[s]})^{\top}\widetilde{\Delta}h^{[t]}\right|

\displaystyle=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-\frac{\delta}{2}+\epsilon}\right)B_{\leq p}(s,t)

\displaystyle\qquad+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{\frac{3\delta}{2}-2+\epsilon}\right)\left((\Gamma_{p+1})_{s,s}+(\Gamma_{p+1})_{t,t}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-1-\frac{3\delta}{2}+\epsilon}+d^{-2+\frac{\delta}{2}+\epsilon}\right)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right).

(107)

which gives (
J.2
).

Appendix K
Proof of lemmas and propositions for the main results

K.1
Proof of Lemma
27

For notational simplicity, we suppress the superscript
[t]
in

u^{[t]}
. A direct coordinate calculation gives the following identity: for
every
J\in\bigcup_{j=0}^{p}{\mathcal{S}}_{j}
,

\displaystyle(D_{\leq p+1}^{-1}E_{:,J})_{S}=\begin{cases}u_{i},&\text{if }S=J\sqcup\{i\},\\[2.84526pt]
\displaystyle\frac{1}{d^{2}}\bigl(g^{|S|}(0)\bigr)^{-1}g^{|S|+2}(0)\,u_{i},&\text{if }S=J\setminus\{i\},\\[2.84526pt]
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(108)

Consequently, this identity implies the uniform bound

\displaystyle\left\|D_{\leq p+1}^{-1}E_{:,J}\right\|_{2}=O_{d}(1).

(109)

We next restrict to
J\in\bigcup_{j=0}^{p-1}{\mathcal{S}}_{j}
. In this case,

E_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1},J}=0
. Therefore, the corresponding coordinate of
h
satisfies

\displaystyle h_{J}

\displaystyle=\beta^{\top}\Phi_{\leq p+1}E_{:,J}

\displaystyle=\beta^{\top}\left(\Phi_{\leq p}E_{:L(p),J}+\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}E_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1},J}\right)

\displaystyle=\beta^{\top}\Phi_{\leq p}E_{:L(p),J}.

Since
D_{\leq p+1}
is diagonal, we define

\displaystyle\hat{e}_{J}:=(D_{\leq p+1}^{-1}E_{:,J})_{:L(p)}=D_{\leq p}^{-1}E_{:L(p),J}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{L(p)}.

(110)

Thus, when the context is clear, we write
D
in place of
D_{\leq p}
.

By Lemma
13
, applied with
m=2p+1
, the
regularized kernel admits the refined decomposition

\displaystyle K_{\lambda}=\Phi_{\leq p}D\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}+(\rho_{p,2p+1}+\lambda)H,

(111)

where
H
is given by

\displaystyle H=I_{n}+(\rho_{p,2p+1}+\lambda)^{-1}\left[\sum_{k=p+1}^{2p+1}\theta_{k}\,{\mathsf{off}}\!\left(\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{k}}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{k}}^{\top}\right)+\Delta\right].

(112)

Moreover, the perturbation
\Delta
satisfies

\displaystyle\left\|\Delta\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\!\left(\frac{\log n}{d^{\frac{2p+2-p-\delta}{2}-\epsilon}}\right)=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(n^{-1/2}).

(113)

Here the coefficients satisfy

\theta_{k}=g^{(k)}(0)d^{-k}+O_{d}(d^{-k-1})
for

k=p+1,\ldots,2p+1
, and

\rho_{p,2p+1}=g(1)-g_{p}(1)+O_{d}(d^{-1})
.

For notational simplicity, write

\displaystyle\rho:=\rho_{p,2p+1}.

Then
H
can be written as

\displaystyle H=I_{n}+\Delta_{1},

(114)

where the perturbation is

\displaystyle\Delta_{1}:=(\rho+\lambda)^{-1}\left[\sum_{k=p+1}^{2p+1}\theta_{k}\,{\mathsf{off}}\!\left(\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{k}}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{k}}^{\top}\right)+\Delta\right].

(115)

By Lemma
12
, this perturbation satisfies

\displaystyle\left\|\Delta_{1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\!\left(d^{(\delta-1)/2+\epsilon}\right).

(116)

Set

\displaystyle\Lambda:=(nD)^{-1}.

(117)

Since
E_{:L(p),J}=D\hat{e}_{J}
, the Sherman–Morrison–Woodbury formula gives

\displaystyle h_{J}

\displaystyle=\beta^{\top}\Phi_{\leq p}D\hat{e}_{J}

\displaystyle=(f^{*}(X)+{\bm{\varepsilon}})^{\top}K_{\lambda}^{-1}\Phi_{\leq p}D\hat{e}_{J}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}(f^{*}(X)+{\bm{\varepsilon}})^{\top}H^{-1}\Phi_{\leq p}\left(\underbrace{(\rho+\lambda)\Lambda+\frac{\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}H^{-1}\Phi_{\leq p}}{n}}_{=:G}\right)^{-1}\hat{e}_{J}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}H^{-1}\Phi_{\leq p}G^{-1}\hat{e}_{J}+\frac{1}{n}f^{*}(X)^{\top}H^{-1}\Phi_{\leq p}G^{-1}\hat{e}_{J}.

(118)

We will use the following norm bounds. Their proof is given in
Appendix
K.5
.

Claim 3
.

The following bounds hold:

\displaystyle\left\|H^{-1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(1),\qquad\left\|G^{-1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(1).

(119)

We first bound the noise term in (
118
). Define

\displaystyle h_{J}^{(3)}:=\frac{1}{n}{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}H^{-1}\Phi_{\leq p}G^{-1}\hat{e}_{J}.

(120)

Conditioning on
X
and using the independence of
{\bm{\varepsilon}}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{\varepsilon}}}\!\left[\left(h_{J}^{(3)}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}}{n^{2}}\left\|H^{-1}\Phi_{\leq p}G^{-1}\hat{e}_{J}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}}{n^{2}}\left\|H^{-1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\left\|\Phi_{\leq p}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\left\|G^{-1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\left\|\hat{e}_{J}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(n^{-1})\,\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}.

In the last line, we used (
119
),
Lemma
7
, and (
109
). More explicitly,
these bounds give

\displaystyle\left\|H^{-1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(1),\qquad\left\|G^{-1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(1),\qquad\left\|\Phi_{\leq p}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(n),\qquad\left\|\hat{e}_{J}\right\|_{2}=O_{d}(1).

After summing over all
J\in{\mathcal{S}}_{j}
,
j=0,\ldots,p-1
, and applying
Markov’s inequality, we get, for every
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}\sum_{J\in{\mathcal{S}}_{j}}\left(h_{J}^{(3)}\right)^{2}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\!\left(d^{-1-\delta+\epsilon}\right)\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}.

(121)

We next decompose the signal part according to degree. Namely, write

\displaystyle f^{*}(X)=\Phi_{\leq p}\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}+\Phi_{>p}\mathsf{c}_{>p}.

(122)

Substituting this decomposition into the second term in
(
118
) gives
