\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}f^{*}(X)^{\top}H^{-1}\Phi_{\leq p}G^{-1}\hat{e}_{J}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}^{\top}\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}H^{-1}\Phi_{\leq p}G^{-1}\hat{e}_{J}}_{=:h_{J}^{(1)}}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\mathsf{c}_{>p}^{\top}\Phi_{>p}^{\top}H^{-1}\Phi_{\leq p}G^{-1}\hat{e}_{J}}_{=:h_{J}^{(2)}}.

The remaining estimates are supplied by the following two propositions. Their
proofs are given in Appendices
K.6
and

K.7
, respectively.

Proposition 31
.

For every
\epsilon>0
, the following estimate holds uniformly over

\{u^{[j]}\}_{j=1}^{d}
:

\displaystyle\sum_{J:|J|\leq p-1}\left(h_{J}^{(1)}-\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}^{\top}\hat{e}_{J}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\!\left(d^{-2\delta+\epsilon}\right)\left\|r_{\leq p-1}\right\|_{2}^{2}+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\!\left(d^{-p-\delta+\epsilon}\right)\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}.

(123)

Proposition 32
.

For every
\epsilon>0
, the following estimate holds uniformly over

\{u^{[j]}\}_{j=1}^{d}
:

\displaystyle\sum_{J:|J|\leq p-1}\left(h_{J}^{(2)}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\!\left(d^{-1-\delta+\epsilon}\right)\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}.

(124)

We now combine the three pieces. Since

h_{J}=h_{J}^{(1)}+h_{J}^{(2)}+h_{J}^{(3)}
for
|J|\leq p-1
, and since

r_{J}=\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}^{\top}\hat{e}_{J}
, the elementary inequality

(a+b+c)^{2}\leq 3a^{2}+3b^{2}+3c^{2}
gives

\displaystyle\left\|h_{\leq p-1}-r_{\leq p-1}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}\sum_{J\in{\mathcal{S}}_{j}}\left(h_{J}-\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}^{\top}\hat{e}_{J}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 3\sum_{J:|J|\leq p-1}\left(h_{J}^{(1)}-\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}^{\top}\hat{e}_{J}\right)^{2}+3\sum_{J:|J|\leq p-1}\left(h_{J}^{(2)}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+3\sum_{J:|J|\leq p-1}\left(h_{J}^{(3)}\right)^{2}.

Using (
123
), (
124
), and
(
121
), we therefore obtain

\displaystyle\left\|h_{\leq p-1}-r_{\leq p-1}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\!\left(d^{-2\delta+\epsilon}\right)\left\|r_{\leq p-1}\right\|_{2}^{2}+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\!\left(d^{-p-\delta+\epsilon}\right)\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\!\left(d^{-1-\delta+\epsilon}\right)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right).

Since
p\geq 1
, the term
d^{-p-\delta+\epsilon}\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}
is
absorbed by the
d^{-1-\delta+\epsilon}\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}
term. Hence

\displaystyle\left\|h_{\leq p-1}-r_{\leq p-1}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\!\left(d^{-2\delta+\epsilon}\right)\left\|r_{\leq p-1}\right\|_{2}^{2}+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\!\left(d^{-1-\delta+\epsilon}\right)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right).

This proves the desired estimate.

K.2
Proof of Lemma
28

Fix
J\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}
. We begin by substituting the decomposition of
y
into
the definition of
h_{J}
. Namely, we use

y=\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}+\Phi_{\mathcal{Q}}\mathsf{c}_{\mathcal{Q}}+{\bm{\varepsilon}}.

Since

h_{J}=\beta^{\top}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}E_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1},J},

the substitution gives

\displaystyle h_{J}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}^{\top}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}^{\top}K_{\lambda}^{-1}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}E_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1},J}

\displaystyle\quad+\mathsf{c}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\top}\Phi_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\top}K_{\lambda}^{-1}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}E_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1},J}+{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}K_{\lambda}^{-1}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}E_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1},J}.

(125)

To simplify notation, set

e_{J}:=E_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1},J}.

We first estimate the noise contribution. Taking expectation over

{\bm{\varepsilon}}
, conditionally on the design, gives

\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{\varepsilon}}}\left[\left({\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}K_{\lambda}^{-1}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}e_{J}\right)^{2}\right]=\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\left\|K_{\lambda}^{-1}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}e_{J}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

Using the estimates

\|K_{\lambda}^{-1}\|_{{\rm{op}}}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(1)
from
(
85
) and

\|\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}e_{J}\|_{2}^{2}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(d^{p+\delta+\epsilon})\|e_{J}\|_{2}^{2}
from
(
26
), we obtain

\mathbb{E}_{{\bm{\varepsilon}}}\left[\left({\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}K_{\lambda}^{-1}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}e_{J}\right)^{2}\right]=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-p-2+\delta+\epsilon}\right)\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}.

After summing over
J\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}
and applying Markov’s inequality, the preceding
bound yields

\displaystyle\sum_{J\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}\left({\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}K_{\lambda}^{-1}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}e_{J}\right)^{2}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-2+\delta+\epsilon}\right)\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}.

(126)

We next estimate the first deterministic term in
(
K.2
). The proof of the following estimate is given in
Appendix
K.8
.

Proposition 33
.

For every
\epsilon>0
, the following estimate holds:

\displaystyle\sum_{J\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}\left(\frac{1}{n}\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}^{\top}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}^{\top}K_{\lambda}^{-1}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}e_{J}-\frac{1}{\rho+\lambda}\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}^{\top}e_{J}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-2\delta+\epsilon}\right)\sum_{J\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}\left(\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}^{\top}e_{J}\right)^{2}+O_{d}\left(d^{-2p-2-\delta+\epsilon}\right)\left\|\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

(127)

We then estimate the second deterministic term in
(
K.2
). The proof of the next estimate is given in
Appendix
K.9
.

Proposition 34
.

For every
\epsilon>0
, the following estimate holds:

\displaystyle\sum_{J\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}\left(\frac{1}{n}\mathsf{c}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\top}\Phi_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\top}K_{\lambda}^{-1}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}e_{J}\right)^{2}=O_{d}\left(d^{-2p-2-\delta+\epsilon}\right)\left\|\mathsf{c}_{\mathcal{Q}}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

(128)

We now combine the three estimates. First, applying

(a+b+c)^{2}\leq 3(a^{2}+b^{2}+c^{2})
to the decomposition
(
K.2
) gives

\displaystyle\sum_{J\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}\left(h_{J}-\frac{n}{\rho+\lambda}\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}^{\top}e_{J}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 3\sum_{J\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}\left(\mathsf{c}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\top}\Phi_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\top}K_{\lambda}^{-1}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}e_{J}\right)^{2}+3\sum_{J\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}\left(\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}^{\top}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}^{\top}K_{\lambda}^{-1}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}e_{J}-\frac{n}{\rho+\lambda}\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}^{\top}e_{J}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+3\sum_{J\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}\left({\bm{\varepsilon}}^{\top}K_{\lambda}^{-1}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}e_{J}\right)^{2}.

(129)

Multiplying the estimates in Propositions
33
and

34
by
n^{2}
, and then using
(
126
), gives

\displaystyle\sum_{J\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}\left(h_{J}-\frac{n}{\rho+\lambda}\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}^{\top}e_{J}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-2\delta+\epsilon}\right)\sum_{J\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}\left(n\,\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}^{\top}e_{J}\right)^{2}+O_{d}\left(d^{-2+\delta+\epsilon}\right)\left(\|f^{*}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right).

(130)

It remains to rewrite the leading deterministic term in the desired
normalization. For every
J\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}
, the diagonal expansion gives

D_{J,J}=g^{(p+1)}(0)d^{-p-1}+O_{d}(d^{-p-2}).

Therefore, multiplying by
n
and using the scaling of
n
, we have

nD_{J,J}=g^{(p+1)}(0)d^{-1+\delta}+O_{d}(d^{-2+\delta}).

Consequently, the following identity holds:

n\,\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}^{\top}e_{J}=nD_{J,J}\left(D_{J,J}^{-1}\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}^{\top}e_{J}\right).

Substituting the preceding estimate for
nD_{J,J}
, we obtain

n\,\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}^{\top}e_{J}=\left(g^{(p+1)}(0)d^{-1+\delta}+O_{d}(d^{-2+\delta})\right)\left(\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}^{\top}D_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}^{-1}e_{J}\right).

We now substitute this expression into (
K.2
). The result is

\displaystyle\sum_{J\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}\left(h_{J}-\frac{g^{(p+1)}(0)}{\rho+\lambda}d^{-1+\delta}\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}^{\top}D_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}^{-1}e_{J}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-2+\epsilon}\right)\sum_{J\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}\left(\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}^{\top}D_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}^{-1}e_{J}\right)^{2}+O_{d}\left(d^{-2+\delta+\epsilon}\right)\left(\|f^{*}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right).

(131)

Finally, define

C_{0}:=(\rho+\lambda)^{-1}g^{(p+1)}(0).

Also recall the notation

r_{J}:=\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}^{\top}D_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}^{-1}e_{J}.

With these definitions, (
K.2
) becomes

\sum_{J\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}\left(h_{J}-C_{0}d^{-1+\delta}r_{J}\right)^{2}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-2+\epsilon}\right)\sum_{J\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}r_{J}^{2}+O_{d}\left(d^{-2+\delta+\epsilon}\right)\left(\|f^{*}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right).

This is the desired estimate.

K.3
Proof of Proposition
29

We set

P:=\mathcal{H}(f_{U}^{*}),\qquad P_{q}:=[P]_{\deg q}.

W also write
P_{q}=\mathcal{H}(f_{U}^{*})_{q}

By definition, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{J:|J|=q}\sum_{i\in[d]\setminus J}u_{i}\mathsf{c}_{J\sqcup\{i\}}x^{J}=u^{\top}\nabla P_{q+1}.

With this expression, we can deduce

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[(u^{[s]})^{\top}\nabla P_{q+1}(\nabla P_{q+1})^{\top}u^{[t]}\right]=\sum_{J:|J|=q}\left(\sum_{i\in[d]\setminus J}u_{i}^{[s]}\mathsf{c}_{J\sqcup\{i\}}\right)\left(\sum_{i\in[d]\setminus J}u^{[t]}_{i}\mathsf{c}_{J\sqcup\{i\}}\right).

(132)

For any
J\in{\mathcal{S}}_{q}
, we can write
r_{J}
as

\displaystyle r_{J}

\displaystyle=E_{:,J}^{\top}D_{\leq p+1}\mathsf{c}_{\leq p+1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in[d]\setminus J}u_{i}\mathsf{c}_{J\sqcup\{i\}}+\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i\in J}D^{\prime}_{J,J}D_{J\setminus\{i\},J\setminus\{i\}}^{-1}u_{i}\mathsf{c}_{J\setminus\{i\}},

which implies

\displaystyle\left|r_{J}-\sum_{i\in[d]\setminus J}u_{i}\mathsf{c}_{J\sqcup\{i\}}\right|=\left|\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i\in J}D^{\prime}_{J,J}D_{J\setminus\{i\},J\setminus\{i\}}^{-1}u_{i}\mathsf{c}_{J\setminus\{i\}}\right|={O}_{d}(d^{-2})|\sum_{i\in J}u_{i}\mathsf{c}_{J\setminus\{i\}}|.

(133)

Using basic inequality for any
a,b,c,d\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

\displaystyle|\left<a,b\right>-\left<c,d\right>|\leq\left\|d\right\|_{2}\left\|a-c\right\|_{2}+\left\|c\right\|_{2}\left\|b-d\right\|_{2}+\left\|a-c\right\|_{2}\left\|b-d\right\|_{2},

(134)

Eq. (
132
) and (
133
) together gives

\displaystyle\left|\left<r^{[s]}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{q}},r^{[t]}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{q}}\right>-\left(\Gamma_{q+1}\right)_{s,t}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{J:|J|=q}r_{J}^{[s]}r_{J}^{[t]}-\mathbb{E}\left[(u^{[s]})^{\top}\nabla P_{q+1}(\nabla P_{q+1})^{\top}u^{[t]}\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq{O}_{d}(d^{-2})\sqrt{\sum_{J:|J|=q}\left(\sum_{i\in[d]\setminus J}u_{i}^{[s]}\mathsf{c}_{J\sqcup\{i\}}\right)^{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{J:|J|=q}\left(\sum_{i\in J}u_{i}^{[s]}\mathsf{c}_{J\setminus\{i\}}\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle~~~+{O}_{d}(d^{-2})\sqrt{\sum_{J:|J|=q}\left(\sum_{i\in[d]\setminus J}u_{i}^{[t]}\mathsf{c}_{J\sqcup\{i\}}\right)^{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{J:|J|=q}\left(\sum_{i\in J}u_{i}^{[t]}\mathsf{c}_{J\setminus\{i\}}\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle~~~+{O}_{d}(d^{-4})\sqrt{\sum_{J:|J|=q}\left(\sum_{i\in J}u_{i}^{[s]}\mathsf{c}_{J\setminus\{i\}}\right)^{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{J:|J|=q}\left(\sum_{i\in J}u_{i}^{[t]}\mathsf{c}_{J\setminus\{i\}}\right)^{2}}.

(135)

By Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have

\displaystyle\sqrt{\sum_{J:|J|=q}\left(\sum_{i\in J}u_{i}\mathsf{c}_{J\setminus\{i\}}\right)^{2}}\leq\sqrt{\sum_{J:|J|=q}\sum_{i\in J}\left|\mathsf{c}_{J\setminus\{i\}}\right|^{2}}\leq\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}.

(136)

Plugging this back into equation (
135
) yields

\displaystyle\left|\left<r^{[s]}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{q}},r^{[t]}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{q}}\right>-\left(\Gamma_{q+1}\right)_{s,t}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{J:|J|=q}r_{J}^{[s]}r_{J}^{[t]}-\mathbb{E}\left[(u^{[s]})^{\top}\nabla P_{q+1}(\nabla P_{q+1})^{\top}u^{[t]}\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq{O}_{d}(d^{-2})\left(\sqrt{(\Gamma_{q+1})_{s,s}}+\sqrt{(\Gamma_{q+1})_{t,t}}\right)\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}+O_{d}(d^{-4})\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2},

which completes the proof.

K.4
Proof of Proposition
30

We first record two residual-size estimates which follow from the diagonal case of Proposition
29
. Indeed, by applying Proposition
29
with
s=t
, and then summing over the orthogonal components, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|r_{\leq p-1}^{[s]}\right\|_{2}^{2}+\left\|r_{\leq p-1}^{[t]}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=B_{\leq p}(s,t)+O_{d}(d^{-2})A_{\leq p}(s,t)\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}+O_{d}(d^{-4})\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}.

(137)

Moreover, taking square roots componentwise in the same diagonal estimate gives

\displaystyle\left\|r_{\leq p-1}^{[s]}\right\|_{2}+\left\|r_{\leq p-1}^{[t]}\right\|_{2}=O_{d}\left(A_{\leq p}(s,t)+d^{-2}\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}\right).

(138)

Similarly, for the top component, the same argument gives

\displaystyle\left\|r_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[s]}\right\|_{2}+\left\|r_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[t]}\right\|_{2}=O_{d}\left(A_{p+1}(s,t)+d^{-2}\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}\right).

(139)

We now prove (
30
). First, for
u\in\{s,t\}
, the approximation estimate (
96
) gives

\left\|h_{\leq p-1}^{[u]}-r_{\leq p-1}^{[u]}\right\|_{2}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-\delta+\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\right)\left\|r_{\leq p-1}^{[u]}\right\|_{2}+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-\frac{1+\delta}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\right)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

Therefore, applying (
134
) with

a=h_{\leq p-1}^{[s]},\qquad b=h_{\leq p-1}^{[t]},\qquad c=r_{\leq p-1}^{[s]},\qquad d=r_{\leq p-1}^{[t]},

we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\left<h_{\leq p-1}^{[s]},h_{\leq p-1}^{[t]}\right>-\left<r_{\leq p-1}^{[s]},r_{\leq p-1}^{[t]}\right>\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|r_{\leq p-1}^{[t]}\right\|_{2}\left\|h_{\leq p-1}^{[s]}-r_{\leq p-1}^{[s]}\right\|_{2}+\left\|r_{\leq p-1}^{[s]}\right\|_{2}\left\|h_{\leq p-1}^{[t]}-r_{\leq p-1}^{[t]}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle+\left\|h_{\leq p-1}^{[s]}-r_{\leq p-1}^{[s]}\right\|_{2}\left\|h_{\leq p-1}^{[t]}-r_{\leq p-1}^{[t]}\right\|_{2}.

Consequently, substituting the preceding approximation estimate and using AM-GM yields

\displaystyle\left|\left<h_{\leq p-1}^{[s]},h_{\leq p-1}^{[t]}\right>-\left<r_{\leq p-1}^{[s]},r_{\leq p-1}^{[t]}\right>\right|

\displaystyle\leq O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-\delta+\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\right)\left(\left\|r_{\leq p-1}^{[s]}\right\|_{2}^{2}+\left\|r_{\leq p-1}^{[t]}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-\frac{1+\delta}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\right)\left(\left\|r_{\leq p-1}^{[s]}\right\|_{2}+\left\|r_{\leq p-1}^{[t]}\right\|_{2}\right)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-1-\delta+\epsilon}\right)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right).

Next, using (
137
) and (
138
), and using

\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right)^{1/2},

we get

\displaystyle\left|\left<h_{\leq p-1}^{[s]},h_{\leq p-1}^{[t]}\right>-\left<r_{\leq p-1}^{[s]},r_{\leq p-1}^{[t]}\right>\right|

\displaystyle=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-\delta+\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\right)B_{\leq p}(s,t)

\displaystyle+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-\frac{1+\delta}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\right)A_{\leq p}(s,t)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-1-\delta+\epsilon}\right)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right).

Here the first term is kept as
B_{\leq p}(s,t)
, rather than being replaced by

A_{\leq p}(s,t)(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2})
, because

A_{\leq p}(s,t)
already has the scale of
\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}
.

On the other hand, by the orthogonality of the components and Proposition
29
, we also have

\displaystyle\left|\left<r_{\leq p-1}^{[s]},r_{\leq p-1}^{[t]}\right>-\sum_{0\leq q\leq p}\left(\Gamma_{q}\right)_{s,t}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{0\leq q\leq p}\left|\left<r_{{\mathcal{S}}_{q-1}}^{[s]},r_{{\mathcal{S}}_{q-1}}^{[t]}\right>-\left(\Gamma_{q}\right)_{s,t}\right|

\displaystyle=O_{d}(d^{-2})A_{\leq p}(s,t)\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}+O_{d}(d^{-4})\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}.

Since
0<\delta<1
, the last display may be weakened as

\displaystyle\left|\left<r_{\leq p-1}^{[s]},r_{\leq p-1}^{[t]}\right>-\sum_{0\leq q\leq p}\left(\Gamma_{q}\right)_{s,t}\right|

\displaystyle=O_{d}\left(d^{-\frac{1+\delta}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\right)A_{\leq p}(s,t)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle+O_{d}\left(d^{-1-\delta+\epsilon}\right)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right).

Finally, combining the previous two estimates by the triangle inequality gives (
30
).

We next prove (
30
). First, for
u\in\{s,t\}
, the
{\mathcal{S}}_{p}
-component approximation gives

\left\|h_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[u]}-C_{0}d^{\delta-1}r_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[u]}\right\|_{2}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{\frac{\delta-2}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\right)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

Therefore, applying (
134
) with

a=h_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[s]},\qquad b=h_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[t]},\qquad c=C_{0}d^{\delta-1}r_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[s]},\qquad d=C_{0}d^{\delta-1}r_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[t]},

we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\left<h_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[s]},h_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[t]}\right>-C_{0}^{2}d^{2\delta-2}\left<r_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[s]},r_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[t]}\right>\right|

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}d^{\delta-1}\left\|r_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[t]}\right\|_{2}\left\|h_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[s]}-C_{0}d^{\delta-1}r_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[s]}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle+C_{0}d^{\delta-1}\left\|r_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[s]}\right\|_{2}\left\|h_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[t]}-C_{0}d^{\delta-1}r_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[t]}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle+\left\|h_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[s]}-C_{0}d^{\delta-1}r_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[s]}\right\|_{2}\left\|h_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[t]}-C_{0}d^{\delta-1}r_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[t]}\right\|_{2}.

Consequently, substituting the preceding
{\mathcal{S}}_{p}
-component approximation gives

\displaystyle\left|\left<h_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[s]},h_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[t]}\right>-C_{0}^{2}d^{2\delta-2}\left<r_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[s]},r_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[t]}\right>\right|

\displaystyle\leq O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{\delta-1}d^{\frac{\delta-2}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\right)\left(\left\|r_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[s]}\right\|_{2}+\left\|r_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[t]}\right\|_{2}\right)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-2+\delta+\epsilon}\right)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right).

Next, using (
139
), we get

\displaystyle\left|\left<h_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[s]},h_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[t]}\right>-C_{0}^{2}d^{2\delta-2}\left<r_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[s]},r_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[t]}\right>\right|

\displaystyle=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{\frac{-4+3\delta}{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}}\right)A_{p+1}(s,t)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\left(d^{-2+\delta+\epsilon}\right)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right).

It remains to replace

\left<r_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[s]},r_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[t]}\right>

by
\left(\Gamma_{p+1}\right)_{s,t}
. By Proposition
29
with
q=p
, we have

\displaystyle C_{0}^{2}d^{2\delta-2}\left|\left<r_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[s]},r_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[t]}\right>-\left(\Gamma_{p+1}\right)_{s,t}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}^{2}d^{2\delta-2}\left(O_{d}(d^{-2})A_{p+1}(s,t)\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}+O_{d}(d^{-4})\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}\right).

Since
0<\delta<1
, the preceding display implies

\displaystyle C_{0}^{2}d^{2\delta-2}\left|\left<r_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[s]},r_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p}}^{[t]}\right>-\left(\Gamma_{p+1}\right)_{s,t}\right|

\displaystyle=O_{d}\left(d^{\frac{-4+3\delta}{2}}\right)A_{p+1}(s,t)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle+O_{d}\left(d^{-2+\delta}\right)\left(\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\sigma_{\bm{\varepsilon}}^{2}\right).

Finally, combining the last two estimates by the triangle inequality proves (
30
), and the proof is complete.

K.5
Proof of Claim
3

Proof.

The following estimate for any
\Delta
with
\left\|\Delta\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=o_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(1)
:

\displaystyle\left\|(I+\Delta)^{-1}-I\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(\left\|\Delta\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}),

(140)

which can be deduced using Neumann series.

Based on the definition of
D
, we immediately have
\left\|(nD)^{-1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{d}(d^{-\delta})
. Notice that
\left\|\frac{\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}\Phi_{\leq p}}{n}-I_{n}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(d^{-\delta/2+\epsilon})
(Theorem
8
) and

\displaystyle\left\|H^{-1}-I_{n}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{d}(\left\|\Delta_{1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}})=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(d^{(\delta-1)/2+\epsilon}),

(141)

which follows from Eq. (
140
).
Thus we deduce

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}H^{-1}\Phi_{\leq p}}{n}-I\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle=\left\|\frac{\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}\Phi_{\leq p}}{n}+\frac{\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}(H^{-1}-I)\Phi_{\leq p}}{n}-I\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\frac{\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}\Phi_{\leq p}}{n}-I\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}+\left\|\frac{\Phi_{\leq p}}{\sqrt{n}}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\left\|H^{-1}-I\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(d^{-\delta/2+\epsilon}+d^{(\delta-1)/2+\epsilon}).

(142)

With this bound, we can deduce

\displaystyle\left\|G-I\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{d}(d^{-\delta})+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(d^{-\delta/2+\epsilon}+d^{(\delta-1)/2+\epsilon})=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(d^{-\delta/2+\epsilon}+d^{(\delta-1)/2+\epsilon}).

Invoking Eq. (
140
) again we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|G^{-1}-I\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(d^{-\delta/2+\epsilon}+d^{(\delta-1)/2+\epsilon}).

(143)

∎

K.6
Proof of Proposition
31

First, we express
H^{-1}
and
G^{-1}
in (
118
) using feature matrices. The proof appears in Appendix
K.10
.

Proposition 35
.

The following equalities hold:

\displaystyle H^{-1}=I_{n}+\underbrace{\sum_{q=1}^{c_{H}}\left(\sum_{j=p+1}^{2p+1}-\frac{c_{j}}{d^{j}}{\mathsf{off}}\left(\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j}}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j}}^{\top}\right)\right)^{q}}_{:=\Delta_{H}}+\Delta_{1},

(144)

and

\displaystyle G^{-1}

\displaystyle=I_{|L(p)|}+\underbrace{\sum_{r=1}^{c_{G}}\Biggl[-(\rho+\lambda)\Lambda+\tfrac{1}{n}{\mathsf{off}}\left(\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}\Phi_{\leq p}\right)+\frac{\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}}{\sqrt{n}}\Delta_{H}\frac{\Phi_{\leq p}}{\sqrt{n}}\Biggr]^{r}}_{:=\Delta_{G}}+\Delta_{2},

(145)

where
c_{j},c_{G},c_{H}>0
are constants and matrices
\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}
satisfy

\left\|\Delta_{1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}},\left\|\Delta_{2}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(n^{-\tfrac{1}{2}}).

To simplify the notation, we write

\displaystyle A_{H}:=\frac{1}{n}\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}\Delta_{H}\Phi_{\leq p},\qquad A_{G}:=\frac{1}{n}\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}\Phi_{\leq p}\Delta_{G},\qquad A_{HG}:=\frac{1}{n}\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}\Delta_{H}\Phi_{\leq p}\Delta_{G}

(146)

Plugging the expressions of
H^{-1}=I+\Delta_{H}+\Delta_{1}
and
G^{-1}=I+\Delta_{G}+\Delta_{2}
into
\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}H^{-1}\Phi_{\leq p}G^{-1}
and expanding the powers, we see that it can be written as

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{n}\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}H^{-1}\Phi_{\leq p}G^{-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{n}}\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}\Phi_{\leq p}+A_{H}+A_{G}+A_{HG}

\displaystyle~~~+\underbrace{\tfrac{1}{n}(\Phi_{\leq p})^{\top}H^{-1}(\Phi_{\leq p})\Delta_{2}+\tfrac{1}{n}(\Phi_{\leq p})^{\top}\Delta_{1}(\Phi_{\leq p})G^{-1}}_{:=\Delta_{3}}.

(147)

We can immediately deduce that
\Delta_{3}
satisfies

\displaystyle\left\|\Delta_{3}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(1)(\left\|\Delta_{2}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}+\left\|\Delta_{4}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}})=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(n^{-\tfrac{1}{2}}),

(148)

where we use the estimate of
\left\|H^{-1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(1)
(
141
),
\left\|G^{-1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(1)
(
143
) and
\left\|\Phi_{\leq p}/\sqrt{n}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(1)
(Lemma
7
)

The following proposition estimates
A_{H}
,
A_{G}
and
A_{HG}
. The proof appears in Appendix
K.11
.

Proposition 36
.

Let

P_{G}(\Lambda):=\sum_{r=1}^{c_{G}}\bigl(-(\rho+\lambda)\Lambda\bigr)^{r}.

For all

\displaystyle N\in\{A_{H},\ A_{G}-P_{G}(\Lambda),\ A_{HG}\},

the following equalities hold:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[(\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}^{\top}N\hat{e}_{J})^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=O_{d}(d^{-2\delta})(\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}^{\top}\hat{e}_{J})^{2}+O_{d}(d^{-4-2\delta})\cdot\left(\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{c}_{J\setminus\{i\}}^{2}\right)+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(n^{-1})\left\|\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

(149)

Then, we plug the decomposition (
K.6
) into the expression of
h_{J}^{(1)}
and using Cauchy-Schwartz inequality we obtain

\displaystyle~~~{\left(h_{J}^{(1)}-\left<\mathsf{c}_{\leq p},\left(I+P_{G}(\Lambda)\right)\hat{e}_{J}\right>\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 5{\left(\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}^{\top}\left(\tfrac{1}{n}\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}\Phi_{\leq p}+P_{G}(\Lambda)\right)\hat{e}_{J}-\left<\mathsf{c}_{\leq p},\left(I+P_{G}(\Lambda)\right)\hat{e}_{J}\right>\right)^{2}}+5{\left(\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}^{\top}A_{H}\hat{e}_{J}\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle~~~+5{\left(\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}^{\top}A_{G}\hat{e}_{J}\right)^{2}}+5{\left(\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}^{\top}\left(A_{HG}-P_{G}(\Lambda)\right)(\hat{e}_{J})\right)^{2}}+5\left(\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}^{\top}\Delta_{3}(\hat{e}_{J})\right)^{2}.

(150)

Using equation (
148
), the last term
\left(\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}^{\top}\Delta_{3}(\hat{e}_{J})\right)^{2}
can be bounded by

\displaystyle\left\|\Delta_{3}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\cdot\left\|\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}\right\|_{2}^{2}\cdot\left\|\hat{e}_{J}\right\|^{2}_{2}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(n^{-1})\left\|\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

(151)

Taking the expectation of the first term and expanding the square gives

\displaystyle~~~\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}^{\top}\left(\tfrac{1}{n}\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}\Phi_{\leq p}+P_{G}(\Lambda)\right)\hat{e}_{J}-\left<\mathsf{c}_{\leq p},\left(I+P_{G}(\Lambda)\right)\hat{e}_{J}\right>\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}^{\top}\left(\tfrac{1}{n}\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}\Phi_{\leq p}+P_{G}(\Lambda)\right)\hat{e}_{J}\right)^{2}\right]-\left(\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}^{\top}(I+P_{G}(\Lambda))\hat{e}_{J}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}\left(\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}^{\top}(I+P_{G}(\Lambda))\hat{e}_{J}\right)^{2}+O_{d}(\tfrac{1}{n})\left\|\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|(I+P_{G}(\Lambda))\hat{e}_{J}\right\|_{2}^{2}-\left(\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}^{\top}(I+P_{G}(\Lambda))\hat{e}_{J}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=O_{d}(\tfrac{1}{n})\left\|\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}\right\|_{2}^{2},

(152)

where
(a)
follows from Lemma
39
.

For the second, third and fourth terms in equation (
K.6
), we take the expectation and the bound (
149
) immediately applies.

Applying Proposition
43
shows the bound (
149
) and (
152
) hold uniformly for all
u^{[1]},\ldots u^{[d]}
, with an extra multiplicative factor
d^{\epsilon}
. Summing over
J
with
|J|\leq p-1
and applying Markov’s inequality, we have with probability
1-1/\log(d)
, the following holds

\displaystyle~~~~\sum_{J:|J|\leq p-1}\left(\frac{1}{{n}}\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}\Phi_{\leq p}+A_{H}+A_{G}+A_{HG}-\left<\mathsf{c}_{\leq p},(I+P_{G}(\Lambda))\hat{e}_{J}\right>\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(d^{-1-\delta+\epsilon})\log(d)\left\|\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

Combining with equation (
151
), we conclude

\displaystyle\sum_{J:|J|\leq p-1}\left(h_{J}^{(1)}-\left<\mathsf{c}_{\leq p},(I+P_{G}(\Lambda))\hat{e}_{J}\right>\right)^{2}=O_{d}(d^{-1-\delta+\epsilon})\left\|\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}\right\|_{2}^{2}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(d^{-1-\delta+\epsilon})\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2},

where we absorb the logarithmic actor into
d^{\epsilon}
.

Lastly, using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{J:|J|\leq p-1}\left(h_{J}^{(1)}-\left<\mathsf{c}_{\leq p},\hat{e}_{J}\right>\right)^{2}=2\sum_{J:|J|\leq p-1}\left(\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}^{\top}P_{G}(\Lambda)\hat{e}_{J}\right)^{2}+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(d^{-1-\delta+\epsilon})\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}.

(153)

The following claim shows that
\left(\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}^{\top}P_{G}(\Lambda)\hat{e}_{J}\right)^{2}
is of a smaller order then combining it with the equation (
153
) completes the proof. The proof of the claim appears in Appendix
K.12
.

Claim 4
.

The following estimate holds:

\displaystyle\left(\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}^{\top}P_{G}(\Lambda)\hat{e}_{J}\right)^{2}=O_{d}(d^{-2\delta})\left(\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}\hat{e}_{J}\right)^{2}+{O}_{d}(d^{-8-2\delta})\left(\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{c}_{J\setminus\{i\}}^{2}\right).

(154)

K.7
Proof of Proposition
32

The proof follows analogously to the proof of Proposition
31
. For

\displaystyle h_{J}^{(2)}=\frac{1}{n}\mathsf{c}_{>p}^{\top}\Phi_{>p}^{\top}H^{-1}\Phi_{\leq p}G^{-1}\hat{e}_{J},

plugging the expression of
H^{-1}
(
144
) and
G^{-1}
(
145
) gives

\displaystyle\eta_{J}=\frac{1}{n}\mathsf{c}_{>p}^{\top}\Phi_{>p}^{\top}(I+\Delta_{H}+\Delta_{1})\Phi_{\leq p}(I+\Delta_{G}+\Delta_{2})\hat{e}_{J}

As in the proof of Proposition
31
, the terms involving the remainder matrices
\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}
are
easier and are absorbed into the same bound, so it suffices to treat the principal part

X_{J}:=\frac{1}{n}\,\mathsf{c}_{>p}^{\top}\Phi_{>p}^{\top}(I+\Delta_{H})\Phi_{\leq p}(I+\Delta_{G})\hat{e}_{J}.

We decompose both endpoint spaces by exact degree. On the low-degree side, we use the
same notation as in the proof of Proposition
36
:

W_{q}:=n^{-1/2}\Phi_{\mathcal{S}_{q}},\qquad W=[W_{0},\dots,W_{p}],\qquad\Lambda=\sum_{q=0}^{p}\beta_{q}P_{q},\quad\beta_{q}:=\frac{d^{q}}{n}.

On the high-degree side, write

\Phi_{>p}=[\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}},\dots,\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\ell}}],\qquad V_{j}:=|{\mathcal{S}}_{j}|^{-1/2}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j}}\quad(p+1\leq j\leq\ell),

and decompose

\mathsf{c}_{>p}=(\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}},\dots,\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\ell}}).

Exactly as in the proof of Proposition
36
, every summand in
\Delta_{H}
is a finite product of matrices

{\mathsf{off}}(V_{j}V_{j}^{\top})
with deterministic coefficient
O_{d}(1)
, and every word in

\Delta_{G}
is obtained by expanding powers of

B_{0}:=-(\rho+\lambda)\Lambda,\qquad B_{1}:={\mathsf{off}}(W^{\top}W),\qquad B_{2}:=W^{\top}\Delta_{H}W.

Hence, after expanding
I+\Delta_{H}
and
I+\Delta_{G}
, every summand contributing to

X_{J}
is of the form

\frac{1}{n}\,\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j}}^{\top}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j}}^{\top}H_{0}\,\Phi_{\leq p}\,\Xi\,\hat{e}_{J},

where
p+1\leq j\leq\ell
,
H_{0}
is either
I
or one of the words appearing in

\Delta_{H}
, and
\Xi
is either
I
or one of the words appearing in
\Delta_{G}
.

We now expand the low-degree factor
\Phi_{\leq p}\Xi
exactly as in the proof for the
low-degree endpoint words, by inserting the exact-degree projectors
P_{q}
around every
copy of
\Lambda
and using the decompositions of
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
.
This yields a finite representation

\displaystyle X_{J}=\sum_{\eta\in\Omega}\alpha_{\eta}\,\bigl((a_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j_{\eta}}})^{\top}M_{\eta}(P_{q_{\eta}}\hat{e}_{J})\bigr),

(155)

where
|\Omega|=O_{p,c_{H},c_{G}}(1)
, each
M_{\eta}
is a normalized chain whose families
belong to
{\mathcal{S}}_{0},\ldots,{\mathcal{S}}_{\ell}
,
and whose endpoint families are
{\mathcal{S}}_{j_{\eta}}
and
\mathcal{{\mathcal{S}}}_{q_{\eta}}
.
In particular, the endpoint families are always different, since
j_{\eta}>p
whereas

q_{\eta}\leq p
.

Moreover, each coefficient
\alpha_{\eta}
is deterministic and satisfies

\displaystyle|\alpha_{\eta}|=O_{d}\left(\sqrt{\frac{|{\mathcal{S}}_{j_{\eta}}|}{n}}\right).

(156)

Indeed, the outer factor
n^{-1}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j}}^{\top}\Phi_{\mathcal{{\mathcal{S}}}_{q}}
contributes

\frac{1}{n}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j}}^{\top}\Phi_{\mathcal{{\mathcal{S}}}_{q}}=\sqrt{\frac{|{\mathcal{S}}_{j}|}{n}}\;V_{j}^{\top}W_{q},

while all remaining coefficients coming from
\Delta_{H}
are
O_{d}(1)
, and every
additional factor coming from
\Lambda
is one of the
\beta_{q}\leq 1
.

Fix one
\eta\in\Omega
. Since the endpoint families of
M_{\eta}
are different,
Lemma
39
gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl(\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j_{\eta}}}^{\top}M_{\eta}(P_{q_{\eta}}\hat{e}_{J})\bigr)^{2}\right]\leq O_{d}\!\left((nw^{(1)}w^{(m+1)})^{2}n^{-1}\|a_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j_{\eta}}}\|_{2}^{2}\|P_{q_{\eta}}\hat{e}_{J}\|_{2}^{2}\right).

(157)

Note that the weights satisfy

w^{(1)}=|{\mathcal{S}}_{j_{\eta}}|^{-1/2},\qquad w^{(m+1)}=n^{-1/2},

therefore we have

(nw^{(1)}w^{(m+1)})^{2}=\frac{n}{|{\mathcal{S}}_{j_{\eta}}|}.

Substituting this into
157
yields

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl(\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j_{\eta}}}^{\top}M_{\eta}(P_{q_{\eta}}\hat{e}_{J})\bigr)^{2}\right]\leq O_{d}\!\left(\frac{1}{|{\mathcal{S}}_{j_{\eta}}|}\|\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j_{\eta}}}\|_{2}^{2}\|P_{q_{\eta}}\hat{e}_{J}\|_{2}^{2}\right).

Combining this with (
156
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl(\alpha_{\eta}(a_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j_{\eta}}})^{\top}M_{\eta}(P_{q_{\eta}}\hat{e}_{J})\bigr)^{2}\right]\leq O_{d}\!\left(n^{-1}\|a_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j_{\eta}}}\|_{2}^{2}\|P_{q_{\eta}}\hat{e}_{J}\|_{2}^{2}\right).

(158)

Since
|\Omega|=O_{p,c_{H},c_{G}}(1)
, Cauchy–Schwarz in
(23)
gives

\mathbb{E}[X_{J}^{2}]\leq O_{p,c_{H},c_{G}}(1)\sum_{\eta\in\Omega}\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl(\alpha_{\eta}(\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j_{\eta}}})^{\top}M_{\eta}(P_{q_{\eta}}\hat{e}_{J})\bigr)^{2}\right].

Using (
158
) and the orthogonality of the exact-degree blocks,

\sum_{j=p+1}^{\ell}\|\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j}}\|_{2}^{2}=\|\mathsf{c}_{>p}\|_{2}^{2},\qquad\sum_{q=0}^{p}\|P_{q}\hat{e}_{J}\|_{2}^{2}=\|\hat{e}_{J}\|_{2}^{2},

we conclude that

\mathbb{E}[X_{J}^{2}]\leq O_{d}\!\left(n^{-1}\|\mathsf{c}_{>p}\|_{2}^{2}\|\hat{e}_{J}\|_{2}^{2}\right)=O_{d}\!\left(n^{-1}\|\mathsf{c}_{>p}\|_{2}^{2}\right).

Finally, the same bound holds for the terms containing
\Delta_{1}
or
\Delta_{2}
:
these are simpler, since the extra factor
\Delta_{1}
or
\Delta_{2}
is controlled by
its spectral norm, exactly as in the estimates (
148
). Then using hypercontractivity and Markov’s inequality we conclude the proof.

K.8
Proof of Proposition
33

Note that Sherman-Morrison-Woodbury formula for
K_{\lambda}^{-1}
gives

\displaystyle K_{\lambda}^{-1}=(\rho+\lambda)^{-1}H^{-1}-\tfrac{1}{n}(\rho+\lambda)^{-1}H^{-1}\Phi_{\leq p}G^{-1}\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}H^{-1}.

(159)

Then correspondingly, we have the decomposition

\displaystyle~~~\tfrac{1}{n}\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}^{\top}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}^{\top}K_{\lambda}^{-1}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}e_{J}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{(\rho+\lambda)}\underbrace{\left(\tfrac{1}{n}\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}^{\top}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}^{\top}H^{-1}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}e_{J}-\tfrac{1}{n^{2}}\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}^{\top}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}^{\top}H^{-1}\Phi_{\leq p}G^{-1}\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}H^{-1}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}e_{J}\right)}_{:={\mathcal{D}}_{J}}.

We now expand
{\mathcal{D}}
as a whole. Since both summands in
{\mathcal{D}}
involve the decomposition of
H^{-1}
, it is more convenient to classify terms only after expanding the entire expression.

For brevity, write

\Phi:=\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}},\qquad\mathsf{c}:=\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}.

Using

H^{-1}=I_{n}+\Delta_{H}+\Delta_{1},\qquad G^{-1}=I+\Delta_{G}+\Delta_{2},

we obtain

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{J}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\mathsf{c}^{\top}\Phi^{\top}(I_{n}+\Delta_{H}+\Delta_{1})\Phi e_{J}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\mathsf{c}^{\top}\Phi^{\top}(I_{n}+\Delta_{H}+\Delta_{1})\Phi_{\leq p}(I+\Delta_{G}+\Delta_{2})\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}(I_{n}+\Delta_{H}+\Delta_{1})\Phi e_{J}.

We decompose the full expansion of
{\mathcal{D}}_{J}
into three disjoint classes:

{\mathcal{D}}_{J}={\mathcal{D}}_{J}^{\mathrm{lead}}+{\mathcal{D}}_{J}^{\mathrm{corr}}+{\mathcal{D}}_{J}^{\mathrm{rem}}.

The leading part collects the terms with no
\Delta
-factor:

{\mathcal{D}}_{J}^{\mathrm{lead}}:=\frac{1}{n}\mathsf{c}^{\top}\Phi^{\top}\Phi e_{J}-\frac{1}{n}\mathsf{c}^{\top}\Phi^{\top}\Pi_{p}\Phi e_{J},\qquad\Pi_{p}:=\tfrac{1}{n}\Phi_{\leq p}\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}.

The structured correction part collects the terms that contain at least one factor
\Delta_{H}
or
\Delta_{G}
, but no factor
\Delta_{1}
or
\Delta_{2}
. Define

\Pi_{p,G}:=\tfrac{1}{n}\Phi_{\leq p}\Delta_{G}\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}

and

\mathcal{M}_{p}:=\Big\{\Delta_{H}\Pi_{p},\ \Pi_{p}\Delta_{H},\ \Delta_{H}\Pi_{p}\Delta_{H},\ \Delta_{H}\Pi_{p,G},\ \Pi_{p,G}\Delta_{H},\ \Delta_{H}\Pi_{p,G}\Delta_{H},\ \Pi_{p,G}\Big\}.

Then we have the expression

{\mathcal{D}}_{J}^{\mathrm{corr}}:=\frac{1}{n}\mathsf{c}^{\top}\Phi^{\top}\Delta_{H}\Phi e_{j}-\frac{1}{n}\mathsf{c}^{\top}\Phi^{\top}\Big(\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{p}}M\Big)\Phi e_{J}.

Finally, we define

{\mathcal{D}}_{J}^{\mathrm{rem}}:={\mathcal{D}}_{J}-{\mathcal{D}}_{J}^{\mathrm{lead}}-{\mathcal{D}}_{J}^{\mathrm{corr}}.

By construction, every term in
{\mathcal{D}}_{J}^{\mathrm{rem}}
contains at least one factor
\Delta_{1}
or
\Delta_{2}
.

Thus, the three classes are:

1.

{\mathcal{D}}_{J}^{\mathrm{rem}}
: terms containing
\Delta_{1}
or
\Delta_{2}
;

2.

{\mathcal{D}}_{J}^{\mathrm{corr}}
: terms containing
\Delta_{H}
or
\Delta_{G}
, but not
\Delta_{1}
or
\Delta_{2}
;

3.

{\mathcal{D}}_{J}^{\mathrm{lead}}
: terms containing no
\Delta
-factor.

We treat these three classes separately.

Analysis of
{\mathcal{D}}_{J}^{\mathrm{rem}}
.

An analogous analysis to equation (
148
) immediately gives

\displaystyle~~~|{\mathcal{D}}_{J}^{\mathrm{rem}}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\Phi\mathbf{c}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\Phi e_{J}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|\Delta_{1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}

\displaystyle~~~~+\left\|\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\Phi\mathbf{c}\right\|_{2}^{2}(2\left\|H^{-1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\left\|G^{-1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}+\left\|H^{-1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{4})\left\|\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\Phi_{\leq p}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\left\|\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\Phi e_{J}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(d^{-p-\delta}\log(d))\left\|\mathsf{c}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\Phi e_{J}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

(160)

where we use the estimate of
\left\|H^{-1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(1)
(Eq. (
141
)),
\left\|G^{-1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(1)
(Eq. (
143
)),
\left\|\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\Phi_{\leq p}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(1)
(Lemma
7
) and
\left\|\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\Phi\mathsf{c}\right\|_{2}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(\log(d))\left\|\mathsf{c}\right\|_{2}
(Proposition
26
).

Then we take the expectation on both sides and obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|{\mathcal{D}}_{J}^{\mathrm{rem}}|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(d^{-p-\delta}\log^{2}(d))\left\|\mathsf{c}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|e_{J}\right\|^{2}.

(161)

Analysis of
{\mathcal{D}}_{J}^{\mathrm{lead}}
.

Applying Lemma
39
immediately gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{1}{n}\mathsf{c}^{\top}\Phi^{\top}\Phi e)J\right)^{2}\right]=(\mathsf{c}^{\top}e_{J})^{2}+O_{d}(n^{-1})\left\|\mathsf{c}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|e_{J}\right\|_{2}^{2},

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{1}{n}\mathsf{c}^{\top}\Phi^{\top}\Pi_{p}\Phi e_{J}\right)^{2}\right]=O_{d}(d^{-2\delta})(\mathsf{c}^{\top}e_{J})^{2}+O_{d}(n^{-1})\left\|\mathsf{c}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|e_{J}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

Therefore, we can deduce

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[({\mathcal{D}}_{J}^{\mathrm{lead}}-\mathsf{c}^{\top}e_{J})^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{1}{n}a^{\top}\Phi^{\top}\Phi e_{J}-(\mathsf{c}^{\top}e_{J})\right)^{2}\right]+2\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{1}{n}\mathsf{c}^{\top}\Phi^{\top}\Pi_{p}\Phi e_{J}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=O_{d}(d^{-2\delta})(\mathsf{c}^{\top}e_{J})^{2}+O_{d}(n^{-1})\left\|\mathsf{c}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|e_{J}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

(162)

Analysis of
{\mathcal{D}}_{J}^{\mathrm{corr}}
.

We use the following proposition to bound each summand in
{\mathcal{D}}_{J}^{\mathrm{corr}}
. The proof appears in Appendix
K.13
.

Proposition 37
.

Define

\Pi_{p}:=\tfrac{1}{n}\Phi_{\leq p}\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top},\qquad\Pi_{p,G}:=\tfrac{1}{n}\Phi_{\leq p}\,\Delta_{G}\,\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top},

and the finite collection

\mathcal{M}_{p}:=\Big\{\Delta_{H}\Pi_{p},\ \Pi_{p}\Delta_{H},\ \Delta_{H}\Pi_{p}\Delta_{H},\ \Delta_{H}\Pi_{p,G},\ \Pi_{p,G}\Delta_{H},\ \Delta_{H}\Pi_{p,G}\Delta_{H},\ \Pi_{p,G}\Big\}.

Then, for every
M\in\mathcal{M}_{p}
, the following holds

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\,\mathsf{c}^{\top}\Phi^{\top}M\Phi\,e_{J}\right)^{2}\right]=O_{d}(d^{-2\delta})(\mathsf{c}^{\top}e_{J})^{2}+O_{d}(n^{-1})\left\|\mathsf{c}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|e_{J}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

(163)

Using Cauchy Schwartz inequality we obtain

\displaystyle({\mathcal{D}}_{J}^{\mathrm{corr}})^{2}\leq|{\mathcal{M}}_{p}|\sum_{M\in{\mathcal{M}}_{p}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\,\mathsf{c}^{\top}\Phi^{\top}M\Phi\,e_{J}\right)^{2}\right]=O_{d}(d^{-2\delta})(\mathsf{c}^{\top}e_{J})^{2}+O_{d}(n^{-1})\left\|\mathsf{c}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|e_{J}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

(164)

Lastly, combining equation (
K.8
), (
161
) and (
164
) and using Cauchy-Schwartz inequality again gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[({\mathcal{D}}-\mathsf{c}^{\top}e_{J})^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 3\mathbb{E}\left[({\mathcal{D}}_{J}^{\mathrm{lead}}-\mathsf{c}^{\top}e_{J})^{2}\right]+3\mathbb{E}\left[({\mathcal{D}}_{J}^{\mathrm{rem}})^{2}\right]+3\mathbb{E}\left[({\mathcal{D}}_{J}^{\mathrm{corr}})^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=O_{d}(d^{-2\delta})(\mathsf{c}^{\top}e_{J})^{2}+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(d^{-p-\delta})\log(d)\left\|\mathsf{c}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|e_{J}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

(165)

Using hypercontractivity, we have the following holds uniformly for all
\{u^{[j]}\}_{j=1}^{d}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[({\mathcal{D}}-\mathsf{c}^{\top}e_{J})^{2}\right]=O_{d}(d^{-2\delta+\epsilon})(\mathsf{c}^{\top}e_{J})^{2}+O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(d^{-p-\delta+\epsilon})\left\|\mathsf{c}\right\|_{2}^{2}\left\|e_{J}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

(166)

With
\left\|e_{J}\right\|_{2}=O_{d}(d^{-p-1})
, taking the summation over
J\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}
and using Markov’s inequality completes the proof.

K.9
Proof of Proposition
34

Using the decomposition of
K_{\lambda}^{-1}
in (
159
), we obtain

\displaystyle~~~~\frac{1}{n}\mathsf{c}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\top}\Phi_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\top}K_{\lambda}^{-1}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}e_{J}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(\rho+\lambda)n}\,\mathsf{c}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\top}\Phi_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\top}H^{-1}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}e_{J}

\displaystyle~~~~-\frac{1}{(\rho+\lambda)n^{2}}\,\mathsf{c}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\top}\Phi_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\top}H^{-1}\Phi_{\leq p}G^{-1}\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}H^{-1}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}e_{J}.

(167)

It therefore suffices to bound the two terms on the right-hand side.

We expand
H^{-1}
and
G^{-1}
using (
144
) and (
145
), and decompose

\Phi_{\leq p}
into exact-degree blocks exactly as in the proof of
Proposition
33
. In particular, every occurrence of an
off-diagonal factor is expanded as

{\mathsf{off}}(B)=B-{\mathsf{Diag}}(B).

Thus each of the two terms in (
K.9
) becomes a finite sum
of expressions of the form

c_{\eta}\,\mathsf{c}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\top}M_{\eta}e_{J},

where
c_{\eta}
is deterministic, the number of summands is
O_{p,c_{H},c_{G}}(1)
, and

M_{\eta}
is a matrix chain of the form covered by
Lemma
39
. The left endpoint family of
M_{\eta}
is always

\mathcal{Q}
, while the right endpoint family is always
{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}
.

Now the key simplification is that

\mathcal{Q}\cap{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}=\varnothing.

Hence the endpoint families in every such chain are distinct, so the leading term in
Lemma
39
vanishes automatically. Therefore, for every
\eta
,

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl(\mathsf{c}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\top}M_{\eta}e_{J}\bigr)^{2}\right]=O_{d}\!\left((nw_{\eta}^{(1)}w_{\eta}^{(m_{\eta}+1)})^{2}\,n^{-1}\,\|\mathsf{c}_{\mathcal{Q}}\|_{2}^{2}\|e_{J}\|_{2}^{2}\right).

The deterministic coefficients
c_{\eta}
are estimated exactly as in the proof of
Proposition
33
; in particular,

|c_{\eta}|^{2}(nw_{\eta}^{(1)}w_{\eta}^{(m_{\eta}+1)})^{2}n^{-1}=O_{d}(n^{-1}).

Since there are only
O_{p,c_{H},c_{G}}(1)
summands, we obtain

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{1}{n}\mathsf{c}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\top}\Phi_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\top}H^{-1}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}e_{J}\right)^{2}\right]=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\!\left(d^{-p-\delta+\epsilon}\,\|\mathsf{c}_{\mathcal{Q}}\|_{2}^{2}\|e_{J}\|_{2}^{2}\right),

(168)

and similarly

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\mathsf{c}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\top}\Phi_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\top}H^{-1}\Phi_{\leq p}G^{-1}\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}H^{-1}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}e_{J}\right)^{2}\right]=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\!\left(d^{-p-\delta+\epsilon}\,\|\mathsf{c}_{\mathcal{Q}}\|_{2}^{2}\|e_{J}\|_{2}^{2}\right).

(169)

Substituting (
168
) and
(
169
) into (
K.9
)
and using Cauchy-Schwartz inequality gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{1}{n}\mathsf{c}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\top}\Phi_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\top}K_{\lambda}^{-1}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}e_{J}\right)^{2}\right]=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}\!\left(d^{-p-\delta+\epsilon}\,\|\mathsf{c}_{\mathcal{Q}}\|_{2}^{2}\|e_{J}\|_{2}^{2}\right).

(170)

With
\left\|e_{J}\right\|_{2}=O_{d}(d^{-p-1})
, then taking the summation over
J\in{\mathcal{S}}_{p}
and applying Markov’s inequality completes the proof.

K.10
Proof of Proposition
35

Note that for any
\Delta=o_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(1)
, we can express
(I+\Delta)^{-1}
using the Neumann series:

\displaystyle(I+\Delta)^{-1}=I+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(-\Delta)^{k}.

We compute the inverse of
H
(
114
) using Neumann series yielding

\displaystyle H^{-1}

\displaystyle=I_{n}+\sum_{q=1}^{\infty}\left(\sum_{j=p+1}^{2p+1}-\underbrace{(\rho+\lambda)^{-1}d^{j}\theta_{j}}_{:=c_{j}}d^{-j}{{\mathsf{off}}(\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j}}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j}}^{\top})}-(\rho+\lambda)^{-1}\Delta\right)^{q}.

(171)

Note that the triangle inequality on Eq. (
31
) and (
32
) shows for
j\geq p+1
we have

\displaystyle d^{-j}{{\mathsf{off}}(\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j}}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j}}^{\top})}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(d^{(p+\delta-j)/2+\epsilon})=o_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(1),

(172)

where
{\mathsf{off}}(\cdot)
denotes the off-diagonal component of a matrix.
Next, we show that we can truncate higher-order terms in
H^{-1}
at constant order to achieve a desired error on the order of
O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(n^{-\tfrac{1}{2}})
.

We set a constant
c_{H}:=\lceil\frac{p-1+2\delta+2\epsilon}{1-\delta-2\epsilon}\rceil
.
Using the triangle inequality and the formula for the summation of a geometric series, we can derive

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{q=c_{H}+1}^{\infty}\left(\sum_{j=p+1}^{2p+1}-c_{j}d^{-j}{{\mathsf{off}}(\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j}}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j}}^{\top})}+(\rho+\lambda)^{-1}\Delta\right)^{q}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{q=c_{H}+1}^{\infty}(O_{d,\mathbb{P}}(d^{(\delta-1)/2+\epsilon}))^{q}

\displaystyle=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(d^{\left((\delta-1)/2+\epsilon\right)(c_{H}+1)})

\displaystyle=O_{d,\mathbb{P}}(n^{-\tfrac{1}{2}}).

We expand the binomial for each
q
and bound the terms that contain
\Delta
, which yields

\displaystyle H^{-1}

\displaystyle=I_{n}+\sum_{q=1}^{c_{H}}\left(\sum_{j=p+1}^{2p+1}-c_{j}d^{-j}{{\mathsf{off}}(\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j}}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j}}^{\top})}+(\rho+\lambda)^{-1}\Delta\right)^{q}

\displaystyle=I_{n}+\sum_{q=1}^{c_{H}}\left(\sum_{j=p+1}^{2p+1}-c_{j}d^{-j}{{\mathsf{off}}(\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j}}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j}}^{\top})}\right)^{q}+\Delta_{1},

(173)

for some
\Delta_{1}
satisfying

\left\|\Delta_{1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(n^{-\tfrac{1}{2}})
. We denote

\Delta_{H}:=\sum_{q=1}^{c_{H}}\left(\sum_{j=p+1}^{2p+1}-c_{j}d^{-j}{{\mathsf{off}}(\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j}}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j}}^{\top})}\right)^{q},

which satisfies
\left\|\Delta_{H}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(d^{-\tfrac{1-\delta}{2}+\epsilon})
with a quick calculation using Eq. (
172
).

We now plug the expression (
K.10
) for
H^{-1}
into
G
in Eq. (
118
). We further substitute
\frac{\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}\Phi_{\leq p}}{n}
for
{\mathsf{off}}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}\Phi_{\leq p}}{n}\right)+I_{L(p)}
and we obtain

\displaystyle G=(\rho+\lambda)\Lambda+I+{\mathsf{off}}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}\Phi_{\leq p}}{n}\right)+\frac{\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}}{\sqrt{n}}\Delta_{H}\frac{\Phi_{\leq p}}{\sqrt{n}}+\Delta_{2},

with
\left\|\Delta_{2}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\left\|\Phi_{\leq p}/\sqrt{n}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\left\|\Delta_{1}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(n^{-\tfrac{1}{2}})
.

Expressing
G^{-1}
using Neumann series again gives us

\displaystyle G^{-1}=I+\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\left(-(\rho+\lambda)\Lambda-{\mathsf{off}}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}\Phi_{\leq p}}{n}\right)-\frac{\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}}{\sqrt{n}}\Delta_{H}\frac{\Phi_{\leq p}}{\sqrt{n}}+\Delta_{2}\right)^{r},

Similarly, we can truncate the infinite summation while maintaining the same error order. Note that the spectral norm of each item in the parentheses is of the order
o_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(1)
, which can be deduced using equation (
172
) and the following estimates:

\displaystyle\left\|\Lambda\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{d}(d^{-\delta}),\qquad\left\|{\mathsf{off}}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}\Phi_{\leq p}}{n}\right)\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(d^{-\tfrac{\delta}{2}+\epsilon}),

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}}{\sqrt{n}}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(1),\qquad\left\|\Delta_{2}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(n^{-1/2}),

where particularly the second estimate can be deduced from Lemma
9
since
{\mathsf{diag}}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}\Phi_{\leq p}}{n}\right)=I_{n}
.

Therefore, an analogous argument for
H^{-1}
shows that we can find a constant
c_{G}=\max(c_{H},\lceil\frac{p+\epsilon}{\delta-2\epsilon}\rceil)
and a matrix
\Delta_{3}
that satisfies
\left\|\Delta_{3}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(n^{-\tfrac{1}{2}})
such that the following holds

\displaystyle G^{-1}=I+\sum_{r=1}^{c_{G}}\left(-(\rho+\lambda)\Lambda^{-1}-{\mathsf{off}}\left(\frac{\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}\Phi_{\leq p}}{n}\right)-\frac{\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}}{\sqrt{n}}\Delta_{H}\frac{\Phi_{\leq p}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{r}+\Delta_{3}.

(174)

Then we conclude the proof for Eq. (
145
).

K.11
Proof of Proposition
36

To simplify the notation, write

W:=n^{-1/2}\Phi_{\leq p},\qquad V_{j}:=|{\mathcal{S}}_{j}|^{-1/2}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j}}\quad(p+1\leq j\leq 2p+1).

Then we have

\frac{1}{n}\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}\Phi_{\leq p}=W^{\top}W=I+{\mathsf{off}}(W^{\top}W),

and for
p+1\leq j\leq 2p+1
,

-\frac{c_{j}}{d^{j}}{\mathsf{off}}(\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j}}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{j}}^{\top})=c^{\prime}_{j}\,{\mathsf{off}}(V_{j}V_{j}^{\top}),\qquad c^{\prime}_{j}:=-c_{j}\,\frac{|{\mathcal{S}}_{j}|}{d^{j}}=O_{d}(1).

Hence every summand in
\Delta_{H}
is a finite product of matrices

{\mathsf{off}}(V_{j}V_{j}^{\top})
with deterministic coefficient of the order
O_{d}(1)
.

We also write

R_{G}:=A_{G}-P_{G}(\Lambda),

and denote

B_{0}:=-(\rho+\lambda)\Lambda,\qquad B_{1}:={\mathsf{off}}(W^{\top}W),\qquad B_{2}:=W^{\top}\Delta_{H}W.

Then we have

\Delta_{G}=\sum_{r=1}^{c_{G}}(B_{0}+B_{1}+B_{2})^{r},

and consequently

A_{H}=B_{2},\qquad A_{G}=(I+B_{1})\Delta_{G},\qquad A_{HG}=B_{2}\Delta_{G}.

We now decompose the low-degree space according to exact degree.
For
0\leq q\leq p
, let

W_{q}:=n^{-1/2}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{q}},

and let
P_{q}:{\mathbb{R}}^{L(p)}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{L(p)}
be the orthogonal projector onto the
coordinates indexed by
{\mathcal{S}}_{q}
. Then

W=[W_{0},\dots,W_{p}],\qquad W_{q}=WP_{q},\qquad I=\sum_{q=0}^{p}P_{q},\qquad\Lambda=\sum_{q=0}^{p}\beta_{q}P_{q},\quad\beta_{q}:=\frac{d^{q}}{n}.

We have the decomposition

\displaystyle B_{1}={\mathsf{off}}(W^{\top}W)=\sum_{q=0}^{p}{\mathsf{off}}(W_{q}^{\top}W_{q})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq q,q^{\prime}\leq p\\
q\neq q^{\prime}\end{subarray}}W_{q}^{\top}W_{q^{\prime}}.

(175)

Next, write

\Delta_{H}=\sum_{\omega\in\Omega_{H}}c_{\omega}H_{\omega},\qquad H_{\omega}:=\prod_{\ell=1}^{m_{\omega}}{\mathsf{off}}\!\bigl(V_{j_{\omega,\ell}}V_{j_{\omega,\ell}}^{\top}\bigr),

where
\Omega_{H}
is a finite index set and each
c_{\omega}=O_{d}(1)
.
Then we have

\displaystyle B_{2}=\sum_{\omega\in\Omega_{H}}\sum_{q,q^{\prime}=0}^{p}c_{\omega}\,W_{q}^{\top}H_{\omega}W_{q^{\prime}}.

(176)

Thus every word contributing to
A_{H}
,
R_{G}
, or
A_{HG}
can be expanded
using (
175
), (
176
) and

\displaystyle B_{0}=-(\rho+\lambda)\sum_{q=0}^{p}\beta_{q}P_{q}.

(177)

Since
p,c_{H},c_{G}
are fixed, this produces only
O_{p,c_{H},c_{G}}(1)
summands.

We also define the blockwise quantities

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{t}(b_{1},b_{2}):=\sum_{q=0}^{p}\beta_{q}^{2t}\bigl((P_{q}b_{1})^{\top}(P_{q}b_{2})\bigr)^{2},\qquad t\geq 0.

(178)

Next we present the key claim. The proof appears in Appendix
K.14
.

Proposition 38
.

Let
T
be one expanded word contributing to
A_{H}
,
R_{G}
, or
A_{HG}
.
Assume that
T
contains exactly
s
copies of
B_{0}
, and that
T
contains
at least one off-diagonal factor, namely either a factor
{\mathsf{off}}(W_{q}^{\top}W_{q})

or one of the factors inside some
H_{\omega}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[(b_{1}^{\top}Tb_{2})^{2}\right]\leq\sum_{t=0}^{s}O_{d}(d^{-2\delta})\,\mathcal{B}_{t}(b_{1},b_{2})+O_{d}\!\left(n^{-1}\|b_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\|b_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\right).

(179)

We now return to
A_{H}
,
R_{G}
, and
A_{HG}
.

The pure
\Lambda
-words occur only when we take the identity part of
W^{\top}W

and every factor in
\Delta_{G}
is
B_{0}
; collecting these terms gives precisely

P_{G}(\Lambda)=\sum_{r=1}^{c_{G}}\bigl(-(\rho+\lambda)\Lambda\bigr)^{r}.

Hence, by definition, every word contributing to
R_{G}
contains at least one
off-diagonal factor. The same is clearly true for every word in
A_{H}=B_{2}
and
in
A_{HG}=B_{2}\Delta_{G}
.

Applying the claim word-by-word, and using that each word contains at most
c_{G}

copies of
B_{0}
, we obtain, for each
X\in\{A_{H},R_{G},A_{HG}\}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[(b_{1}^{\top}Xb_{2})^{2}\right]\leq\sum_{t=0}^{c_{G}}O_{d}(d^{-2\delta})\,\mathcal{B}_{t}(b_{1},b_{2})+O_{d}\!\left(n^{-1}\|b_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\|b_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\right).

(180)

The final piece of the proof is the upper bound for
\sum_{t=0}^{s}\mathcal{B}_{t}(b_{1},b_{2})
. Combining the bound below with Eq. (
180
) completes the proof.

Claim 5
.

The following estimate holds:

\displaystyle\sum_{t=0}^{c_{G}}{\mathcal{B}}_{t}(\mathsf{c}_{\leq p},\hat{e}_{J})=O_{d}(1)\left(\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}\hat{e}_{J}\right)^{2}+O_{d}(d^{-4})\cdot\left(\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{c}_{J\setminus\{i\}}^{2}\right).

(181)

K.12
Proof of Claim
4

Expanding
P_{G}(\Lambda)
gives

\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}^{\top}P_{G}(\Lambda)\hat{e}_{J}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}^{\top}\left(\sum_{r=1}^{c_{G}}\bigl(-(\rho+\lambda)\Lambda\bigr)^{r}\right)\hat{e}_{J}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{c_{G}}(-(\rho+\lambda))^{r}\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}^{\top}\Lambda^{r}\hat{e}_{J}.

(182)

Talking the square and applying Cauchy–Schwarz inequality yields

\displaystyle\left(\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}^{\top}P_{G}(\Lambda)\hat{e}_{J}\right)^{2}\leq c_{G}\sum_{r=1}^{c_{G}}(\rho+\lambda)^{2r}\left(\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}^{\top}\Lambda^{r}\hat{e}_{J}\right)^{2}.

For each
r=1,\ldots,c_{G}
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}^{\top}\Lambda^{r}\hat{e}_{J}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}^{\top}(nD)^{-r}D^{-1}E_{:L(p),J}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{i\in[d]\setminus J}(nD_{J\sqcup\{i\}})^{-r}\mathsf{c}_{J\sqcup\{i\}}u_{i}+{(nD_{J\setminus\{i\}})^{-r}d^{-1}D^{\prime}_{J,J}D_{J\setminus\{i\}}^{-1}\sum_{i\in S}\mathsf{c}_{J\setminus\{i\}}u_{i}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq O_{d}(d^{-2r\left(p+\delta-|J|-1\right)})\underbrace{\left(\sum_{i\in[d]\setminus J}\mathsf{c}_{J\sqcup\{i\}}u_{i}\right)^{2}}_{:={\mathcal{I}}^{2}}

\displaystyle~~~~+O_{d}(d^{-2r\left(p+\delta-|J|+1\right)})\underbrace{\left(d^{-1}D^{\prime}_{J,J}D_{J\setminus\{i\}}^{-1}\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{c}_{J\setminus\{i\}}u_{i}\right)^{2}}_{:={\mathcal{J}}^{2}},

where we use the basic inequality
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2(a^{2}+b^{2})
in the last inequality.

Now using Young’s inequality
ax^{2}+by^{2}\leq 2a(x+y)^{2}+(2a+b)y^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle~~~O_{d}(d^{-2r\left(p+\delta-|J|-1\right)}){\mathcal{I}}^{2}+O_{d}(d^{-2r\left(p+\delta-|J|+1\right)}){\mathcal{J}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq O_{d}(d^{-2r\left(p+\delta-|J|-1\right)})({\mathcal{I}}+{\mathcal{J}})^{2}+O_{d}(d^{-2r\left(p+\delta-|J|+1\right)}){\mathcal{J}}^{2}

\displaystyle=O_{d}(d^{-2r\left(p+\delta-|J|-1\right)})\left(\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}\hat{e}_{J}\right)^{2}+O_{d}(d^{-2r\left(p+\delta-|J|+1\right)})O_{d}(d^{-4})\cdot\left(\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{c}_{J\setminus\{i\}}^{2}\right),

where we apply the bound for
{\mathcal{J}}^{2}
from equation (
195
) in the last equality.

When
r=1
and
|J|=p-1
, we have the smallest upper bound which is

\displaystyle O_{d}(d^{-2\delta})\left(\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}\hat{e}_{J}\right)^{2}+{O}_{d}(d^{-8-2\delta})\left(\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{c}_{J\setminus\{i\}}^{2}\right).

(183)

Then we complete the proof.

K.13
Proof of Proposition
37

Set

V:=V_{p+1}=|{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}|^{-1/2}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}},\qquad\gamma:=\frac{|{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}|}{n},

and define

Q_{M}:=\frac{1}{n}\,a^{\top}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}^{\top}M\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}\,e,\qquad M\in\mathcal{N}_{p}.

Since

\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}=|{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}|^{1/2}V,\qquad\Pi_{p}=\frac{1}{n}\Phi_{\leq p}\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}=WW^{\top},\qquad\Pi_{p,G}=\frac{1}{n}\Phi_{\leq p}\Delta_{G}\Phi_{\leq p}^{\top}=W\Delta_{G}W^{\top},

every expanded word contributing to
Q_{M}
has the form

\gamma\,a^{\top}V^{\top}H_{0}\,W\,\Xi\,W^{\top}H_{1}\,V\,e,

(184)

where
H_{0},H_{1}
are either
I
or one of the words appearing in the expansion
of
\Delta_{H}
, and
\Xi
is either
I
or one of the words appearing in the
expansion of
\Delta_{G}
.

As in the low-degree endpoint proof, we decompose the low-degree space by exact degree:
for
0\leq q\leq p
,

W_{q}:=WP_{q}=n^{-1/2}\Phi_{\mathcal{S}_{q}},\qquad\Lambda=\sum_{q=0}^{p}\beta_{q}P_{q},\qquad\beta_{q}:=\frac{d^{q}}{n}.

Expanding every occurrence of
B_{0},B_{1},B_{2}
, and then resolving every occurrence of

W
and
W^{\top}
into the exact-degree blocks
W_{q},W_{q}^{\top}
, we obtain a finite
decomposition

Q_{M}=\sum_{\eta\in\Omega}\gamma\,\alpha_{\eta}\,a^{\top}M_{\eta}e,

(185)

where
|\Omega|=O_{p,c_{H},c_{G}}(1)
, and for each
\eta\in\Omega
:

1.

\alpha_{\eta}
is the product of all deterministic scalar coefficients coming
from the chosen words in
\Delta_{H}
and
\Delta_{G}
, together with the factors

\beta_{q}
contributed by the selected copies of
B_{0}
. In particular,

|\alpha_{\eta}|=O_{d}(1).

2.

M_{\eta}
is the corresponding normalized matrix chain obtained after removing
those scalar coefficients. Concretely,
M_{\eta}
is a product of matrices chosen from

V^{\top},\quad V,\quad W_{q},\quad W_{q}^{\top},\quad{\mathsf{off}}(V_{j}V_{j}^{\top}),\quad{\mathsf{off}}(W_{q}^{\top}W_{q}),\quad W_{q}^{\top}W_{q^{\prime}},

with endpoint family
{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}
on both sides.

We claim that every summand in (
185
) satisfies

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl(\gamma\,\alpha_{\eta}\,a^{\top}M_{\eta}e\bigr)^{2}\right]\leq o_{d}(1)\,(a^{\top}e)^{2}+O_{d}\!\left(n^{-1}\|a\|_{2}^{2}\|e\|_{2}^{2}\right).

(186)

There are two cases.

Case 1:
M_{\eta}
contains at least one off-diagonal replacement.

Let

\mathfrak{S}^{\eta}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{S}^{\eta}_{m_{\eta}+1}

be the sequence of families appearing in the chain
M_{\eta}
, and let

\mathcal{V}_{\eta},\mathcal{E}_{\eta}
be the corresponding sets of sample-space and
feature-space off-diagonal replacements.
Then
M_{\eta}
satisfies the hypotheses of Corollary
42
.

The crucial point is that
M_{\eta}
always contains at least one internal low-degree
family
\mathcal{S}_{q}
: indeed, every word in (
184
) contains the
middle factor
W\,\Xi\,W^{\top}
, and after exact-degree expansion this contributes
at least one block
W_{q}
or
W_{q}^{\top}
. Therefore

\kappa_{M_{\eta}}:=\min_{2\leq k\leq m_{\eta}}\frac{|\mathfrak{S}^{\eta}_{k}|}{n}\leq\frac{|\mathcal{S}_{q}|}{n}=O_{d}(d^{-2\delta}),

since
p
is fixed and
|\mathcal{S}_{q}|\asymp d^{q}=O_{d}(d^{-\delta})
for all
q\leq p
.

Applying Corollary
42
to
M_{\eta}
, we obtain

\mathbb{E}\!\left[(a^{\top}M_{\eta}e)^{2}\right]\leq O_{d}\!\bigl(\kappa_{M_{\eta}}|\mathcal{E}_{\eta}|\bigr)\,(nw^{(1)}w^{(m_{\eta}+1)})^{2}(a^{\top}e)^{2}+O_{d}\!\left((nw^{(1)}w^{(m_{\eta}+1)})^{2}n^{-1}\|a\|_{2}^{2}\|e\|_{2}^{2}\right).

(187)

Here the endpoint family is
{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}
on both sides, so

w^{(1)}=w^{(m_{\eta}+1)}=|{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}|^{-1/2},\qquad(nw^{(1)}w^{(m_{\eta}+1)})^{2}=\left(\frac{n}{|{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}|}\right)^{2}=\gamma^{-2}.

Also
|\mathcal{E}_{\eta}|=O_{p,c_{H},c_{G}}(1)
, since the total number of factors is
uniformly bounded. Hence (
187
) simplifies to

\mathbb{E}\!\left[(a^{\top}M_{\eta}e)^{2}\right]\leq O_{d}(d^{-2\delta})\,\gamma^{-2}(a^{\top}e)^{2}+O_{d}\!\left(\gamma^{-2}n^{-1}\|a\|_{2}^{2}\|e\|_{2}^{2}\right).

Multiplying by
\gamma^{2}|\alpha_{\eta}|^{2}
, and using
|\alpha_{\eta}|=O_{d}(1)
,
yields (
186
).

Case 2:
M_{\eta}
contains no off-diagonal replacement.

This can happen only when
M=\Pi_{p,G}
,
H_{0}=H_{1}=I
, and the chosen word in

\Delta_{G}
is a pure
B_{0}
-word. Indeed, every occurrence of
\Delta_{H}
contributes
an off-diagonal factor, and every occurrence of
B_{1}
does as well. Thus

\Xi=B_{0}^{r}\qquad\text{for some }1\leq r\leq c_{G},

and (
185
) reduces to

Q_{M}=\gamma\sum_{q=0}^{p}\alpha_{q}\,a^{\top}V^{\top}W_{q}W_{q}^{\top}Ve,\qquad\alpha_{q}=O_{d}(\beta_{q}^{r}).

(188)

For each fixed
q
, the chain
V^{\top}W_{q}W_{q}^{\top}V
has endpoint family

{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}
on both sides and only one low-degree family
\mathcal{D}_{q}
. Therefore
Lemma
39
gives

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl(a^{\top}V^{\top}W_{q}W_{q}^{\top}Ve\bigr)^{2}\right]\leq C_{d}\,\gamma^{-2}\Bigl((a^{\top}e)^{2}+n^{-1}\|a\|_{2}^{2}\|e\|_{2}^{2}\Bigr).

Using Cauchy–Schwarz in (
188
), we obtain

\mathbb{E}[Q_{M}^{2}]\leq C_{p}\sum_{q=0}^{p}|\alpha_{q}|^{2}\,\gamma^{2}\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl(a^{\top}V^{\top}W_{q}W_{q}^{\top}Ve\bigr)^{2}\right].

Hence we have

\mathbb{E}[Q_{M}^{2}]\leq C_{d}\left(\sum_{q=0}^{p}|\alpha_{q}|^{2}\right)\Bigl((a^{\top}e)^{2}+n^{-1}\|a\|_{2}^{2}\|e\|_{2}^{2}\Bigr).

Since
|\alpha_{q}|=O_{d}(\beta_{q}^{r})
,
r\geq 1
, and

\max_{0\leq q\leq p}\beta_{q}=\frac{d^{p}}{n}=O_{d}(d^{-\delta})

we have

\sum_{q=0}^{p}|\alpha_{q}|^{2}=O_{d}(d^{-2\delta}),

so (
186
) follows in this case as well.

Combining the two cases proves (
186
). Finally, since

|\Omega|=O_{p,c_{H},c_{G}}(1)
, Cauchy–Schwarz in (
185
) yields,
for every
M\in\mathcal{N}_{p}
,

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\left(\frac{1}{n}\,a^{\top}\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}^{\top}M\Phi_{{\mathcal{S}}_{p+1}}\,e\right)^{2}\right]\leq O_{d}(d^{-2\delta})\,(a^{\top}e)^{2}+O_{d}\!\left(n^{-1}\|a\|_{2}^{2}\|e\|_{2}^{2}\right).

This proves Proposition
37
.

K.14
Proof of Proposition
38

Expanding
T
using (
175
), (
176
), and the block decomposition of
B_{0}
(
177
),
we may write

\displaystyle b_{1}^{\top}Tb_{2}=\sum_{\eta\in\Omega(T)}\alpha_{\eta}\,\bigl((P_{q_{-}^{\eta}}b_{1})^{\top}M_{\eta}(P_{q_{+}^{\eta}}b_{2})\bigr),

(189)

where
\Omega(T)
is a finite set of cardinality
O_{p,c_{H},c_{G}}(1)
,
each coefficient
\alpha_{\eta}
is deterministic and has the form

\displaystyle\alpha_{\eta}=O_{d}\!\Bigl(\prod_{\ell=1}^{s}\beta_{u_{\eta,\ell}}\Bigr),\qquad u_{\eta,\ell}\in\{0,\dots,p\},

(190)

and each
M_{\eta}
is a normalized matrix chain whose families belong to
\mathcal{S}_{0},\ldots,{\mathcal{S}}_{2p+1}

with the same off-diagonal replacements as in the original word
T
.
In particular, Lemma
39
and
Corollary
42
apply to each
M_{\eta}
.

Fix one
\eta\in\Omega(T)
.

If
q_{-}^{\eta}\neq q_{+}^{\eta}
, then the endpoint families differ, and
Lemma
39
gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl((P_{q_{-}^{\eta}}b_{1})^{\top}M_{\eta}(P_{q_{+}^{\eta}}b_{2})\bigr)^{2}\right]=O_{d}\!\left(n^{-1}\|P_{q_{-}^{\eta}}b_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\|P_{q_{+}^{\eta}}b_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\right).

(191)

Now suppose
q_{-}^{\eta}=q_{+}^{\eta}=q
.

If every low-degree family occurring in
M_{\eta}
is equal to
\mathcal{S}_{q}
,
then
M_{\eta}
has the same low-degree family at both endpoints and, by assumption,
contains at least one off-diagonal replacement. Hence
Corollary
42
applies and yields

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl((P_{q}b_{1})^{\top}M_{\eta}(P_{q}b_{2})\bigr)^{2}\right]=O_{d}(d^{2(q-p-\delta)})\,\bigl((P_{q}b_{1})^{\top}(P_{q}b_{2})\bigr)^{2}+O_{d}\!\left(n^{-1}\|P_{q}b_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\|P_{q}b_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\right).

If
M_{\eta}
contains another low-degree family
\mathcal{S}_{q^{\prime}}
with
q^{\prime}\neq q
,
then
M_{\eta}
contains at least two distinct families of size
o(n)
, namely

\mathcal{S}_{q}
and
\mathcal{S}_{q^{\prime}}
. Therefore we are in the second case of
Lemma
39
, and

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl((P_{q}b_{1})^{\top}M_{\eta}(P_{q}b_{2})\bigr)^{2}\right]=O_{d}(d^{2(q^{\prime}\wedge q)-2p-2\delta})\,\bigl((P_{q}b_{1})^{\top}(P_{q}b_{2})\bigr)^{2}+O_{d}\!\left(n^{-1}\|P_{q}b_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\|P_{q}b_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\right).

Thus, whenever the endpoints agree, we have the uniform bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl((P_{q}b_{1})^{\top}M_{\eta}(P_{q}b_{2})\bigr)^{2}\right]\leq O_{d}(d^{2(q-p-\delta)})\,\bigl((P_{q}b_{1})^{\top}(P_{q}b_{2})\bigr)^{2}+O_{d}\!\left(n^{-1}\|P_{q}b_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\|P_{q}b_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\right).

(192)

It remains to account for the coefficient
\alpha_{\eta}
.
If the endpoints agree and are equal to
q
, let
t_{\eta}(q)\in\{0,\dots,s\}

denote the number of copies of
B_{0}
that act on the degree-
q
block in the
summand indexed by
\eta
. Then, by Eq. (
190
), we have

\displaystyle|\alpha_{\eta}|^{2}\lesssim_{d}\beta_{q}^{2t_{\eta}(q)}\,\beta_{p}^{\,2(s-t_{\eta}(q))}.

(193)

Since
\beta_{p}=d^{p}/n=o_{d}(1)
, the factor
\beta_{p}^{2(s-t_{\eta}(q))}
is

o_{d}(1)
whenever
t_{\eta}(q)<s
, while if
t_{\eta}(q)=s
it is simply
1
.
Combining (
192
) with (
193
), and using (
191
) when the endpoints differ,
we obtain

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl(\alpha_{\eta}(P_{q_{-}^{\eta}}b_{1})^{\top}M_{\eta}(P_{q_{+}^{\eta}}b_{2})\bigr)^{2}\right]\leq\sum_{t=0}^{s}O_{d}(d^{-2\delta})\sum_{q=0}^{p}\beta_{q}^{2t}\bigl((P_{q}b_{1})^{\top}(P_{q}b_{2})\bigr)^{2}+O_{d}\!\left(n^{-1}\|b_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\|b_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\right).

This is exactly

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl(\alpha_{\eta}(P_{q_{-}^{\eta}}b_{1})^{\top}M_{\eta}(P_{q_{+}^{\eta}}b_{2})\bigr)^{2}\right]\leq\sum_{t=0}^{s}O_{d}(d^{-2\delta})\,\mathcal{B}_{t}(b_{1},b_{2})+O_{d}\!\left(n^{-1}\|b_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\|b_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\right).

Since
|\Omega(T)|=O_{p,c_{H},c_{G}}(1)
, Cauchy–Schwarz in (
189
) proves (
179
).

K.15
Proof of Claim
5

Since
(nD)_{{\mathcal{S}}_{q}}^{-1}
is a diagonal matrix with each entry of the order
O_{d}(d^{q-p-\delta})=o_{d}(1)
for
q\leq p
, expanding
{\mathcal{B}}_{t}
gives

\displaystyle\sum_{t=0}^{c_{G}}{\mathcal{B}}_{t}(\mathsf{c}_{\leq p},\hat{e}_{J})

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=0}^{c_{G}}\sum_{q=0}^{p}\left<\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{q}},(nD_{{\mathcal{S}}_{q}})^{-2t}D_{{\mathcal{S}}_{q}}^{-1}E_{{\mathcal{S}}_{q},J}\right>^{2}

\displaystyle=(1+o_{d}(1))\sum_{q=0}^{p}\left<\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{q}},D_{{\mathcal{S}}_{q}}^{-1}E_{{\mathcal{S}}_{q},J}\right>^{2}.

(194)

Plugging the expression of
D_{{\mathcal{S}}_{q}}^{-1}E_{{\mathcal{S}}_{q},J}
namely (
108
) into the above equation gives

\displaystyle\sum_{q=0}^{p}\left<\mathsf{c}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{q}},D_{{\mathcal{S}}_{q}}^{-1}E_{{\mathcal{S}}_{q},J}\right>^{2}=\underbrace{\left(\sum_{i\in[d]\setminus J}\mathsf{c}_{J\sqcup\{i\}}u_{i}\right)^{2}}_{:={\mathcal{I}}^{2}}+\underbrace{\left(d^{-1}D^{\prime}_{J,J}D_{J\setminus\{i\}}^{-1}\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{c}_{J\setminus\{i\}}u_{i}\right)^{2}}_{:={\mathcal{J}}^{2}}.

By Cauchy-Schwarz, we obtain

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq d^{-2}\cdot(D^{\prime}_{J,J}D_{J\setminus\{i\}}^{-1})^{2}\cdot(\sum_{i\in J}u_{i}^{2})\left(\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{c}_{J\setminus\{i\}}^{2}\right)

(195)

\displaystyle=O_{d}(d^{-4})\cdot\left(\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{c}_{J\setminus\{i\}}^{2}\right).

(196)

thus by Young’s inequality we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}^{2}+{\mathcal{J}}^{2}\leq 2({\mathcal{I}}+{\mathcal{J}})^{2}+3{\mathcal{J}}^{2}=2\left(\mathsf{c}_{\leq p}\hat{e}_{J}\right)^{2}+O_{d}(d^{-4})\cdot\left(\sum_{i\in J}\mathsf{c}_{J\setminus\{i\}}^{2}\right).

Combining it with Eq. (
K.15
) completes the proof.

Appendix L
Product of random Fourier-Walsh matrices

In this section, we state and prove a key lemma for our main results. The proof is adapted from
[
57
]
.

Lemma 39
.

Fix families
{\mathcal{S}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{S}}_{m+1}\subseteq 2^{[d]}
, and let

M:=A_{1}^{\top}A_{2}A_{2}^{\top}\cdots A_{m}A_{m}^{\top}A_{m+1},\qquad A_{i}:=w^{(i)}X_{{\mathcal{S}}_{i}},

where

w^{(i)}=n^{-1/2}\wedge|{\mathcal{S}}_{i}|^{-1/2}.

Assume the following:

(i)

every
S\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
satisfies
|S|\leq p_{i}
;

(ii)

if
{\mathcal{S}}_{i}\cap{\mathcal{S}}_{j}\neq\varnothing
, then
{\mathcal{S}}_{i}={\mathcal{S}}_{j}
.

Let
b_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\mathcal{S}}_{1}|}
,
b_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\mathcal{S}}_{m+1}|}
, and set

\kappa:=(n\,w^{(1)}w^{(m+1)})^{2}.

If
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}\neq{\mathcal{S}}_{m+1}
, then the following estimate for second-moment holds:

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl(b_{1}^{\top}Mb_{2}\bigr)^{2}\right]=\kappa\cdot O_{d}\!\left(n^{-1}\|b_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\|b_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\right).

Assume now that
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}={\mathcal{S}}_{m+1}
. If every distinct internal family

{\mathcal{S}}_{k}\neq{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
,
2\leq k\leq m
, satisfies
n=O(|{\mathcal{S}}_{k}|)
, then the
second-moment expansion

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl(b_{1}^{\top}Mb_{2}\bigr)^{2}\right]=\kappa\left[(b_{1}^{\top}b_{2})^{2}+O_{d}\!\left(n^{-1}\|b_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\|b_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\right)\right]

(197)

holds, and the estimate for first-moment expansion also holds:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[b_{1}^{\top}Mb_{2}\right]=\sqrt{\kappa}\left[b_{1}^{\top}b_{2}+O_{d}\!\left(n^{-\tfrac{1}{2}}\|b_{1}\|_{2}\|b_{2}\|_{2}\right)\right].

(198)

In the complementary case, which necessarily has
m\geq 2
, the following estimates hold:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl(b_{1}^{\top}Mb_{2}\bigr)^{2}\right]=\kappa\left[O_{d}\!\left(\min_{2\leq k\leq m}\frac{|{\mathcal{S}}_{k}|^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)(b_{1}^{\top}b_{2})^{2}+O_{d}\!\left(n^{-1}\|b_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\|b_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\right)\right]

(199)

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[b_{1}^{\top}Mb_{2}\right]=\sqrt{\kappa}\left[O_{d}\!\left(\min_{2\leq k\leq m}\frac{|{\mathcal{S}}_{k}|}{n}\right)(b_{1}^{\top}b_{2})+O_{d}\!\left(n^{-\tfrac{1}{2}}\|b_{1}\|_{2}\|b_{2}\|_{2}\right)\right].

(200)

Proof of Lemma
39
.

We first prove the second-moment estimates. The first-moment estimates follow
from the same collapse procedure with one copy of the chain instead of two; the
necessary modifications are given at the end of the proof.

For readability, introduce the random variable

\mathbf{S}:=\left(b_{1}^{\top}A_{1}^{\top}(A_{2}A_{2}^{\top})\cdots(A_{m}A_{m}^{\top})A_{m+1}b_{2}\right)^{2}.

Expanding the square gives the identity

\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}]=\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{2m}\in[n]}\mathbb{E}\Biggl[\begin{aligned} &(A_{1}b_{1})_{i_{1}}(A_{2}A_{2}^{\top})_{i_{1},i_{2}}\cdots(A_{m}A_{m}^{\top})_{i_{m-1},i_{m}}(A_{m+1}b_{2})_{i_{m}}\\
&\quad{}\times(A_{1}b_{1})_{i_{m+1}}(A_{2}A_{2}^{\top})_{i_{m+1},i_{m+2}}\cdots(A_{m}A_{m}^{\top})_{i_{2m-1},i_{2m}}(A_{m+1}b_{2})_{i_{2m}}\end{aligned}\Biggr].

For each partition
\pi\in\Pi_{2m}
, define the exact equality class

\mathcal{I}_{\pi}:=\{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{2m})\in[n]^{2m}:i_{s}=i_{t}\text{ if and only if }s\sim_{\pi}t\}.

The resulting partition decomposition is

\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}]=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{2m}}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}_{\pi}],

where the contribution associated with
\pi
is defined by

\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}_{\pi}]:=\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{2m})\in\mathcal{I}_{\pi}}\mathbb{E}\Biggl[\begin{aligned} &(A_{1}b_{1})_{i_{1}}(A_{2}A_{2}^{\top})_{i_{1},i_{2}}\cdots(A_{m}A_{m}^{\top})_{i_{m-1},i_{m}}(A_{m+1}b_{2})_{i_{m}}\\
&\quad{}\times(A_{1}b_{1})_{i_{m+1}}(A_{2}A_{2}^{\top})_{i_{m+1},i_{m+2}}\cdots(A_{m}A_{m}^{\top})_{i_{2m-1},i_{2m}}(A_{m+1}b_{2})_{i_{2m}}\end{aligned}\Biggr].

We now fix
\pi\in\Pi_{2m}
and estimate
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}_{\pi}]
.

At each stage of the reduction, the surviving sample positions are relabeled
from left to right as
i_{1},\ldots,i_{q}
. The current product consists of
factors of the form

(A_{1}b_{1})_{i_{r}},\qquad(A_{u}A_{u}^{\top})_{i_{r},i_{r+1}},\qquad(A_{m+1}b_{2})_{i_{r}},

with family labels inherited from the unreduced product. We repeatedly use the
following two reductions.

First, suppose that the current partition forces two adjacent sample positions
to be equal, say
i_{r}=i_{r+1}
, and that the factor between them is

(A_{u}A_{u}^{\top})_{i_{r},i_{r+1}}
. The diagonal identity gives the scalar value

(A_{u}A_{u}^{\top})_{i_{r},i_{r}}=(w^{(u)})^{2}|{\mathcal{S}}_{u}|=1\wedge\frac{|{\mathcal{S}}_{u}|}{n}.

Thus the Gram factor is replaced by a scalar, which we denote by

C_{F}(u):=(w^{(u)})^{2}|{\mathcal{S}}_{u}|=1\wedge\frac{|{\mathcal{S}}_{u}|}{n}.

One of the two equal sample variables is then deleted. We call this operation a

feature collapse
.

Second, suppose that
\{r\}
is a singleton block of the current partition.
For fixed values of the other current block labels, the label
i_{r}
has

n+O_{m}(1)
admissible choices. Define the endpoint pairing by

\langle b_{1},b_{2}\rangle_{\cap}:=\sum_{S\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}\cap{\mathcal{S}}_{m+1}}(b_{1})_{S}(b_{2})_{S}.

Also set

C_{S}(u,v):=(n+O_{m}(1))w^{(u)}w^{(v)}\mathbbm{1}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{u}={\mathcal{S}}_{v}}.

Independence across samples and orthogonality of distinct feature families give
the following local replacements:

\displaystyle(A_{u}A_{u}^{\top})_{i_{r-1},i_{r}}(A_{v}A_{v}^{\top})_{i_{r},i_{r+1}}

\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow C_{S}(u,v)(A_{u}A_{u}^{\top})_{i_{r-1},i_{r+1}},

\displaystyle(A_{1}b_{1})_{i_{r}}(A_{v}A_{v}^{\top})_{i_{r},i_{r+1}}

\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow C_{S}(1,v)(A_{1}b_{1})_{i_{r+1}},

\displaystyle(A_{u}A_{u}^{\top})_{i_{r-1},i_{r}}(A_{m+1}b_{2})_{i_{r}}

\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow C_{S}(u,m+1)(A_{m+1}b_{2})_{i_{r-1}},

\displaystyle(A_{1}b_{1})_{i_{r}}(A_{m+1}b_{2})_{i_{r}}

\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow C_{S}(1,m+1)\langle b_{1},b_{2}\rangle_{\cap}.

Here
\rightsquigarrow
means that the contribution is unchanged after
summing over the singleton sample label and deleting that variable. In the
first line, the family label on the remaining Gram factor is immaterial because
the factor
\mathbbm{1}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{u}={\mathcal{S}}_{v}}
forces
{\mathcal{S}}_{u}={\mathcal{S}}_{v}
. We call these
operations
sample collapses
.

We iterate the two reductions until neither applies. Let
\pi^{\sharp}
be the
final partition, and let
c_{\pi}
be the product of all scalar coefficients
generated during the reduction. With the convention that any endpoint pairing

\langle b_{1},b_{2}\rangle_{\cap}
produced before the procedure stops is kept as
an external scalar factor, the collapse procedure gives the relation

\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}_{\pi}]=\begin{cases}c_{\pi}\,\langle b_{1},b_{2}\rangle_{\cap}^{2},&|\pi^{\sharp}|=0,\\[5.69054pt]
c_{\pi}\,\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}_{\pi^{\sharp}}],&|\pi^{\sharp}|\geq 1.\end{cases}

(201)

All feature-collapse and sample-collapse coefficients have absolute value at
most one, so the coefficient
c_{\pi}
satisfies

0\leq c_{\pi}\leq 1.

We now record the coefficient estimate for fully collapsed partitions. The proof appears in Appendix
L.1
.

Proposition 40

(Fully collapsed coefficient)
.

Assume
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}={\mathcal{S}}_{m+1}
. Fix
\pi\in\Pi_{2m}
such that

|\pi^{\sharp}|=0
and
c_{\pi}>0
. For a distinct family
{\mathcal{T}}
, write

w_{\mathcal{T}}:=n^{-1/2}\wedge|{\mathcal{T}}|^{-1/2}.

Let
N_{F}({\mathcal{T}})
be the number of feature collapses performed on
{\mathcal{T}}
, and let

N_{S}({\mathcal{T}})
be the number of nonterminal sample collapses performed on
{\mathcal{T}}
,
excluding the two terminal endpoint collapses that produce the two factors

\langle b_{1},b_{2}\rangle_{\cap}
. Then the coefficient admits the factorization

\kappa^{-1}c_{\pi}=\bigl(1+O_{m}(n^{-1})\bigr)\prod_{{\mathcal{T}}}\left(1\wedge\frac{|{\mathcal{T}}|}{n}\right)^{N_{F}({\mathcal{T}})}\left(1\wedge\frac{n}{|{\mathcal{T}}|}\right)^{N_{S}({\mathcal{T}})}.

In particular, if no collapse produces a loss, then the coefficient satisfies

c_{\pi}=\kappa\bigl(1+O_{m}(n^{-1})\bigr).

Moreover, if at least one collapse on a family
{\mathcal{T}}
produces a loss, then

c_{\pi}=\kappa\cdot O\!\left(\min\left\{\frac{|{\mathcal{T}}|}{n},\frac{n}{|{\mathcal{T}}|}\right\}\right).

Finally, if
{\mathcal{T}}\neq{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
is a distinct internal family, say

{\mathcal{T}}={\mathcal{S}}_{k}
for some
2\leq k\leq m
, and
|{\mathcal{T}}|<n
, then every fully
collapsed second-moment contribution satisfies

c_{\pi}=\kappa\cdot O\!\left(\frac{|{\mathcal{T}}|^{2}}{n^{2}}\right).

We next bound the residual terms, namely the terms for which the collapse
procedure stops before the entire product disappears. The proof appears in Appendix
L.2
.

Proposition 41
.

Fix
\pi\in\Pi_{2m}
, and suppose that the recursive collapse procedure
stops at a final partition
\pi^{\sharp}
with
|\pi^{\sharp}|\geq 1
. Then the following estimate for residual holds:

\bigl|\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}_{\pi^{\sharp}}]\bigr|=O_{d}(n^{-1})\|b_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\|b_{2}\|_{2}^{2}

We now assemble the second-moment estimates. The bound

\kappa^{-1}=O_{d}(1)
follows from

|{\mathcal{S}}_{1}|,|{\mathcal{S}}_{m+1}|\leq 2^{d}
. Proposition
41
, together
with
0\leq c_{\pi}\leq 1
, implies that every non-fully-collapsed partition
contributes a term satisfying

c_{\pi}\,\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}_{\pi^{\sharp}}]=\kappa\cdot O_{d}\!\left(n^{-1}\|b_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\|b_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\right).

(202)

If
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}\neq{\mathcal{S}}_{m+1}
, then the endpoint pairing vanishes:

\langle b_{1},b_{2}\rangle_{\cap}=0.

Thus every fully collapsed contribution vanishes. Since the number of
partitions of
[2m]
depends only on
m
, the non-fully-collapsed bound in
(
202
) gives

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl(b_{1}^{\top}Mb_{2}\bigr)^{2}\right]=\kappa\cdot O_{d}\!\left(n^{-1}\|b_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\|b_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\right).

Assume now that
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}={\mathcal{S}}_{m+1}
holds. The endpoint pairing is then

\langle b_{1},b_{2}\rangle_{\cap}=b_{1}^{\top}b_{2}.

Suppose first that every distinct internal family
{\mathcal{S}}_{k}\neq{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
,

2\leq k\leq m
, satisfies
n=O(|{\mathcal{S}}_{k}|)
. The fully collapsed contribution in
which all collapses are loss-free has coefficient

\kappa\bigl(1+O_{m}(n^{-1})\bigr).

Every other fully collapsed contribution contains at least one lossy collapse.
If the loss occurs on a family
{\mathcal{T}}
with
|{\mathcal{T}}|<n
, then the loss

|{\mathcal{T}}|/n
is
O_{d}(n^{-1})
. If the loss occurs on a distinct internal family

{\mathcal{T}}\neq{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
with
|{\mathcal{T}}|>n
, then the loss is
n/|{\mathcal{T}}|
; the assumption

n=O(|{\mathcal{T}}|)
, together with
|{\mathcal{T}}|\leq 2^{d}
