, implies that this loss is also

O_{d}(n^{-1})
. Hence all fully collapsed contributions except the loss-free
one are absorbed into the
O_{d}(n^{-1})
error. The fully collapsed terms
therefore satisfy

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\Pi_{2m}\\
|\pi^{\sharp}|=0\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}_{\pi}]=\kappa\left[(b_{1}^{\top}b_{2})^{2}+O_{d}\!\left(n^{-1}\|b_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\|b_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\right)\right].

Combining this estimate with
(
202
) gives

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl(b_{1}^{\top}Mb_{2}\bigr)^{2}\right]=\kappa\left[(b_{1}^{\top}b_{2})^{2}+O_{d}\!\left(n^{-1}\|b_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\|b_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\right)\right],

which proves (
197
).

It remains to consider the complementary regime. In this case, there is a
distinct internal family
{\mathcal{T}}={\mathcal{S}}_{k}\neq{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
,
2\leq k\leq m
, with

|{\mathcal{T}}|<n
along the relevant range. Define

\rho_{2}:=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}2\leq k\leq m\\
{\mathcal{S}}_{k}\neq{\mathcal{S}}_{1},\ |{\mathcal{S}}_{k}|<n\end{subarray}}\frac{|{\mathcal{S}}_{k}|^{2}}{n^{2}}.

By Proposition
40
, every fully collapsed second-moment
contribution must incur two feature-collapse losses from such an internal
family. This gives the bound

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\Pi_{2m}\\
|\pi^{\sharp}|=0\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}_{\pi}]=\kappa\cdot O_{d}(\rho_{2})(b_{1}^{\top}b_{2})^{2}.

Since all families are nonempty and have size at most
2^{d}
, replacing

\rho_{2}
by
\min_{2\leq k\leq m}|{\mathcal{S}}_{k}|^{2}/n^{2}
changes only the

O_{d}(\cdot)
constant. After adding the non-fully-collapsed contribution from
(
202
), we obtain

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl(b_{1}^{\top}Mb_{2}\bigr)^{2}\right]=\kappa\left[O_{d}\!\left(\min_{2\leq k\leq m}\frac{|{\mathcal{S}}_{k}|^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)(b_{1}^{\top}b_{2})^{2}+O_{d}\!\left(n^{-1}\|b_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\|b_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\right)\right],

which proves (
199
).

We finally prove the first-moment estimates. Set

\mathbf{T}:=b_{1}^{\top}Mb_{2}.

The partition decomposition is now taken over
[m]
, because there is only
one copy of the chain. The same feature and sample collapses apply. A fully
collapsed first-moment term has one terminal endpoint collapse instead of two,
so the terminal coefficient has the form

(n+O_{m}(1))w^{(1)}w^{(m+1)}=\sqrt{\kappa}\bigl(1+O_{m}(n^{-1})\bigr).

The analogue of Proposition
41
, proved by the same
incidence-graph argument with one chain instead of two, gives the stronger
residual estimate

\bigl|\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{T}_{\pi^{\sharp}}]\bigr|=O_{d}(n^{-1})\|b_{1}\|_{2}\|b_{2}\|_{2}

for every non-fully-collapsed first-moment residual. Since

\kappa^{-1/2}=O_{d}(1)
, the weaker estimate needed below also holds:

\bigl|\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{T}_{\pi^{\sharp}}]\bigr|=\sqrt{\kappa}\,O_{d}(n^{-1/2})\|b_{1}\|_{2}\|b_{2}\|_{2}.

(203)

Assume first that every distinct internal family

{\mathcal{S}}_{k}\neq{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
,
2\leq k\leq m
, satisfies
n=O(|{\mathcal{S}}_{k}|)
. The fully
collapsed loss-free contribution has value

\sqrt{\kappa}\,\bigl(1+O_{m}(n^{-1})\bigr)b_{1}^{\top}b_{2}.

As in the second-moment argument, all other fully collapsed contributions are
absorbed into the
O_{d}(n^{-1})
error. The Cauchy–Schwarz inequality gives
the auxiliary bound

n^{-1}|b_{1}^{\top}b_{2}|\leq n^{-1/2}\|b_{1}\|_{2}\|b_{2}\|_{2}.

Combining the fully collapsed estimate with
(
203
) gives

\mathbb{E}\!\left[b_{1}^{\top}Mb_{2}\right]=\sqrt{\kappa}\left[b_{1}^{\top}b_{2}+O_{d}\!\left(n^{-1/2}\|b_{1}\|_{2}\|b_{2}\|_{2}\right)\right],

which proves (
198
).

In the complementary regime, define

\rho_{1}:=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}2\leq k\leq m\\
{\mathcal{S}}_{k}\neq{\mathcal{S}}_{1},\ |{\mathcal{S}}_{k}|<n\end{subarray}}\frac{|{\mathcal{S}}_{k}|}{n}.

A fully collapsed first-moment contribution must incur one feature-collapse
loss from a distinct internal family with size smaller than
n
. This gives
the fully collapsed bound

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\Pi_{m}\\
|\pi^{\sharp}|=0\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{T}_{\pi}]=\sqrt{\kappa}\,O_{d}(\rho_{1})(b_{1}^{\top}b_{2}).

Again, because all family sizes lie between
1
and
2^{d}
, replacing

\rho_{1}
by
\min_{2\leq k\leq m}|{\mathcal{S}}_{k}|/n
changes only the

O_{d}(\cdot)
constant. Adding the non-fully-collapsed first-moment
contribution from (
203
) yields

\mathbb{E}\!\left[b_{1}^{\top}Mb_{2}\right]=\sqrt{\kappa}\left[O_{d}\!\left(\min_{2\leq k\leq m}\frac{|{\mathcal{S}}_{k}|}{n}\right)(b_{1}^{\top}b_{2})+O_{d}\!\left(n^{-1/2}\|b_{1}\|_{2}\|b_{2}\|_{2}\right)\right],

which proves (
200
).
∎

Corollary 42
.

Fix families
{\mathcal{S}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{S}}_{m+1}\subseteq 2^{[d]}
, and define

M:=A_{1}^{\top}A_{2}A_{2}^{\top}\cdots A_{m}A_{m}^{\top}A_{m+1},\qquad A_{i}:=w^{(i)}X_{{\mathcal{S}}_{i}},

where the weights are given by

w^{(i)}:=n^{-1/2}\wedge|{\mathcal{S}}_{i}|^{-1/2}.

Assume that the families
{\mathcal{S}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{S}}_{m+1}
satisfy the hypotheses of
Lemma
39
. Assume moreover that the endpoint
families agree, namely,

{\mathcal{S}}_{1}={\mathcal{S}}_{m+1}.

Let
b_{1},b_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\mathcal{S}}_{1}|}
and let
\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}\subseteq\{2,\ldots,m\}
be such that

\mathcal{V}\cup\mathcal{E}\neq\emptyset,\qquad\mathcal{V}\cap\bigl(\mathcal{E}\cup(\mathcal{E}+1)\bigr)=\emptyset,\qquad\mathcal{E}+1:=\{j+1:j\in\mathcal{E}\}.

Assume also that the adjacent matrices agree along
\mathcal{E}
, that is,

A_{j}=A_{j+1}\qquad\text{for every }j\in\mathcal{E}.

Finally, assume the size conditions

n\leq|{\mathcal{S}}_{j}|\quad\text{for every }j\in\mathcal{V},\qquad|{\mathcal{S}}_{j}|\leq n\quad\text{for every }j\in\mathcal{E}.

Define
M_{\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}}
to be the matrix obtained from
M
by
the following replacements. For each
j\in\mathcal{V}
, replace

A_{j}A_{j}^{\top}\quad\text{by}\quad{\mathsf{off}}(A_{j}A_{j}^{\top}).

Also, for each
j\in\mathcal{E}
, replace

A_{j}^{\top}A_{j+1}\quad\text{by}\quad{\mathsf{off}}(A_{j}^{\top}A_{j+1}).

Set

\kappa:=(n\,w^{(1)}w^{(m+1)})^{2}.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
denote the set of distinct internal families

{\mathcal{T}}\neq{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
for which the first-regime condition
n=O(|{\mathcal{T}}|)
fails.
Equivalently,
\mathfrak{B}
is the set of distinct internal families that
force the “otherwise” case of Lemma
39
. Define

\rho:=\begin{cases}0,&\mathfrak{B}=\emptyset,\\[2.84526pt]
\displaystyle\min_{{\mathcal{T}}\in\mathfrak{B}}\frac{|{\mathcal{T}}|}{n},&\mathfrak{B}\neq\emptyset.\end{cases}

Then

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl(b_{1}^{\top}M_{\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}}b_{2}\bigr)^{2}\right]=\kappa\cdot O_{d}(\rho^{2})(b_{1}^{\top}b_{2})^{2}+\kappa\cdot O_{d}\!\left(n^{-1}\|b_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\|b_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\right).

Proof of Corollary
42
.

We first record the diagonal identities used in the expansion. If

j\in\mathcal{V}
, then
n\leq|{\mathcal{S}}_{j}|
, and therefore

w^{(j)}=|{\mathcal{S}}_{j}|^{-1/2}
. Hence

{\mathsf{Diag}}(A_{j}A_{j}^{\top})=(w^{(j)})^{2}|{\mathcal{S}}_{j}|\,I_{n}=I_{n}.

Similarly, if
j\in\mathcal{E}
, then
A_{j}=A_{j+1}
and

|{\mathcal{S}}_{j}|\leq n
. Thus
w^{(j)}=w^{(j+1)}=n^{-1/2}
, and hence

{\mathsf{Diag}}(A_{j}^{\top}A_{j+1})=n\,w^{(j)}w^{(j+1)}\,I_{|{\mathcal{S}}_{j}|}=I_{|{\mathcal{S}}_{j}|}.

Consequently, for
j\in\mathcal{V}
, we have

{\mathsf{off}}(A_{j}A_{j}^{\top})=A_{j}A_{j}^{\top}-I_{n}.

Likewise, for
j\in\mathcal{E}
, we have

{\mathsf{off}}(A_{j}^{\top}A_{j+1})=A_{j}^{\top}A_{j+1}-I_{|{\mathcal{S}}_{j}|}.

We now expand the off-diagonal constraints by inclusion–exclusion. For

J\subseteq\mathcal{V}
and
K\subseteq\mathcal{E}
, define the remaining
index set by

\Gamma_{J,K}:=[m+1]\setminus\bigl(J\cup(K+1)\bigr)=\{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{r}\}.

On this reduced index set, define the corresponding chain by

M_{J,K}:=(A_{i_{1}}^{\top}A_{i_{2}})(A_{i_{2}}^{\top}A_{i_{3}})\cdots(A_{i_{r-1}}^{\top}A_{i_{r}}).

The condition

\mathcal{V}\cap\bigl(\mathcal{E}\cup(\mathcal{E}+1)\bigr)=\emptyset

ensures that the deletions coming from
J
and
K
do not interfere with
one another. Therefore, inclusion–exclusion gives

M_{\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}}=\sum_{J\subseteq\mathcal{V}}\sum_{K\subseteq\mathcal{E}}(-1)^{|J|+|K|}M_{J,K}.

(204)

We next verify that each reduced chain
M_{J,K}
is still covered by
Lemma
39
. Deleting an index from
\mathcal{V}

removes only a family
{\mathcal{S}}_{j}
with
n\leq|{\mathcal{S}}_{j}|
, and therefore it cannot
remove any family in
\mathfrak{B}
. Deleting
j+1
with
j\in\mathcal{E}

only shortens a block of equal adjacent matrices, since
A_{j}=A_{j+1}
. Thus
no distinct bad internal family is removed entirely.

The endpoint families are also unchanged. The left endpoint is never deleted.
If the right endpoint is deleted, then
K
contains a terminal interval

\{\ell,\ell+1,\ldots,m\}
for some
\ell
. In that case, the new right
endpoint is
\ell
, and the equalities along this terminal block imply

A_{\ell}=A_{\ell+1}=\cdots=A_{m+1}.

Thus the right endpoint family and the right endpoint weight remain those of

{\mathcal{S}}_{m+1}
. Hence every reduced chain has the same endpoint families and the
same value of
\kappa
.

For notational convenience, write the index set of reduced chains as

\mathcal{A}:=\{(J,K):J\subseteq\mathcal{V},\ K\subseteq\mathcal{E}\}.

For
a=(J,K)\in\mathcal{A}
, define

\sigma_{a}:=(-1)^{|J|+|K|},\qquad Z_{a}:=b_{1}^{\top}M_{J,K}b_{2}.

Finally, denote

\beta:=b_{1}^{\top}b_{2},\qquad N:=\|b_{1}\|_{2}\|b_{2}\|_{2}.

With this notation, equation (
204
)
becomes

b_{1}^{\top}M_{\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}}b_{2}=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{a}Z_{a}.

(205)

We now distinguish the two regimes of Lemma
39
.

First regime:
\mathfrak{B}=\emptyset
.

In this case, every reduced chain lies in the first endpoint-matched regime of
Lemma
39
. We use the following mixed second-moment
estimate: for all
a,a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
,

\mathbb{E}[Z_{a}Z_{a^{\prime}}]=\kappa\left[\beta^{2}+O_{d}\!\left(n^{-1}N^{2}\right)\right].

(206)

This is the polarized form of the second-moment estimate in
Lemma
39
. Equivalently, it follows by applying the
same collapse argument to the product
Z_{a}Z_{a^{\prime}}
. The fully collapsed
loss-free contribution gives the common leading term
\kappa\beta^{2}
, while
all lossy or non-fully-collapsed contributions are absorbed into the

O_{d}(n^{-1}N^{2})
error.

Since
\mathcal{V}\cup\mathcal{E}\neq\emptyset
, the alternating coefficients
satisfy

\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{a}=\left(\sum_{J\subseteq\mathcal{V}}(-1)^{|J|}\right)\left(\sum_{K\subseteq\mathcal{E}}(-1)^{|K|}\right)=0.

(207)

Using (
205
),
(
206
), and
(
207
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl(b_{1}^{\top}M_{\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}}b_{2}\bigr)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{a,a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{a}\sigma_{a^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}[Z_{a}Z_{a^{\prime}}]

\displaystyle=\kappa\beta^{2}\left(\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{a}\right)^{2}+\kappa\cdot O_{d}\!\left(n^{-1}N^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\kappa\cdot O_{d}\!\left(n^{-1}N^{2}\right).

Since
\rho=0
in the present regime, this gives the desired bound.

Second regime:
\mathfrak{B}\neq\emptyset
.

In this case, every reduced chain remains in the second regime of
Lemma
39
. Indeed, as observed above, the deletions
do not remove any family in
\mathfrak{B}
. Therefore,
Lemma
39
gives, for every
a\in\mathcal{A}
,

\mathbb{E}[Z_{a}^{2}]=\kappa\left[O_{d}(\rho^{2})\beta^{2}+O_{d}\!\left(n^{-1}N^{2}\right)\right].

(208)

Since
|\mathcal{A}|\leq 2^{m}
, Cauchy–Schwarz applied to the finite sum in
(
205
) gives

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl(b_{1}^{\top}M_{\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}}b_{2}\bigr)^{2}\right]\leq|\mathcal{A}|\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mathbb{E}[Z_{a}^{2}].

Combining this estimate with (
208
), we
obtain

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl(b_{1}^{\top}M_{\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}}b_{2}\bigr)^{2}\right]=\kappa\cdot O_{d}(\rho^{2})\beta^{2}+\kappa\cdot O_{d}\!\left(n^{-1}N^{2}\right).

Finally, substituting back

\beta=b_{1}^{\top}b_{2}
and

N=\|b_{1}\|_{2}\|b_{2}\|_{2},

yields

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl(b_{1}^{\top}M_{\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}}b_{2}\bigr)^{2}\right]=\kappa\cdot O_{d}(\rho^{2})(b_{1}^{\top}b_{2})^{2}+\kappa\cdot O_{d}\!\left(n^{-1}\|b_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\|b_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\right).

This completes the proof.
∎

L.1
Proof of Proposition
40

A feature collapse on
{\mathcal{T}}
has contribution

w_{\mathcal{T}}^{2}|{\mathcal{T}}|=1\wedge\frac{|{\mathcal{T}}|}{n}.

Similarly, the contribution of a nonterminal sample collapse on
{\mathcal{T}}
is

(n+O_{m}(1))w_{\mathcal{T}}^{2}=\bigl(1+O_{m}(n^{-1})\bigr)\left(1\wedge\frac{n}{|{\mathcal{T}}|}\right).

The two terminal endpoint collapses have combined contribution

\bigl((n+O_{m}(1))w^{(1)}w^{(m+1)}\bigr)^{2}=\kappa\bigl(1+O_{m}(n^{-1})\bigr).

Multiplying all collapse contributions gives the factorization

\kappa^{-1}c_{\pi}=\bigl(1+O_{m}(n^{-1})\bigr)\prod_{{\mathcal{T}}}\left(1\wedge\frac{|{\mathcal{T}}|}{n}\right)^{N_{F}({\mathcal{T}})}\left(1\wedge\frac{n}{|{\mathcal{T}}|}\right)^{N_{S}({\mathcal{T}})}.

(209)

Separating the feature families according to whether
|{\mathcal{T}}|<n
or

|{\mathcal{T}}|>n
, and noting that families with
|{\mathcal{T}}|=n
contribute a factor equal
to one, gives the equivalent representation

\kappa^{-1}c_{\pi}=\bigl(1+O_{m}(n^{-1})\bigr)\prod_{|{\mathcal{T}}|<n}\left(\frac{|{\mathcal{T}}|}{n}\right)^{N_{F}({\mathcal{T}})}\prod_{|{\mathcal{T}}|>n}\left(\frac{n}{|{\mathcal{T}}|}\right)^{N_{S}({\mathcal{T}})}.

(210)

The first two consequences follow immediately from
(
210
).

It remains to prove the last claim. Let
{\mathcal{T}}\neq{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
be an internal feature
family with
|{\mathcal{T}}|<n
. In each copy of the chain, sample collapses can merge
adjacent
{\mathcal{T}}
-factors, but they cannot remove the final surviving

{\mathcal{T}}
-Gram factor, because the endpoints have family
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}\neq{\mathcal{T}}
.
Consequently, each copy must contain at least one feature collapse on
{\mathcal{T}}
.
Since there are two copies of the chain, the bound

N_{F}({\mathcal{T}})\geq 2

holds. Applying this bound to (
210
) gives

c_{\pi}=\kappa\cdot O\!\left(\frac{|{\mathcal{T}}|^{2}}{n^{2}}\right).

This proves the proposition.

L.2
Proof of Proposition
41

Let
q
be the number of surviving sample positions after the collapse
procedure stops. Set

r:=|\pi^{\sharp}|,

so that
r
is the number of blocks in the final partition. Since no further
sample collapse is possible, the partition
\pi^{\sharp}
has no singleton
block. This observation gives the counting inequality

q\geq 2r.

(211)

Moreover, since
|\pi^{\sharp}|\geq 1
, the integer
r
satisfies
r\geq 1
.

During the previous reductions, one entire copy of the chain may already have
collapsed and produced a scalar endpoint factor

\langle b_{1},b_{2}\rangle_{\cap}
. Let
\Theta
denote the product of any such
external endpoint factors, with the convention that
\Theta=1
if no such
factor is present. We keep
\Theta
outside the graph notation below.

Let
G^{\sharp}
be the incidence multigraph whose vertices are the blocks of

\pi^{\sharp}
. Each surviving factor
(A_{u}A_{u}^{\top})_{i_{s},i_{t}}
gives an
internal edge
e
joining the two blocks containing
s
and
t
. Each
surviving endpoint factor
(A_{1}b_{1})_{i_{s}}
or

(A_{m+1}b_{2})_{i_{s}}
gives a half-edge
h
attached to the block containing

s
. Since no further feature collapse is possible, no internal edge is a
loop.

Write
E=E(G^{\sharp})
and
H=H(G^{\sharp})
. For each internal edge

e\in E
, let
{\mathcal{F}}_{e}={\mathcal{S}}_{u_{e}}
be the corresponding feature family. The
edge factor associated with
e
admits the representation

(A_{u_{e}}A_{u_{e}}^{\top})_{ij}=\omega_{e}\sum_{S_{e}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{e}}x_{i}^{S_{e}}x_{j}^{S_{e}},\qquad\omega_{e}:=(w^{(u_{e})})^{2}\leq n^{-1}.

Similarly, for each half-edge
h\in H
, let
{\mathcal{F}}_{h}
be either
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
or

{\mathcal{S}}_{m+1}
, and let
c_{h}
be the corresponding coefficient vector, either

b_{1}
or
b_{2}
. The endpoint factor attached to
h
has the form

g_{h}(i)=\eta_{h}\sum_{S_{h}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{h}}c_{h}(S_{h})x_{i}^{S_{h}},\qquad\eta_{h}\leq n^{-1/2}.

For each admissible assignment of distinct sample labels to the
r
blocks,
independence across the distinct block labels factors the expectation over the
vertices of
G^{\sharp}
. Since the number of such assignments is at most

n^{r}
, taking absolute values gives the reduction

\bigl|\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}_{\pi^{\sharp}}]\bigr|\leq|\Theta|\,n^{r}\Bigl(\prod_{e\in E}\omega_{e}\Bigr)\Bigl(\prod_{h\in H}\eta_{h}\Bigr)\Gamma,

(212)

where the remaining feature sum
\Gamma
is defined by

\Gamma:=\sum_{(S_{f})_{f\in E\cup H}}\Bigl(\prod_{h\in H}|c_{h}(S_{h})|\Bigr)\prod_{T\in\pi^{\sharp}}\mu_{T}\bigl((S_{f})_{f\in\partial T}\bigr).

Here, for each block
T\in\pi^{\sharp}
, the local Walsh moment is defined as

\mu_{T}\bigl((S_{f})_{f\in\partial T}\bigr):=\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\prod_{f\in\partial T}x^{S_{f}}\Bigr].

Since each
\mu_{T}
is a Fourier–Walsh moment, it satisfies the pointwise
bound

0\leq\mu_{T}\bigl((S_{f})_{f\in\partial T}\bigr)\leq 1.

We next bound
\Gamma
. Since all feature families are subcollections of

2^{[d]}
, and since the number of surviving edges and half-edges depends
only on
m
, the internal feature sums contribute only an
O_{d}(1)
factor.
This gives the estimate

\Gamma\leq O_{d}(1)\prod_{h\in H}\sum_{S_{h}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{h}}|c_{h}(S_{h})|.

For each endpoint feature family, Cauchy–Schwarz gives the bound

\sum_{S_{h}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{h}}|c_{h}(S_{h})|\leq|{\mathcal{F}}_{h}|^{1/2}\|c_{h}\|_{2}\leq O_{d}(\|c_{h}\|_{2}).

Combining the preceding two estimates yields

\Gamma\leq O_{d}\left(\prod_{h\in H}\|c_{h}\|_{2}\right).

(213)

It remains to account for the endpoint coefficients. In the original
second-moment expansion, there are exactly two
b_{1}
-endpoint factors and two

b_{2}
-endpoint factors. Each such factor either remains as a half-edge or has
already been absorbed into the external scalar
\Theta
. This accounting gives
the endpoint bound

|\Theta|\prod_{h\in H}\|c_{h}\|_{2}\leq\|b_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\|b_{2}\|_{2}^{2}.

Together with (
213
), this bound implies

|\Theta|\,\Gamma\leq O_{d}\bigl(\|b_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\|b_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\bigr).

(214)

We now count the powers of
n
. Each surviving sample position appears in
exactly two surviving factors. The corresponding degree count is the identity

2|E|+|H|=2q.

Since
\omega_{e}\leq n^{-1}
for every internal edge and

\eta_{h}\leq n^{-1/2}
for every half-edge, the product of weights satisfies

\Bigl(\prod_{e\in E}\omega_{e}\Bigr)\Bigl(\prod_{h\in H}\eta_{h}\Bigr)\leq n^{-|E|-|H|/2}=n^{-q}.

(215)

Substituting (
214
) and
(
215
) into
(
212
) gives

\bigl|\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}_{\pi^{\sharp}}]\bigr|\leq n^{r-q}\,O_{d}\bigl(\|b_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\|b_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\bigr).

The counting inequality (
211
), together with

r\geq 1
, implies

r-q\leq-r\leq-1.

The preceding estimate therefore gives

\bigl|\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{S}_{\pi^{\sharp}}]\bigr|=O_{d}(n^{-1})\|b_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\|b_{2}\|_{2}^{2}.

This proves the proposition.

Appendix M
Technical lemmas and propositions

Proposition 43
.

Let
X
be a random element of
\mathbb{H}^{n\times d}
, and let

f_{1},\dots,f_{d}:\mathbb{H}^{n\times d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
.
Assume hypercontractivity: for every
p\geq 2
and every
i\in[d]
,

\|f_{i}(X)\|_{L_{p}}\leq C_{p,\ell}\,\|f_{i}(X)\|_{L_{2}}.

Then, for every
\epsilon>0
, there exists a constant

C_{\epsilon,\ell}
, depending only on
\epsilon
and
\ell
, such that

\Big\|\max_{i\in[d]}f_{i}(X)\Big\|_{L_{2}}^{2}\leq C_{\epsilon,\ell}\,d^{\epsilon}\,\max_{i\in[d]}\|f_{i}(X)\|_{L_{2}}^{2}.

More explicitly, one may take
C_{\epsilon,\ell}=C_{2q,\ell}^{\,2}
for
any choice of
q>1
satisfying
1/q\leq\epsilon
.

Proof of Proposition
43
.

Fix
\epsilon>0
, and choose
q>1
such that
1/q\leq\epsilon
;
for instance, one may take
q=\max\{2,1/\epsilon\}
. Define

M(X):=\max_{i\in[d]}f_{i}(X).

For any real numbers
a_{1},\dots,a_{d}
, the inequality

(\max_{i}a_{i})^{2}\leq\max_{i}a_{i}^{2}
holds. Applying this pointwise gives the
initial reduction

\|M\|_{L_{2}}^{2}=\mathbb{E}\big[M(X)^{2}\big]\leq\mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{i\in[d]}f_{i}(X)^{2}\Big].

(216)

Set

Y(X):=\max_{i\in[d]}f_{i}(X)^{2}.

The random variable
Y
is nonnegative. By Lyapunov’s inequality, or
equivalently the monotonicity of
L_{p}
-norms on a probability space, the

L_{1}
-norm of
Y
is bounded by its
L_{q}
-norm. Thus the right-hand side
of (
216
) satisfies

\mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{i\in[d]}f_{i}(X)^{2}\Big]=\|Y\|_{L_{1}}\leq\|Y\|_{L_{q}}=\big(\mathbb{E}[Y(X)^{q}]\big)^{1/q}.

Using the definition of
Y
, this bound can be written as

\big(\mathbb{E}[Y(X)^{q}]\big)^{1/q}=\Big(\mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{i\in[d]}|f_{i}(X)|^{2q}\Big]\Big)^{1/q}.

The elementary inequality
\max_{i}b_{i}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{d}b_{i}
, valid for
nonnegative numbers
b_{i}
, gives the moment bound

\displaystyle\Big(\mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{i\in[d]}|f_{i}(X)|^{2q}\Big]\Big)^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{i=1}^{d}|f_{i}(X)|^{2q}\Big]\Big)^{1/q}

\displaystyle=\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathbb{E}\big[|f_{i}(X)|^{2q}\big]\Big)^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq d^{1/q}\,\max_{i\in[d]}\Big(\mathbb{E}\big[|f_{i}(X)|^{2q}\big]\Big)^{1/q}.

(217)

For each
i\in[d]
, the assumed hypercontractive estimate, applied with
exponent
2q
, gives

\|f_{i}(X)\|_{L_{2q}}\leq C_{2q,\ell}\,\|f_{i}(X)\|_{L_{2}}.

Equivalently, after squaring both sides, this estimate becomes

\Big(\mathbb{E}\big[|f_{i}(X)|^{2q}\big]\Big)^{1/q}=\|f_{i}(X)\|_{L_{2q}}^{2}\leq C_{2q,\ell}^{\,2}\,\|f_{i}(X)\|_{L_{2}}^{2}.

(218)

Combining (
216
),
(
M
), and
(
218
) gives the estimate

\Big\|\max_{i\in[d]}f_{i}(X)\Big\|_{L_{2}}^{2}\leq d^{1/q}\,C_{2q,\ell}^{\,2}\,\max_{i\in[d]}\|f_{i}(X)\|_{L_{2}}^{2}.

Since
1/q\leq\epsilon
and
d\geq 1
, the factor
d^{1/q}
is bounded
above by
d^{\epsilon}
. Therefore, with

C_{\epsilon,\ell}:=C_{2q,\ell}^{\,2}
, the desired estimate follows.
∎

Proposition 44

(Weighted sums of Fourier–Walsh polynomials)
.

Let
1\leq m\leq q
, let
p=(p_{1},\dots,p_{q})\in\mathbb{N}^{q}
, and fix

S^{*}\subset[d]
with
|S^{*}|=p^{*}
.
For each
i\in[q]
, let
\mathcal{S}_{i}
be a collection of subsets of

[d]
such that
|S|\leq p_{i}
for every
S\in\mathcal{S}_{i}
.
Then, for every choice of vectors

a^{[i]}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{S}_{i}|}
,
i=1,\dots,m
, one has

\left|\sum_{S_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{1},\dots,S_{q}\in\mathcal{S}_{q}}a^{[1]}_{S_{1}}\cdots a^{[m]}_{S_{m}}\,\mathbb{E}\!\left[x^{S_{1}}\cdots x^{S_{q}}x^{S^{*}}\right]\right|\leq C_{q,p}\,d^{\sum_{j=m+1}^{q}p_{j}/2}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\|a^{[i]}\|_{2}.

(219)

Proof of Proposition
44
.

Let
\mu
denote the underlying probability measure, and write

\|\cdot\|_{L_{r}}
for the corresponding
L_{r}(\mu)
-norm.
For each
i\in[m]
, define the weighted Walsh polynomial
f_{i}
by

f_{i}(x):=\sum_{S\in\mathcal{S}_{i}}a^{[i]}_{S}\,x^{S}.

For each
j=m+1,\dots,q
, define the unweighted Walsh polynomial
g_{j}
by

g_{j}(x):=\sum_{S\in\mathcal{S}_{j}}x^{S}.

Expanding these definitions shows that the expression inside the absolute
value in (
219
) is the quantity

L:=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\!\left[x^{S^{*}}\,\prod_{i=1}^{m}f_{i}(x)\,\prod_{j=m+1}^{q}g_{j}(x)\right].

(220)

The identity
|x^{S^{*}}|=1
holds pointwise, so multiplication by

x^{S^{*}}
preserves every
L_{r}(\mu)
-norm. An application of
Cauchy–Schwarz gives the estimate

|L|\leq\left\|\prod_{i=1}^{m}f_{i}\prod_{j=m+1}^{q}g_{j}\right\|_{L_{2}}.

To estimate this
L_{2}
-norm, set

h_{r}:=\begin{cases}f_{r},&1\leq r\leq m,\\
g_{r},&m+1\leq r\leq q.\end{cases}

Hölder’s inequality, applied with exponent
q
to the
q
factors

|h_{r}|^{2}
, yields the bound

\left\|\prod_{r=1}^{q}h_{r}\right\|_{L_{2}}=\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\prod_{r=1}^{q}|h_{r}|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\leq\prod_{r=1}^{q}\|h_{r}\|_{L_{2q}}.

Combining the preceding two estimates gives the key reduction

|L|\leq\prod_{i=1}^{m}\|f_{i}\|_{L_{2q}}\prod_{j=m+1}^{q}\|g_{j}\|_{L_{2q}}.

(221)

It remains to bound the factors on the right-hand side of
(
221
). Since
f_{i}
is a Walsh polynomial of degree
at most
p_{i}
, the Bonami hypercontractive inequality gives the estimate

\|f_{i}\|_{L_{2q}}\leq(2q-1)^{p_{i}/2}\|f_{i}\|_{L_{2}}.

The orthonormality of the Fourier–Walsh basis gives the identity

\|f_{i}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}=\sum_{S\in\mathcal{S}_{i}}|a^{[i]}_{S}|^{2}=\|a^{[i]}\|_{2}^{2}.

Combining these two estimates yields

\|f_{i}\|_{L_{2q}}\leq(2q-1)^{p_{i}/2}\|a^{[i]}\|_{2}.

(222)

The same argument applies to the unweighted factors. Since
g_{j}
is a
Walsh polynomial of degree at most
p_{j}
, the Bonami hypercontractive
inequality gives the estimate

\|g_{j}\|_{L_{2q}}\leq(2q-1)^{p_{j}/2}\|g_{j}\|_{L_{2}}.

The orthonormality of the Fourier–Walsh basis gives the identity

\|g_{j}\|_{L_{2}}^{2}=\sum_{S\in\mathcal{S}_{j}}1=|\mathcal{S}_{j}|.

Since every set in
\mathcal{S}_{j}
has cardinality at most
p_{j}
, the
cardinality of
\mathcal{S}_{j}
satisfies the bound

|\mathcal{S}_{j}|\leq\sum_{r=0}^{p_{j}}\binom{d}{r}\leq C_{p_{j}}d^{p_{j}}.

Combining the preceding three estimates gives

\|g_{j}\|_{L_{2q}}\leq C_{q,p}d^{p_{j}/2}.

(223)

Substituting (
222
) and (
223
) into
(
221
) yields the final estimate

|L|\leq C_{q,p}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m}\|a^{[i]}\|_{2}\right)d^{\sum_{j=m+1}^{q}p_{j}/2}.

By the representation of
L
in (
220
), this estimate is exactly
the desired bound (
219
).
∎
